# U0816 | National - polity and governance: bills, acts, ordinances, landmark judgments
- **Rows:** G-5 - National - polity and governance: bills, acts, ordinances, landmark judgments; S4-6 - National - polity and governance: bills, acts, ordinances and landmark judgments
- **Exams:** G1-Prelims + G2-Screening | **Tier:** HIGH, 3 pp (~1,350 words) | **PYQs:** APPSC 35, other exams 2, last APPSC year 2025, latest papers 2
- **Keywords:**
  1. *Landmark judgments in news - case name, Article interpreted, bench, key directions (triple talaq follow-ups, marriage solemnisation, firecracker bans) [I]
  2. *Electoral reforms and ECI apps -> purpose (e-voting, remote voting, Saksham, Suvidha, cVIGIL) and first states adopting them [I]
  3. *Elections held - phase-wise states, state assembly results -> seats and vote share [I]
  4. *Reservation and constitutional amendments - amendment number, provisions, domicile quotas in private jobs, EWS quota [I]
  5. *Bills passed by Parliament - name, ministry, objective, order or Act amended (Scheduled Tribes Order amendment Bills) [I]
  6. *Acts in news - Act name, key provisions, amending Acts (Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code 2016) [I]
  7. *Judicial infrastructure - virtual courts, e-courts, fast-track courts -> number and coverage [I]
  8. *Renaming of cities, districts and places -> old name + new name [I+II]
  9. Ordinances promulgated - subject, Article invoked, replacement Bill [I]
  10. *Supreme Court judgments and orders - case name, bench strength, law struck down or upheld, follow-up petitions, SC-appointed committees -> head [II]
  11. *Elections - Lok Sabha phases by state, state assembly results, President/Vice President, Rajya Sabha [II]
  12. *Bills passed by Parliament -> ministry + order/Act amended + key provisions [II]
  13. *Election Commission initiatives -> app/portal + purpose (voter-facilitation apps, e-voting pilots, facilities for PwD voters) [II]
  14. *Judicial reforms and legal services - virtual courts, e-courts, NALSA/DLSA initiatives [II]
  15. +New Acts replacing old laws -> old laws repealed + new name (criminal laws, electricity, data protection) [II]
  16. Constitutional amendment bills - Article amended, amendment number, ratification by states [II]
  17. Governance reforms - delimitation, simultaneous elections, reservation changes [II]
- **NEW from 2024-25 papers:**
  N1. State governance and policing initiatives -> launching state + feature/time limit (Mission Investigation@75 days, e-FIR, Shakti app)
- **Latest-paper questions:** G1 Prelims 2024 P2 (NEW: State government initiative); G2 Screening 2024 (Judgements)

### UPSC at 100 — centenary of the Public Service Commission
## 1. Why in news
- UPSC completed 100 years on 1 October 2026; India's first Public Service Commission constituted on 1 October 1926
- HQ: Dholpur House, New Delhi
## 2. Evolution
| Year | Milestone |
| 1855 | ICS exam introduced in London (replacing EIC nomination system) |
| 1863/1864 | Satyendranath Tagore cleared ICS (1863), joined 1864 — first Indian ICS member |
| 1919 | Only 78 of 1,255 ICS officers Indian; GoI Act 1919 provided for a PSC |
| 1922 | ICS exam held in India first time at Allahabad; moved to Delhi in 1928 |
| 1924 | Lee Commission (chair Arthur Hamilton Lee) recommended statutory PSC |
| 1926 | PSC set up under PSC (Functions) Rules 1926; first Chairman Sir Ross Barker; 4 Members; at Metcalfe House, Delhi |
| 1935/1937 | GoI Act 1935 — Federal & Provincial PSCs; renamed Federal PSC in 1937 |
| 1947 | H.K. Kripalani — first Indian Chairman |
| 22 Aug 1949 | PSC provisions moved in Constituent Assembly by Dr B.R. Ambedkar |
| 26 Jan 1950 | Became UPSC; R.N. Banerjee first Chairman under Constitution |
## 3. Constitutional framework — Part XIV, Articles 315–323
| Article | Provision |
| 315 | PSCs for Union and States; Joint PSC for two or more States |
| 316 | Appointment and tenure — UPSC members appointed by President |
| 317 | Removal/suspension by President; misbehaviour needs Supreme Court inquiry |
| 318 | President regulates conditions of service |
| 319 | Post-office bar — UPSC Chairman ineligible for further govt employment |
| 320 | Functions — exams, recruitment, promotions, disciplinary |
| 321 | Parliament/State Legislature can extend functions |
| 323 | Annual report to President (⚠ not CJI) — laid before Parliament |
## 4. Reforms timeline
| Year | Reform |
| 1969 | Eighth Schedule languages for Essay/GS |
| 2022 | 4 Group A railway services unified as Indian Railway Management Service |
## 5. Inclusion and technology
- Women: 3.6% (3 of 83) in 1952 → 31.2% (299 of 958) in CSE 2025; 11 of top 25 ranks
- SC: 9 (1955) → 158 (2025); ST: 2 (1956–57) → 73 (2025); PwBD: 3 (2002) → 42 (2025)
- AI facial authentication: piloted at NDA/CDS Sept 2025; at scale in CS Prelims 24 May 2026 — ~5.50 lakh candidates, 6–8 seconds each
- 569 applicants rejected for false claims (15 years of data)
- Universal Registration Number: May 2025; randomised interview boards from CMS 2025; 30-channel helpdesk from Feb 2026; QPRep portal for answer-key representations; training via NIELIT
- Applications: 42,727 (1950–51) → peak 45,10,044 (2022–23); exam centres 8 (1950) → 86 (2026)
## 6. Lee Commission
- Appointed 1923, report 1924; chairman Lord Lee of Fareham; recommended statutory PSC

### CJI Surya Kant constitutes Judicial Well-Being Committee
## 1. Key facts
- Standing Committee on Judicial Well-Being constituted 10 October 2026 (World Mental Health Day) by CJI Surya Kant
- Chair: Justice K.V. Viswanathan; members — Dr Savita Malhotra (PGIMER), Rajat Mitra, Mrinal Kanwar, Aarti Singh
- Based on research by SC's Centre for Research and Planning
## 2. Background
- Article 124 (SC), Article 50 (separation of judiciary), Article 235; SC inaugurated 28 January 1950; strength 34
- World Mental Health Day 10 October — first 1992 (WFMH)
### Legal Metrology (Indian Standard Time) Rules, 2026
## 1. Rules
- Notified: August 27, 2026 by the Union government
- Make IST the single legal reference for legal, administrative, commercial and official purposes
- Come into force: 180 days after Gazette publication
- Organisations must sync with time traceable to IST; ⚠ no need for every organisation to own an atomic clock
- Enforcement: Legal Metrology Department; technical guidance by CSIR-NPL
- Other time zones may be displayed only along with IST
## 2. IST facts
- Offset: UTC+5:30; maintained by CSIR–National Physical Laboratory (India's National Metrology Institute)
- IST® trademark registered by CSIR-NPL in 2024; uncertainty < 3 nanoseconds vs UTC
- 5 Regional Reference Standard Laboratories (RRSLs) keep secondary time scales
- White Rabbit-based IST dissemination network commissioned July 2026 at RRSL Bengaluru
| Technology | Accuracy |
| NTP | Milliseconds |
| PTP | Microseconds |
| Satellite links | Nanoseconds |
| Optical links | Picoseconds |
## 3. Background
- NavIC (earlier IRNSS) — developed after GPS data was denied during the Kargil War; carries atomic clocks
### Uniform Civil Code debate
## 1. News
- HM Amit Shah: UCC in all 21 NDA-ruled States by 2029
- ⚠ Uttarakhand — UCC in force since January 2025; UCC Bills of Assam, Gujarat, Madhya Pradesh await Presidential assent
- State UCCs exempt tribal populations
## 2. Constitution
- Article 44 (DPSP, Part IV): "State shall endeavour to secure ... UCC"; non-justiciable
- Article 25 — religion; Article 29 — culture
- Law Commission 2018 consultation paper — reform discriminatory personal laws; Ambedkar suggested a voluntary UCC
## 3. Cases
| Case | Point |
| Shah Bano (1985) | Maintenance under Sec 125 CrPC |
| Sarla Mudgal (1995) | Conversion for second marriage invalid |
| Daniel Latifi (2001) | Upheld Muslim Women Act 1986 |
| John Vallamattom (2003) | Struck Sec 118, Indian Succession Act 1925 |
| Shayara Bano (2017) | Instant triple talaq invalid |
| Jose Paulo Coutinho (2019) | Goa as example of common civil code |
- Section 6A Citizenship Act case (2024): casteism/gender discrimination cannot claim Art 29 protection
### SC: grounds of arrest in writing
## 1. Ruling
- Grounds of arrest must be given in writing and in a language the accused understands — under Article 22(1), for all offences incl. PMLA and UAPA
- ⚠ Written grounds at least 2 hours before production for remand, else arrest illegal
- Section 47, BNSS — inform grounds and bail rights
- Exceptional cases: oral first, written within reasonable time; failure → arrest and remand illegal
- Re-arrest: written grounds + permission of Judicial Magistrate; probe to another officer; disciplinary action on erring officer
### Article 142 — SC quashes NEET-UG protest FIRs
## 1. News
- SC used Article 142 ("complete justice") to quash FIRs against students protesting the NEET-UG paper leak
- ⚠ Only the Supreme Court has Art 142 power — not High Courts (Art 226)
## 2. Article 142 and related articles
- 142(1): decrees/orders for complete justice — enforceable across India as Parliament (or, till then, President) prescribes; 142(2): attendance, documents, contempt
- Power is broad but not unlimited — cannot override fundamental constitutional limits
- Cases: Prem Chand Garg (1963), Union Carbide, Delhi Judicial Service Association, Supreme Court Bar Association
| Article | Subject |
| 32 | Constitutional remedies — "heart and soul" (Ambedkar) |
| 129 | SC — Court of Record, contempt |
| 143 | President's advisory reference |
| 226 | High Court writs |
### Inter-State Council (Amendment) Order 2026
## 1. News
- President Droupadi Murmu amended the Inter-State Council Order, 1990 under Article 263; notified September 9, 2026
- Governor attends ISC when Art 356 is in force; LG of Delhi when Art 239AB applies
- ISC: 1990, on Sarkaria Commission advice; chaired by PM
### Ladakh — proposed new constitutional provision
## 1. News
- MHA proposal: directly elected governing body for Ladakh UT, safeguards on land, culture, language — linked to Article 240; meeting in New Delhi (Sept 9, 2026)
- Leh Apex Body and Kargil Democratic Alliance seek statehood and Sixth Schedule
- Ladakh UT since October 31, 2019
## 2. Background
- Article 371: 371A Nagaland, 371B Assam, 371C Manipur, 371D AP and Telangana, 371F Sikkim, 371G Mizoram, 371H Arunachal, 371I Goa, 371J Karnataka
- Sixth Schedule: Arts 244(2), 275(1) — Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura, Mizoram
- Proposal titled ⚠ "Chapter K" under Article 371 (meeting September 9, 2026)
- Art. 371-K: Proposed for Ladakh (Sep 2026)
- Proposal covers land, culture, language, forests, environment; Article 371 is in Part XXI

### Shehzad Bhatti Network — 46th banned terror group
## 1. News
- MHA declared Pakistan-based Shehzad Bhatti Network a terrorist organisation under UAPA 1967 on September 16, 2026 — 46th in First Schedule, Section 35(1)(a)
- Section 35 — Centre can add/remove organisations in the First Schedule
### Vande Mataram stanzas case
## 1. News
- Carnatic musician T.M. Krishna challenged MHA's February 2026 direction to sing all six stanzas of Vande Mataram; SC (bench incl. CJI Surya Kant) to examine under Bijoe Emmanuel (1986) principles
- Prevention of Insults to National Honour (Amendment) Act, 2026 — obstructing National Song can bring up to 3 years
- Vande Mataram: Bankim Chandra Chattopadhyay, Anandamath; first sung by Tagore at 1896 Calcutta INC session
- Bijoe Emmanuel (1986): Arts 19(1)(a) and 25
- SC observed criminal action should not be initiated against a person declining to sing all stanzas on genuine religious grounds
- Bench: CJI Surya Kant, Justice Joymalya Bagchi, Justice V. Mohan — Centre to reply in two weeks
- Prevention of Insults to National Honour (Amendment) Act, 2026 — assent August 6, 2026; extends anthem-level protection to Vande Mataram
### Registration of Births and Deaths (Amendment) Act 2026
## 1. News
- In force from October 1, 2026 — RGI and Census Commissioner Mrityunjay Kumar Narayan
- Strengthens provisions against delayed registration; original RBD Act, 1969; nodal MHA
- Presidential assent August 2026; commencement by Gazette notification
- Delay 1–2 years — Judicial Magistrate permission + fee; delay > 2 years — special order of a First-Class Magistrate
### Lottery regulation in India
## 1. Framework
- Union List Entry 40: lotteries by Union/State govts; State List Entry 34: betting and gambling; Entry 62 changed after 101st Amendment (GST)
- Lotteries (Regulation) Act, 1998: Sec 3 prohibition, Sec 4 conditions, Sec 5 State may ban other States' lotteries, Sec 6 Union power; Rules 2010
- GST: 40% on lottery, betting, casinos, online money gaming from September 22, 2025; winnings taxed 30% (Sec 115BB), TDS Sec 194B
- 9 States run lotteries (LS reply March 14, 2023): Arunachal, Goa, Kerala, Maharashtra, Mizoram, Nagaland, Punjab, Sikkim, West Bengal
## 2. Cases
| Case | Holding |
| R.M.D. Chamarbaugwala (1957) | Gambling res extra commercium |
| B.R. Enterprises (1999) | All-or-nothing rule — TN (2003), Karnataka (2007) went lottery-free |
| Skill Lotto Solutions (2020) | Lotteries = actionable claims = goods under GST |
### SC launches Jan Suchna Seva
## 1. News
- Launched September 14, 2026 (Hindi Diwas) — simplified judgments and 15–30 minute audio-visual explainers
### Telangana HC disqualifies MLA Danam Nagender
## 1. News
- Telangana HC disqualified Khairatabad MLA Danam Nagender (won on BRS ticket 2023, joined Congress, contested Secunderabad LS 2024) on September 18, 2026
- Bench: CJ Aparesh Kumar Singh and Justice G.M. Mohiuddin; set aside Speaker Gaddam Prasad Kumar's decision
- Disqualified from April 23, 2024 under Para 2(1)(a), Tenth Schedule; petitioners Padi Kaushik Reddy (BRS), Aleti Maheshwar Reddy (BJP)
## 2. Background
- Tenth Schedule — 52nd Amendment, 1985; Para 6 — Speaker decides (subject to judicial review); Art 191(2), 190(3)(a), 102(2)
- Khairatabad seat declared vacant; judgment sent to Speaker and Election Commission
- Art 192 — Governor decides other disqualifications; HCs review Speaker's Tenth Schedule decisions under Art 226
### Konda Surekha removed from Telangana cabinet
## 1. News
- CM A. Revanth Reddy removed Minister Konda Surekha on September 3, 2026; G. Chinna Reddy's appointment as State Planning Board Vice-Chairman withdrawn
- Art 163, 164(2), 164(4) (6 months); 91st Amendment 2003 — ministers ≤ 15% of Assembly, minimum 12
- Surekha held Environment, Forests and Endowments; Art 167 — CM's duty to inform the Governor
### PRAHAAR — India's first national counter-terrorism policy
## 1. Key facts
- PRAHAAR: Policy for Response Against Hostile Activities and Radicalism — unveiled by Amit Shah on February 23, 2026
- ⚠ India's first National Counter-Terrorism Policy and Strategy; 10-year framework; nodal MHA
- PRAHAAR – CT Policy and Strategy conference: New Delhi, September 28, 2026
## 2. Seven pillars
- Prevention; Response; Aggregation of Internal Capabilities; Human Rights; Alleviating Conditions Conducive to Terrorism; Harmonising International Efforts; Recovery and Resilience
### Bihar–Jharkhand Son river water agreement
## 1. Agreement
- Bihar and Jharkhand to sign on August 31, 2026 — ends ~25-year dispute (since Jharkhand formed, November 2000)
| State | Share |
| Bihar | 5.75 million acre-feet |
| Jharkhand | 2.0 MAF |
| Total | 7.75 MAF — undivided Bihar's share under 1973 Bansagar Agreement |
## 2. Background
- Bansagar Agreement 1973: MP, UP, undivided Bihar
- Son: rises on Amarkantak Plateau (MP); flows MP, UP, Jharkhand, Bihar; ⚠ major right-bank tributary of Ganga, joins west of Patna; Bansagar Dam
### Tulu — additional administrative language in two Karnataka districts
## 1. News
- Karnataka made Tulu the second additional administrative language in Dakshina Kannada and Udupi (September 2026)
- Based on committee of educationist M. Mohan Alva (report 2023); ₹82 lakh for translation and training
## 2. Background
- Tulu: Dravidian; Tulu Nadu; ⚠ not in the Eighth Schedule
- Karnataka: formed November 1, 1956; official language Kannada

### UPSC at 100
## Evolution of PSCs
- India's first PSC formed 1 October 1926; UPSC completed 100 years on 1 Oct 2026

- Assam Rifles — run by MHA; in operations, the Army
- Article 33 — Parliament may restrict Fundamental Rights of members of the forces
- World Mental Health Day — 10 October; first observed 1992
- Legal Metrology (IST) Rules, 2026 — make IST the only legal time reference (legal, administrative, commercial, official); organisations only match IST-traceable time
- Vande Mataram — written by Bankim Chandra Chattopadhyay; first sung by Rabindranath Tagore at the 1896 Calcutta INC session

- CRPF — 1939; BSF — 1965; CISF — 1969

- Other time zones may be shown only along with IST

### UCC: status & cases
## Uniform Civil Code
- Article 44 (Part IV, DPSP) — State to try to secure a UCC; Article 25 — freedom of religion
- Uttarakhand — first State, UCC in force since January 2025
- Gujarat — second State, UCC Bill–2026 assented by President Droupadi Murmu
- Gujarat Bill also covers live-in relationships
- Assam, Gujarat and Madhya Pradesh Bills awaited President's assent (Sep 2026 debate)
## Cases & other
- Daniel Latifi — upheld the Muslim Women (Protection of Rights on Divorce) Act, 1986; Shah Bano — maintenance under Section 125 CrPC
- Section 6A Citizenship Act case (2024) — casteism and gender discrimination cannot claim Article 29 protection
- Shayara Bano — ended instant triple talaq; Jose Paulo Coutinho — cited Goa's common civil code
- Article 142 — wide but not unlimited; cannot override basic constitutional limits
- Article 161 — Governor's pardoning power; 156 term, 157 qualifications, 159 oath
- Ladakh MHA proposal (9 Sep 2026) — directly elected governing body, safeguards for land, culture, language, linked to Article 240
- Statehood and Sixth Schedule demanded by Leh Apex Body and Kargil Democratic Alliance
### Courts & rulings
## Judiciary initiatives
- Standing Committee on Judicial Well-Being (10 Oct 2026, CJI Surya Kant) — Chair Justice K.V. Viswanathan
- Members — Dr Savita Malhotra (PGIMER), Rajat Mitra, Mrinal Kanwar
- Based on research by the SC's Centre for Research and Planning
- Jan Suchna Seva — SC service started on Hindi Diwas (14 Sep 2026); simple judgments, 15–30 min explainers
## Arrest rulings
- Article 22(1) — grounds of arrest in writing, in a language the accused understands; all offences incl. PMLA, UAPA
- Written grounds at least 2 hours before production for remand, else arrest illegal
- Section 47 BNSS — inform arrested person of grounds and bail rights
- Re-arrest — written grounds + Judicial Magistrate permission; inquiry to another officer
## Article 142 and others
- Article 142 power only with the Supreme Court; 142(2) — attendance, documents, contempt; 'heart and soul' is Article 32
- NEET-UG paper leak — Article 142 used to quash FIRs against protesting students
- Article 155 — Governor appointed by the President
- Sixth Schedule (Arts 244(2), 275(1)) — Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura, Mizoram
- PRAHAAR — launched by Amit Shah (23 Feb 2026); 10-year framework; conference in New Delhi
- Vande Mataram stanzas — MHA direction (Feb 2026) on all six stanzas challenged by T.M. Krishna; bench incl. CJI Surya Kant, Justice V. Mohan; Centre to reply in two weeks
- Betting and gambling — State List Entry 34; lotteries by governments — Union List Entry 40
- Tenth Schedule Paragraph 6 — Speaker decides disqualification, subject to judicial review; Article 192 — other disqualifications
- Danam Nagender (Khairatabad MLA) — disqualified by Telangana HC under Paragraph 2(1)(a), effective 23 April 2024

### SC: grounds of arrest in writing
## 1. Ruling
- Grounds of arrest must be given in writing and in a language the accused understands — under Article 22(1), for all offences incl. PMLA and UAPA
- ⚠ Written grounds at least 2 hours before production for remand, else arrest illegal
- Section 47, BNSS — inform grounds and bail rights
- Exceptional cases: oral first, written within reasonable time; failure → arrest and remand illegal
- Re-arrest: written grounds + permission of Judicial Magistrate; probe to another officer; disciplinary action on erring officer
### Article 142 — SC quashes NEET-UG protest FIRs
## 1. News
- SC used Article 142 ("complete justice") to quash FIRs against students protesting the NEET-UG paper leak
- ⚠ Only the Supreme Court has Art 142 power — not High Courts (Art 226)
## 2. Article 142 and related articles
- 142(1): decrees/orders for complete justice — enforceable across India as Parliament (or, till then, President) prescribes; 142(2): attendance, documents, contempt
- Power is broad but not unlimited — cannot override fundamental constitutional limits
- Cases: Prem Chand Garg (1963), Union Carbide, Delhi Judicial Service Association, Supreme Court Bar Association
| Article | Subject |
| 32 | Constitutional remedies — "heart and soul" (Ambedkar) |
| 129 | SC — Court of Record, contempt |
| 143 | President's advisory reference |
| 226 | High Court writs |

### Supreme Court judges' strength raised to 38
## 1. The Bill
- Bill: Supreme Court (Number of Judges) Amendment Bill, 2026 — passed by Lok Sabha; replaces an ordinance issued earlier in 2026
- Change: puisne judges 33 to 37; total 34 to 38 including the CJI
- Amends: Supreme Court (Number of Judges) Act, 1956
- Money Bill: 34 → 38 judges (37 + CJI); passed as a Money Bill (Aug 2026); amends 1956 Act (Art. 124)
- Ordinance: Supreme Court (Number of Judges) Amendment Ordinance, 2026 (May) under Art. 123 — 33 → 37 judges + CJI; replaced by a Bill passed by the LS on 3 Aug 2026
- SC strength: Raised to 38 incl. CJI (from 34) by ordinance (May 2026) + Bill (LS, 3 Aug 2026)
- ⚠ First increase since 2019 (31 to 34); no constitutional amendment needed — Article 124(1) lets Parliament fix the number by law
- Puisne judge: a Supreme Court judge other than the CJI
## 2. Why
- Pendency: about 92,101 cases on 1 January 2026
- ⚠ Sources differ: other notes say SC pendency ~88,000 cases (2025)
- 2025: 75,410 fresh cases vs 65,615 disposed
- Constitution Bench: at least 5 judges
- All courts backlog: over 5.5 crore cases
- India Justice Report 2025: about 15.9 judges per million; Law Commission recommended 50 per million
- ⚠ Sources differ: other notes say India Justice Report 2025: ~15 judges per million; 5,600+ vacancies; 5.5 crore+ pending cases
## 3. Evolution of strength (including CJI)
| Year | Strength |
| 1950 | 8 |
| 1956 | 11 |
| 2019 | 34 |
| 2026 | 38 |
## 4. Background
- Article 128: CJI, with President's consent, can ask a retired SC judge to sit
- Article 137: SC review power; Article 215: High Courts as courts of record
- Article 123: President's ordinance power
### Naxal-free India: strategy against Left-Wing Extremism
## 1. Milestone
- News: India effectively free from LWE — no district officially LWE-affected
- Origin: Naxalbari uprising, 1967, West Bengal; groups merged into CPI (Maoist) in 2004
- Peak: 2010 — 1,936 incidents; 2004–14: 17,542 incidents, 1,913 security personnel and 5,019 civilians killed
- Policy: National Policy and Action Plan to address LWE, 2015 — approach of Dialogue, Security and Coordination
## 2. Security
- 663 fortified police stations; 408 new CAPF camps (7 years); 68 night-landing helipads; 400 bullet-proof vehicles
- Forces: CoBRA (Commando Battalion for Resolute Action, CRPF), District Reserve Guard, Jharkhand Jaguar, Greyhounds
- Approach: "Trace, Target, Neutralise"
- NIA: dedicated vertical against Naxal financing
## 3. Surrender and development
- Surrender grant: ₹5 lakh (higher-ranked), ₹2.5 lakh (others); ₹10,000 a month for 36 months
- Surrenders: 2,337 in 2025; 3,927 between 2024 and March 2026
- Roads: 15,189 km (Road Requirement Plan, Road Connectivity Project for LWE areas)
- 2015–2026: 1,804 bank branches, 1,321 ATMs, 74,720 banking correspondents, 6,025 post offices
- EMRS: 259 sanctioned, 179 functional; 47 ITIs, 49 Skill Development Centres

- Creamy layer now applies to OBCs only; ⚠ not to SC/STs

### Police custody under BNSS Section 187(2): SC ruling
## 1. Case
- Case: State of Andhra Pradesh vs Suda Suresh Veera Venkata Naga Raju — custodial death probe in AP
- Ruling: police custody can be sought in parts during the initial 40 or 60 days; ⚠ total still capped at 15 days
- Magistrate cannot impose a blanket "no extension" condition; SC allowed 7 more days (within 15)
- Section 38 BNSS: accused may meet an advocate during interrogation, not throughout
## 2. Law
| Provision | Rule |
| Sec 58 BNSS | No detention beyond 24 hours without magistrate |
| Sec 187(2) BNSS | Police custody 15 days total, in parts, within first 40/60 days |
| Sec 187(3) BNSS | Detention up to 90 days (death/life/10+ years) or 60 days (others); then default bail |
| Sec 167 CrPC (old) | 15 days police custody, generally in the first 15 days |
### Vande Mataram gets protection: National Honour (Amendment) Act 2026
## 1. The amendment
- Act: Prevention of Insults to National Honour (Amendment) Act, 2026 — amends Section 3 of the 1971 Act
- Effect: National Song (Vande Mataram) gets same protection as National Anthem — punishes intentional prevention or disturbance of singing
- Punishment: up to 3 years, fine or both; Section 3A (added 2003) — repeat offence minimum 1 year
- ⚠ Does not make singing compulsory, prescribe stanzas or punish respectful silence
- Passage: introduced Rajya Sabha 24 July 2026; passed RS 29 July, LS 30 July
## 2. 1971 Act
- Enacted: 23 December 1971; Section 2 — Flag and Constitution (burning, defacing etc.)
## 3. Vande Mataram history
- Composer: Bankim Chandra Chatterjee; first published in Bangadarshan, 7 November 1875; in novel Anandamath (1882)
- Tagore sang it at 1896 INC session, Calcutta; slogan on 7 August 1905 (Swadeshi)
- October 1937: CWC — only first two stanzas at official gatherings
- 24 January 1950: Rajendra Prasad — equal honour with Jana Gana Mana; ⚠ Constitution does not mention a "National Song"
- Bijoe Emmanuel v. State of Kerala (1986): Jehovah's Witness students — silence protected under Art 19(1)(a) and Art 25
## 4. Extra
- ⚠ 2026 marks the 150th anniversary of Vande Mataram

### SC refines legal definition of 'industry'
## 1. Verdict
- Bench: 9-judge Constitution Bench led by CJI Surya Kant; 6:3 majority
- Reconsidered: Bangalore Water Supply and Sewerage Board v. A. Rajappa (1978) — 7-judge bench of Justice V.R. Krishna Iyer, "Triple Test" (systematic activity; employer–employee cooperation; goods/services for human wants)
- Held: 1947 definition cannot be applied unchanged under Industrial Relations Code 2020; ruling prospective
- IR Code 2020 merged Industrial Disputes Act 1947, Trade Unions Act 1926, Industrial Employment (Standing Orders) Act 1946

### Legal Metrology (Indian Standard Time) Rules 2026
## 1. Rules
- Under: Legal Metrology Act 2009 (Ministry of Consumer Affairs); IST mandatory official time — UTC+05:30
- In force: 180 days after Gazette publication
- Display in hh:mm:ss mandatory at railway stations, airports, government offices

## 2. Background

### Public Examinations (Prevention of Unfair Means) Amendment Act 2026
## 1. Key points
- Administered by: Ministry of Personnel; original Act in force 2024
- Amends 2024 Act; assent 31 Jul 2026; STFs, probe within 60 days; special fast-track courts
- Trigger: Gen-Z student protests over paper leaks → Education Minister's resignation (25 Jul 2026) + the Public Examinations Amendment (Aug 2026)
- Mandatory fast-track courts — trials within 2 months
- Punishment (CA-360): involvement in leaks 5–10 years + fine up to ₹50 lakh; organised crime 7 years + fine up to ₹10 crore
- ⚠ One-liners give organised leaks as up to 10 years and ₹10 crore
- Covers: 15 forms of malpractice — paper leaks, OMR tampering, fake websites, fake ID cards

### Trial in absentia — Section 356 BNSS
## 1. What happened
- Special NIA Court, Jammu issued non-bailable warrant against Hafiz Saeed (Lashkar-e-Taiba chief) in Pahalgam attack case
- NIA may seek trial in absentia under Section 356, BNSS 2023
## 2. Provisions
- Applies only to: proclaimed offender under Section 84 BNSS — offence punishable with 10 years+, life or death
- Court must record reasons; absence treated as waiver of right to be present
- Safeguards: two consecutive arrest warrants at least 30 days apart; newspaper notice with 30 days to appear; notice at last residence; inform relative/friend; trial only after 90 days from framing of charges; State-appointed defence lawyer; audiovisual recording
- Accused appearing later may cross-examine witnesses
- ⚠ Not for minor offences
## 3. CrPC comparison
| CrPC section | Purpose |
| 82 | Proclamation against absconder |
| 299 | Evidence in absence of absconder |
| 317 | Dispense with personal attendance |
- ⚠ CrPC did not permit full trial in absentia; BNSS replaced CrPC 1973
### FCRA — FAQ and Amendment Bill 2026
## 1. About FCRA
- Foreign Contribution (Regulation) Act: administered by Ministry of Home Affairs
- History: first Act 1976; registration mandatory 1984; replaced by FCRA 2010; 2020 amendment — single SBI New Delhi Main Branch account, sub-granting banned; 2022 relatives' limit raised
- 2024–25: about 16,200 registered associations received about ₹22,963 crore
- ⚠ FCRA does not ban foreign donations — registration (organisations 3 years+ old) or prior permission
- Registration validity: 5 years; annual return Form FC-4
- Admin expense cap: 20% of annual FC
- Barred: election candidates, legislators, judges, public servants, political parties, political organisations, certain media persons
## 2. 2026 changes
- Provisional / permanent vesting of FC-created assets in Designated Authority (vesting concept from FCRA 2010)
- Revision and appeal to District Judge
- State agencies need Central approval before FCRA probes
- Registration to specify purpose and State/UT; existing bodies file Form FC-6F
- Renewal: must show utilisation of at least ₹10 lakh over prior two years
- Ultimate-donor, project-wise disclosure
## 3. Global comparison
| Country | Law |
| USA | FARA — Foreign Agents Registration Act |
| Australia | FITS — Foreign Influence Transparency Scheme |
| UK | FIRS — Foreign Influence Registration Scheme |
| Canada | Foreign Influence Transparency and Accountability Act |
### Bar Associations cannot refuse to represent an accused
## 1. What happened
- Faizabad/Ayodhya Bar Association resolved not to represent accused in alleged Ayodhya Ram Temple funds embezzlement case
- Legal position: such resolutions are illegal, unconstitutional, null and void
## 2. Constitutional basis
| Article | Provision |
| 22(1) | Arrested person's right to consult and be defended by a legal practitioner of choice |
| 21 | Fair trial part of right to life |
| 14 | Equality before law |
| 39A | Equal justice and free legal aid (DPSP) |
## 3. Cases
| Case | Holding |
| A.S. Mohammed Rafi v. State of Tamil Nadu (2010) | SC — boycott resolutions wholly illegal, null and void (Coimbatore police case) |
| Kuldeep Agarwal v. State of Uttarakhand (2019) | Uttarakhand HC — "special circumstances" refusal is for individual advocate, not Bar |
| Karnataka HC (2020) | Obstruction of lawyers for Kashmiri students may be contempt |
| Madras HC (2025) | Bar is not a trade union |
- BCI Rules: advocate generally bound to accept a brief; may refuse only in special circumstances
### Public Examinations Act and fast-track court for paper leaks
## 1. Facts
- A Fast Track Court for paper leak cases set up in Delhi
- Public Examinations (Prevention of Unfair Means) Act, 2024: introduced in Lok Sabha February 5, 2024; in force June 21, 2024; administered by Ministry of Personnel
- Covers: UPSC, SSC, RRB, NTA, IBPS exams
- Fast Track Court set up by the Delhi High Court at Rouse Avenue Court complex, New Delhi — presided by Judge Anu Grover Baliga; for paper leaks in competitive exams and admissions
- Fast Track Courts first introduced in 2000 (Centre–State cooperation)
- Fast-track courts: 884 in 21 States/UTs; 17.54 lakh pending cases (Law Minister, Aug 2026)
- Union Cabinet approved amendment with harsher penalties — fines up to ₹10 crore and up to 10 years imprisonment for paper leaks
### Right to be Forgotten — Delhi High Court guidelines
## 1. Ruling
- Case: Laksh Vir Singh Yadav v. Union of India — Delhi HC guidelines on Right to be Forgotten (removal / de-indexing of personal data)
- Part of Right to Privacy under Article 21; applies where old allegations or expired disputes cause harm without public interest — balanced against public's right to information
- K.S. Puttaswamy (2017): privacy a Fundamental Right
- DPDP Act, 2023 — implemented by MeitY
### MHA guidelines on Vande Mataram and Jana Gana Mana
## 1. Guidelines
- Order in official programmes with a State song: Vande Mataram → Jana Gana Mana → State Song
- Official Vande Mataram: six stanzas, 3 minutes 10 seconds; no change in words
## 2. Background
- Vande Mataram: Bankim Chandra Chattopadhyay, novel Anandamath (1882); adopted January 24, 1950
- Jana Gana Mana: Rabindranath Tagore; adopted January 24, 1950; duration 52 seconds
### ADR report on criminal cases against Chief Ministers
## 1. Findings
- ADR + National Election Watch analysed affidavits of 31 sitting CMs
- 14 of 31 (45%) declared criminal cases; about 11 (35%) serious charges (5+ years); two attempted-murder cases
- ⚠ Wealthiest CM: Karnataka CM D.K. Shivakumar — ₹1,413 crore+
- Karnataka: D.K. Shivakumar replaced Siddaramaiah (Jun 2026)
- ADR: est. 1999, New Delhi
### SC on SIR — ECI can examine but not decide citizenship
## 1. Verdict
- Case: Association for Democratic Reforms v. Election Commission of India
- Upheld: ECI order for Special Intensive Revision (SIR) of electoral rolls — conducted before November 2025 Bihar Assembly elections
- Held: SIR broadly proportionate; ECI is best placed to decide method of revision
- Citizenship: ECI may examine citizenship prima facie and contextually (for electoral roll only)
- ⚠ ECI cannot finally declare a person a non-citizen — deletion from roll ≠ loss of citizenship
- Referral: doubtful cases go to competent authority under Citizenship Act, 1955 — decide preferably before the next election in that constituency
- Presumption: name on roll gives only an evidentiary presumption of citizenship, not absolute
## 2. Safeguards ordered
- Aadhaar accepted as proof of identity
## 3. Background
- Article 324: superintendence, direction and control of elections — ECI
- RPA, 1950: preparation/revision of electoral rolls; Section 16 — non-citizen cannot be registered as voter
- Assam D-voter: 1997 revision marked "doubtful voters", referred to Foreigners Tribunals
### Right to be forgotten vs open justice
## 1. News
- Trigger: Delhi High Court order of 29 May 2026 on limited online discoverability of judicial records
- Right to be forgotten: removal / de-indexing of outdated personal info — called right to erasure in European law (GDPR)
- Linked to: informational privacy under Article 21 — ⚠ not absolute
- DPDP Act, 2023: recognises right to erasure; application to court records unclear
- Delhi HC (Justice Sachin Datta): recognised RTBF as part of privacy under Article 21; search engines may delist judgments linked to a name — mainly for acquitted persons, private civil disputes, non-parties
## 2. Key cases
| Case | Holding |
| Rajagopal v. State of TN (1994) | Right to be let alone; court records open to comment |
| K.S. Puttaswamy v. UoI (2017) | Privacy a fundamental right; informational privacy |
| Dharamraj Bhanushankar Dave v. State of Gujarat (2017) | Gujarat HC denied removal of acquittal details |
| Orissa HC (2020) | Online sexual abuse — called for debate on RTBF |
| Delhi HC (2021) | Allowed limited removal from search results |
| Supreme Court (2022) | Registry to remove a couple's details (marital dispute) from search engines |
| Kerala HC (2023) | RTBF not automatic for ongoing proceedings |
| Indian Kanoon matter (2024) | Warned obfuscation harms public record |
- PYQ: Right to Privacy — Article 21 and Part III freedoms
### Supreme Court strength raised to 38 by Ordinance
## 1. News
- Sanctioned strength: 34 → 38 judges via Presidential Ordinance (Article 123)
- Five judges sworn in: 4 former HC Chief Justices + 1 from the Bar; 3 against existing vacancies, 2 on new posts
- Concern: if Ordinance lapses, strength reverts to 34 — security of tenure, judicial independence
## 2. Background
- Ordinance: ceases six weeks after Parliament reassembles unless approved
- D.C. Wadhwa v. State of Bihar (1986): repeated re-promulgation unconstitutional
- Krishna Kumar Singh v. State of Bihar (2017): Ordinance not a substitute for legislation
- SC Advocates-on-Record Association (2015): struck down 99th Amendment and NJAC Act
- PYQ (2026): "law" under Article 13(3)(a) includes custom or usage having force of law

- Draft: Andaman and Nicobar Islands Tribal Councils (Preparation of Electoral Rolls and Conduct of Elections) Rules, 2026
- Proposes: formal elections for Village Councils and Tribal Councils; electoral rolls; delimitation; reservation for women; 5–9 Captains per village; direct vote for Chief Captain of each Island Tribal Council

### FCRA rules tightened and FCRA Amendment Bill, 2026
## 1. MHA notifications
- First: amends Foreign Contribution (Regulation) Rules, 2011 — registration tied to purposes from a Schedule of 105 permissible purposes; States/UTs of operation specified; existing NGOs get one year to choose
- Proselytisation: explicitly excluded from permitted religious activities
- Disclosures: social-media accounts, official website, ultimate/original donors (even for routed donations), donor-advised funds
- CA-360 details: FCRA (Amendment) Rules, 2026 notified 22 June 2026 with immediate effect; existing NGOs file Form FC-6F within one year; extra fee ₹300 per additional objective or State; Home Minister Amit Shah
- Second: revises compounding penalties under Section 41(1)
| Violation | Penalty (higher of) |
| Admin spend above 20% limit | ₹1 lakh or 5% of excess |
| Speculative / stock investment | ₹1 lakh or 30% |
| Diversion of funds | ₹1 lakh or 30% |
## 2. FCRA Amendment Bill, 2026
- Designated authority (appointed by Centre) to manage foreign-funded assets when registration is cancelled/surrendered/expires — replaces Section 15 framework
- Places of worship: management may be entrusted to another person, preserving religious character
## 3. Background
- FCRA 1976 → FCRA 2010; amended 2016, 2018, 2020
- Funds via designated SBI account, New Delhi; no transfer to other NGOs
- Barred: election candidates, parties, legislators, judges, govt servants, journalists, media companies
- Validity: 5 years; renew 6 months before expiry; cancellation if inactive 2 years; re-registration barred 3 years
- Article 25: profess, practise, propagate; Rev. Stainislaus v. State of MP (1977) — no fundamental right to convert another person

### Landmark rulings
## Definition of 'industry'
- Aug 2026 — 9-judge Bench led by CJI Surya Kant, 6:3 majority
- Held the 1978 Bangalore Water Supply definition does not apply to the Industrial Relations Code, 2020
- Bangalore Water Supply — 7-judge Bench led by Justice V.R. Krishna Iyer (Triple Test)
- IR Code 2020 merged Trade Unions Act 1926, Industrial Employment (Standing Orders) Act 1946 and Industrial Disputes Act, 1947
## BNSS & fair trial
- Section 187(2) — police custody limited to 15 days total, even if asked in parts in first 40/60 days
- Section 187(3) — detention up to 90 days (death, life, 10+ years) or 60 days (others), then default bail
- Section 356 — trial in absentia: court records reasons; absence taken as waiver of right to be present
- Article 22(1) — counsel of choice; Article 21 — fair trial part of right to life
- Article 39A — equal justice and free legal aid (DPSP)
- Kuldeep Agarwal (2019) — refusing in special cases is for an individual advocate, not the Bar
## Right to be forgotten
- Part of privacy / informational privacy under Article 21; Puttaswamy (2017) — privacy a Fundamental Right
- Delhi HC guidelines — Laksh Vir Singh Yadav v. Union of India; 29 May 2026 order — right to be forgotten vs open justice
- DPDP Act, 2023 — run by MeitY; accepts right to erasure, but its use for court records unclear
- Kerala HC (2023) — not automatic for pending cases
- Orissa HC (2020) — only asked for debate (online sexual abuse context)
- 2022 SC matter — couple's details removed from search engines
## SIR & citizenship
- ADR v. ECI — SC upheld the SIR order before Bihar elections as broadly proportionate
- ECI may examine citizenship; exclusion from roll is not a declaration of non-citizenship
- Name on roll — only an evidentiary presumption
- Doubtful cases sent to the Central Government under the Citizenship Act, 1955; revision power linked to Article 324 and RP Act 1950
- Passport — a travel document, not conclusive proof of citizenship (Passports Act 1967)
## Other rulings
- Bijoe Emmanuel v. State of Kerala — silence protected under Articles 19(1)(a) and 25
- Rev. Stainislaus v. State of MP (1977) — no fundamental right to convert another person under Article 25
- Right to walk on footpaths — under Articles 19(1)(d) and 21 (not Article 20); pedestrians come before vehicles
- JustIS App — for judges; e-Courts Services App — for litigants
- Nyaya Shruti (2024, ICJS) — virtual appearance of accused, witnesses and prisoners
### Creamy layer in SC/ST
- Ashoka Kumar Thakur (2008) — creamy layer does not apply to SCs and STs; Indra Sawhney upheld it for OBCs
- Davinder Singh (Aug 2024), 7-judge bench — States may sub-categorise SCs and STs

### Supreme Court judges' strength raised to 38
## 1. The Bill
- Bill: Supreme Court (Number of Judges) Amendment Bill, 2026 — passed by Lok Sabha; replaces an ordinance issued earlier in 2026
- Change: puisne judges 33 to 37; total 34 to 38 including the CJI
- Amends: Supreme Court (Number of Judges) Act, 1956
- Money Bill: 34 → 38 judges (37 + CJI); passed as a Money Bill (Aug 2026); amends 1956 Act (Art. 124)
- Ordinance: Supreme Court (Number of Judges) Amendment Ordinance, 2026 (May) under Art. 123 — 33 → 37 judges + CJI; replaced by a Bill passed by the LS on 3 Aug 2026
- SC strength: Raised to 38 incl. CJI (from 34) by ordinance (May 2026) + Bill (LS, 3 Aug 2026)
- ⚠ First increase since 2019 (31 to 34); no constitutional amendment needed — Article 124(1) lets Parliament fix the number by law
- Puisne judge: a Supreme Court judge other than the CJI
## 2. Why
- Pendency: about 92,101 cases on 1 January 2026
- ⚠ Sources differ: other notes say SC pendency ~88,000 cases (2025)
- 2025: 75,410 fresh cases vs 65,615 disposed
- Constitution Bench: at least 5 judges
- All courts backlog: over 5.5 crore cases
- India Justice Report 2025: about 15.9 judges per million; Law Commission recommended 50 per million
- ⚠ Sources differ: other notes say India Justice Report 2025: ~15 judges per million; 5,600+ vacancies; 5.5 crore+ pending cases
## 3. Evolution of strength (including CJI)
| Year | Strength |
| 1950 | 8 |
| 1956 | 11 |
| 2019 | 34 |
| 2026 | 38 |
## 4. Background
- Article 128: CJI, with President's consent, can ask a retired SC judge to sit
- Article 137: SC review power; Article 215: High Courts as courts of record
- Article 123: President's ordinance power
### Naxal-free India: strategy against Left-Wing Extremism
## 1. Milestone
- News: India effectively free from LWE — no district officially LWE-affected
- Origin: Naxalbari uprising, 1967, West Bengal; groups merged into CPI (Maoist) in 2004
- Peak: 2010 — 1,936 incidents; 2004–14: 17,542 incidents, 1,913 security personnel and 5,019 civilians killed
- Policy: National Policy and Action Plan to address LWE, 2015 — approach of Dialogue, Security and Coordination
## 2. Security
- 663 fortified police stations; 408 new CAPF camps (7 years); 68 night-landing helipads; 400 bullet-proof vehicles
- Forces: CoBRA (Commando Battalion for Resolute Action, CRPF), District Reserve Guard, Jharkhand Jaguar, Greyhounds
- Approach: "Trace, Target, Neutralise"
- NIA: dedicated vertical against Naxal financing
## 3. Surrender and development
- Surrender grant: ₹5 lakh (higher-ranked), ₹2.5 lakh (others); ₹10,000 a month for 36 months
- Surrenders: 2,337 in 2025; 3,927 between 2024 and March 2026
- Roads: 15,189 km (Road Requirement Plan, Road Connectivity Project for LWE areas)
- 2015–2026: 1,804 bank branches, 1,321 ATMs, 74,720 banking correspondents, 6,025 post offices
- EMRS: 259 sanctioned, 179 functional; 47 ITIs, 49 Skill Development Centres
### Creamy layer in SC/ST quotas: Centre opposes

### Police custody under BNSS Section 187(2): SC ruling
## 1. Case
- Case: State of Andhra Pradesh vs Suda Suresh Veera Venkata Naga Raju — custodial death probe in AP
- Ruling: police custody can be sought in parts during the initial 40 or 60 days; ⚠ total still capped at 15 days
- Magistrate cannot impose a blanket "no extension" condition; SC allowed 7 more days (within 15)
- Section 38 BNSS: accused may meet an advocate during interrogation, not throughout
## 2. Law
| Provision | Rule |
| Sec 58 BNSS | No detention beyond 24 hours without magistrate |
| Sec 187(2) BNSS | Police custody 15 days total, in parts, within first 40/60 days |
| Sec 187(3) BNSS | Detention up to 90 days (death/life/10+ years) or 60 days (others); then default bail |
| Sec 167 CrPC (old) | 15 days police custody, generally in the first 15 days |
### Vande Mataram gets protection: National Honour (Amendment) Act 2026
## 1. The amendment
- Act: Prevention of Insults to National Honour (Amendment) Act, 2026 — amends Section 3 of the 1971 Act
- Effect: National Song (Vande Mataram) gets same protection as National Anthem — punishes intentional prevention or disturbance of singing
- Punishment: up to 3 years, fine or both; Section 3A (added 2003) — repeat offence minimum 1 year
- ⚠ Does not make singing compulsory, prescribe stanzas or punish respectful silence
- Passage: introduced Rajya Sabha 24 July 2026; passed RS 29 July, LS 30 July
## 2. 1971 Act
- Enacted: 23 December 1971; Section 2 — Flag and Constitution (burning, defacing etc.)
## 3. Vande Mataram history
- Composer: Bankim Chandra Chatterjee; first published in Bangadarshan, 7 November 1875; in novel Anandamath (1882)
- Tagore sang it at 1896 INC session, Calcutta; slogan on 7 August 1905 (Swadeshi)
- October 1937: CWC — only first two stanzas at official gatherings
- 24 January 1950: Rajendra Prasad — equal honour with Jana Gana Mana; ⚠ Constitution does not mention a "National Song"
- Bijoe Emmanuel v. State of Kerala (1986): Jehovah's Witness students — silence protected under Art 19(1)(a) and Art 25
## 4. Extra
- ⚠ 2026 marks the 150th anniversary of Vande Mataram

### FCRA — FAQ and Amendment Bill 2026
## 1. About FCRA
- Foreign Contribution (Regulation) Act: administered by Ministry of Home Affairs
- History: first Act 1976; registration mandatory 1984; replaced by FCRA 2010; 2020 amendment — single SBI New Delhi Main Branch account, sub-granting banned; 2022 relatives' limit raised
- 2024–25: about 16,200 registered associations received about ₹22,963 crore
- ⚠ FCRA does not ban foreign donations — registration (organisations 3 years+ old) or prior permission
- Registration validity: 5 years; annual return Form FC-4
- Admin expense cap: 20% of annual FC
- Barred: election candidates, legislators, judges, public servants, political parties, political organisations, certain media persons
## 2. 2026 changes
- Provisional / permanent vesting of FC-created assets in Designated Authority (vesting concept from FCRA 2010)
- Revision and appeal to District Judge
- State agencies need Central approval before FCRA probes
- Registration to specify purpose and State/UT; existing bodies file Form FC-6F
- Renewal: must show utilisation of at least ₹10 lakh over prior two years
- Ultimate-donor, project-wise disclosure
## 3. Global comparison
| Country | Law |
| USA | FARA — Foreign Agents Registration Act |
| Australia | FITS — Foreign Influence Transparency Scheme |
| UK | FIRS — Foreign Influence Registration Scheme |
| Canada | Foreign Influence Transparency and Accountability Act |
### Bar Associations cannot refuse to represent an accused
## 1. What happened
- Faizabad/Ayodhya Bar Association resolved not to represent accused in alleged Ayodhya Ram Temple funds embezzlement case
- Legal position: such resolutions are illegal, unconstitutional, null and void
## 2. Constitutional basis
| Article | Provision |
| 22(1) | Arrested person's right to consult and be defended by a legal practitioner of choice |
| 21 | Fair trial part of right to life |
| 14 | Equality before law |
| 39A | Equal justice and free legal aid (DPSP) |
## 3. Cases
| Case | Holding |
| A.S. Mohammed Rafi v. State of Tamil Nadu (2010) | SC — boycott resolutions wholly illegal, null and void (Coimbatore police case) |
| Kuldeep Agarwal v. State of Uttarakhand (2019) | Uttarakhand HC — "special circumstances" refusal is for individual advocate, not Bar |
| Karnataka HC (2020) | Obstruction of lawyers for Kashmiri students may be contempt |
| Madras HC (2025) | Bar is not a trade union |
- BCI Rules: advocate generally bound to accept a brief; may refuse only in special circumstances
### Right to be Forgotten — Delhi High Court guidelines
## 1. Ruling
- Case: Laksh Vir Singh Yadav v. Union of India — Delhi HC guidelines on Right to be Forgotten (removal / de-indexing of personal data)
- Part of Right to Privacy under Article 21; applies where old allegations or expired disputes cause harm without public interest — balanced against public's right to information
- K.S. Puttaswamy (2017): privacy a Fundamental Right
- DPDP Act, 2023 — implemented by MeitY
### MHA guidelines on Vande Mataram and Jana Gana Mana
## 1. Guidelines
- Order in official programmes with a State song: Vande Mataram → Jana Gana Mana → State Song
- Official Vande Mataram: six stanzas, 3 minutes 10 seconds; no change in words
## 2. Background
- Vande Mataram: Bankim Chandra Chattopadhyay, novel Anandamath (1882); adopted January 24, 1950
- Jana Gana Mana: Rabindranath Tagore; adopted January 24, 1950; duration 52 seconds
### SC on SIR — ECI can examine but not decide citizenship
- Aadhaar — to be accepted as proof of identity in roll revision (not proof of citizenship)
### Right to be forgotten vs open justice
## 1. News
- Trigger: Delhi High Court order of 29 May 2026 on limited online discoverability of judicial records
- Right to be forgotten: removal / de-indexing of outdated personal info — called right to erasure in European law (GDPR)
- Linked to: informational privacy under Article 21 — ⚠ not absolute
- DPDP Act, 2023: recognises right to erasure; application to court records unclear
- Delhi HC (Justice Sachin Datta): recognised RTBF as part of privacy under Article 21; search engines may delist judgments linked to a name — mainly for acquitted persons, private civil disputes, non-parties
## 2. Key cases
| Case | Holding |
| Rajagopal v. State of TN (1994) | Right to be let alone; court records open to comment |
| K.S. Puttaswamy v. UoI (2017) | Privacy a fundamental right; informational privacy |
| Dharamraj Bhanushankar Dave v. State of Gujarat (2017) | Gujarat HC denied removal of acquittal details |
| Orissa HC (2020) | Online sexual abuse — called for debate on RTBF |
| Delhi HC (2021) | Allowed limited removal from search results |
| Supreme Court (2022) | Registry to remove a couple's details (marital dispute) from search engines |
| Kerala HC (2023) | RTBF not automatic for ongoing proceedings |
| Indian Kanoon matter (2024) | Warned obfuscation harms public record |
- PYQ: Right to Privacy — Article 21 and Part III freedoms
### Supreme Court strength raised to 38 by Ordinance
## 1. News
- Sanctioned strength: 34 → 38 judges via Presidential Ordinance (Article 123)
- Five judges sworn in: 4 former HC Chief Justices + 1 from the Bar; 3 against existing vacancies, 2 on new posts
- Concern: if Ordinance lapses, strength reverts to 34 — security of tenure, judicial independence
## 2. Background
- Ordinance: ceases six weeks after Parliament reassembles unless approved
- D.C. Wadhwa v. State of Bihar (1986): repeated re-promulgation unconstitutional
- Krishna Kumar Singh v. State of Bihar (2017): Ordinance not a substitute for legislation
- SC Advocates-on-Record Association (2015): struck down 99th Amendment and NJAC Act
- PYQ (2026): "law" under Article 13(3)(a) includes custom or usage having force of law
### Nicobar tribal council election rules debate
- Coral reefs — Andaman and Nicobar, Gulf of Kachchh, Gulf of Mannar
### FCRA rules tightened and FCRA Amendment Bill, 2026
## 1. MHA notifications
- First: amends Foreign Contribution (Regulation) Rules, 2011 — registration tied to purposes from a Schedule of 105 permissible purposes; States/UTs of operation specified; existing NGOs get one year to choose
- Proselytisation: explicitly excluded from permitted religious activities
- Disclosures: social-media accounts, official website, ultimate/original donors (even for routed donations), donor-advised funds
- CA-360 details: FCRA (Amendment) Rules, 2026 notified 22 June 2026 with immediate effect; existing NGOs file Form FC-6F within one year; extra fee ₹300 per additional objective or State; Home Minister Amit Shah
- Second: revises compounding penalties under Section 41(1)
| Violation | Penalty (higher of) |
| Admin spend above 20% limit | ₹1 lakh or 5% of excess |
| Speculative / stock investment | ₹1 lakh or 30% |
| Diversion of funds | ₹1 lakh or 30% |
## 2. FCRA Amendment Bill, 2026
- Designated authority (appointed by Centre) to manage foreign-funded assets when registration is cancelled/surrendered/expires — replaces Section 15 framework
- Places of worship: management may be entrusted to another person, preserving religious character
## 3. Background
- FCRA 1976 → FCRA 2010; amended 2016, 2018, 2020
- Funds via designated SBI account, New Delhi; no transfer to other NGOs
- Barred: election candidates, parties, legislators, judges, govt servants, journalists, media companies
- Validity: 5 years; renew 6 months before expiry; cancellation if inactive 2 years; re-registration barred 3 years
- Article 25: profess, practise, propagate; Rev. Stainislaus v. State of MP (1977) — no fundamental right to convert another person
### Polity and judiciary one-liners — June 2026
## 1. Judiciary
- ⚠ First time four High Courts simultaneously led by full-time women Chief Justices: Meenakshi Madan Rai (Patna), Sunita Agarwal (Gujarat), Revati Mohite Dere (Meghalaya), Lisa Gill (Andhra Pradesh); India has 25 HCs
- ⚠ In 2018 four women also headed HCs but one was acting; women = 116 of 781 HC judges (14.85%, Feb 2026); HC CJs appointed by President — Article 217
- Right to walk on footpaths: SC (Justices P.S. Narasimha, A.S. Chandurkar) — a fundamental right under Article 19(1)(d) and Article 21; pedestrians take precedence over vehicles
- India–Russia: Supreme Courts signed judicial cooperation MoU in Moscow — CJI Surya Kant and Russian CJ Igor Krasnov
- India–Russia MoU: CJI Surya Kant — AI may aid translation/transcription but must not influence judicial decisions
## 2. Bodies and law officers
- Passport ≠ proof of citizenship: MEA clarification (no new policy on 24 June 2026) — passport is a travel document and may be issued to some non-citizens; Passports Act, 1967 vs Citizenship Act, 1955
- NCW recommended a National Prisons Commission in Law Review 2025–26 on women prisoners (NCW est. 1992, chair Vijaya Rahatkar)
- Solicitor General Tushar Mehta: tenure extended 3 years from 1 July 2026; ASGs Vikramjit Banerjee, K.M. Nataraj, Surya Prakash V. Raju, N. Venkataraman, Aishwarya Bhati also extended
- Attorney General: highest law officer — Article 76
## 3. ADR report on women candidates
- Since Sept 2023: only 5,095 of 51,708 candidates (~10%) were women
- 2024 Lok Sabha: women 9.6% of 8,360 candidates; AP Assembly 2024 — 9.7%
- Highest share: Odisha (13.9%), Delhi (13.7%), Puducherry (13.6%)
- Women's Reservation Act, 2023: 33% in Lok Sabha and Assemblies — after next Census & delimitation (ADR est. 1999)

### Election Commissioners appointment — "tyranny of the elected"
## 1. News

- Hearing challenge (by ADR, 2024) to CEC and Other ECs (Appointment, Conditions of Service and Term of Office) Act, 2023

| PM | PM (Chairperson) |
| Leader of Opposition (LS) | Leader of Opposition (LS) |

- Search Committee: headed by Law Minister + 2 senior bureaucrats → shortlist of 5
- Section 8: Selection Committee may go beyond shortlist; Section 7(2): vacancy does not invalidate appointment

## 1. Facts
- Introduced 25 May 2026 by Parliamentary Affairs Minister Atul Bora; passed 27 May 2026 by voice vote
- Uniform law on marriage, divorce, inheritance, live-in (registration within 1 month); prohibits polygamy
- ⚠ Scheduled Tribes exempted; pre-existing polygamous marriages protected
- Repeals Assam Compulsory Registration of Muslim Marriages and Divorces Act, 2024
- ⚠ Third State after Uttarakhand and Gujarat; first in Northeast
- Article 44 (DPSP) — UCC; Assam: CM Himanta Biswa Sarma, Governor Lakshman Prasad Acharya
### Vande Mataram to get status equal to National Anthem

### Citizenship (Amendment) Rules, 2026 — e-OCI
## 1. Facts
- Effective 30 April 2026; amend Citizenship Rules, 2009 (under Citizenship Act, 1955)
- Minors cannot hold both Indian and foreign passports (single citizenship)
- e-OCI: digital records, physical or electronic OCI cards, centralised database; OCI introduced 2005
### Promotion and Regulation of Online Gaming Rules, 2026
## 1. Rules
- Notified by: MeitY; in force 1 May 2026; under Section 19 of PROG Act, 2025
- PROG Act: passed August 2025, implemented October 2025 — bans online money games; promotes e-sports and online social games
- Structure: 6 Parts, 26 Rules
## 2. Online Gaming Authority of India
- Chair: Additional Secretary, MeitY (ex officio); JS-level members from MHA, DFS, I&B, Youth Affairs & Sports, Legal Affairs
- Rule 9: objective factors (stakes, winnings, revenue model); Rule 10: determination within 90 days
- Registration certificate: valid up to 10 years; mandatory for e-sports; ⚠ money game cannot be registered as e-sport
- Grievances: service provider → Authority (within 30 days) → Secretary, MeitY (appellate)
- Penalties: Rules 21–22; proceedings within 90 days
## 3. Concern — offshore shift (CUTS International study)
- Sivaganga (TN) fraud, Feb 2026 — fake "Old Coin Purchase Task" on Telegram; mule accounts
- UAE moved to licensing framework in 2023; Sri Lanka Gambling Regulatory Authority by June 2026
### Forest Rights Act — Allahabad vs Madras HC rulings
## 1. Rulings
| Court | Ruling |
| Allahabad HC | Set aside DLC rejection of Tharu tribe claims (UP); FRA overrides earlier laws |
| Madras HC | Upheld eviction notices and grazing bans under Tamil Nadu Forest Act, 1882 despite pending FRA claims |
- Doctrine: lex posterior derogat priori — later law overrides earlier law
## 2. Background — FRA 2006
- Official name: Scheduled Tribes and Other Traditional Forest Dwellers (Recognition of Forest Rights) Act, 2006
- Gram Sabha central; no eviction until verification complete
- Rights: IFR, CFR (grazing, MFP, fishing), Habitat rights for PVTGs, developmental rights
- ⚠ Grazing rights recognised even in national parks and sanctuaries
### SC: 12 weeks maternity leave for all adoptive mothers
## 1. Ruling
- Case: Hamsanandini Nanduri v. Union of India; Bench Justice J.B. Pardiwala and Justice R. Mahadevan (March 2026)
- Struck down Section 60(4) of Code on Social Security, 2020 — earlier only mothers adopting child below 3 months got 12 weeks
- Now all adoptive mothers get 12 weeks irrespective of child's age; violated Article 14 and Article 21
### SC permits euthanasia of rabid and incurably ill stray dogs
## 1. Ruling
- 19 May 2026; Bench Justices Vikram Nath, Sandeep Mehta, N.V. Anjaria
- Euthanasia only after vet examination; in line with Animal Birth Control Rules, 2023
- Refused to modify 7 November 2025 order on removing stray dogs from schools, hospitals, bus stations; sterilised dogs not to be released at same public places
- One ABC Centre in every district; anti-rabies medicines
- Prevention of Cruelty to Animals Act, 1960
### Judicial digitisation — Sikkim paperless, One Case One Data, Su Sahay
## 1. Sikkim

- Sikkim: CM Prem Singh Tamang, Governor Om Prakash Mathur, statehood 16 May 1975, 32 seats

- Headed by Justice Aravind Kumar; target funding ₹40,000–50,000 crore; report by 31 August 2026 (to Sanjeev Sanyal, PM-EAC)
## 3. One Case One Data and Su Sahay

- Su Sahay: AI chatbot launched 11 May 2026 — by NIC + SC Registry (NIC est. 1976, under MeitY)
- ⚠ Sources differ: other notes say "One Case One Data" + "Su Sahayak" (May 2026)

- SC special Lok Adalat on 21–23 August 2026 (physical or virtual; register by 31 May 2026) to settle pending cases by mutual consent; NALSA est. 1995; Legal Services Authorities Act, 1987 (in force 1995)
- Law Minister: Arjun Ram Meghwal
### Bhojshala is a Saraswati Temple — MP High Court
## 1. Ruling
- Madhya Pradesh HC (Justices Vijay Kumar Shukla and Alok Awasthi): Bhojshala Complex, Dhar district, historically a Saraswati Temple and centre of Sanskrit learning — linked to King Bhoja of the Parmar (Rajput) dynasty
- Based on ASI survey (2024); Centre and ASI to decide management; Muslim side may seek alternative land for a mosque
- Muslims call it Kamal Maula Mosque; under 2003 ASI arrangement — Hindus pray on Tuesdays, Muslims on Fridays
- ASI: est. 1861, Ministry of Culture; MP CM Mohan Yadav, Governor Mangubhai Patel
### Vande Mataram mandatory in West Bengal madrasas
## 1. Facts
- West Bengal made Vande Mataram compulsory in morning assemblies of all recognised madrasas (Minority Affairs and Madrasah Education Department) — after making it mandatory in all schools
- Written by Bankim Chandra Chattopadhyay, in novel Anandamath
- West Bengal: CM Suvendu Adhikari, Governor R.N. Ravi
### Inter-State redistribution of Lok Sabha seats on 2011 Census
## 1. Proposal
- Union considering Constitutional Amendment + fresh delimitation on 2011 Census; Lok Sabha to ~850 seats; enables 33% women's reservation
- Now: inter-State allocation on 1971 Census; constituency boundaries on 2001 Census
## 2. Freeze history
| Amendment | Effect |
| 42nd (1976) | Froze seats on 1971 Census — to reward population control |
| 84th (2001) | Freeze extended till first Census after 2026 |
| 87th (2003) | Constituency delimitation on 2001 Census; seat totals still frozen |
- Art 82 (Lok Sabha), Art 170 (Assemblies); Delimitation Commission orders cannot be challenged in courts
- Principle: "one person, one vote, one value"
## 3. Parliament static GK
- Art 79: Parliament = President + Lok Sabha + Rajya Sabha
- Art 80: RS max 250 (238 elected + 12 nominated); RS permanent — one-third retire every two years; seats by population (not equal)
- RS members elected only by elected MLAs (⚠ not MLCs) — STV proportional representation
- Art 170: Assembly max 500, min 60 (exceptions: Goa, Sikkim, Mizoram)
- Art 333 Anglo-Indian nomination abolished by 104th Amendment (2019)
## 4. Constitution (131st Amendment) Bill and Delimitation Bill
- Introduced: Constitution (131st Amendment) Bill — Lok Sabha max 850 (815 States + 35 UTs); Delimitation Bill on 2011 Census; one-third women's reservation
- Amends Art 81, Art 82 (removes post-2026 freeze) and Art 334A (early women's reservation)
- "Schedule" mechanism: law to list State-wise seats — Lok Sabha up ~50% while keeping each State's percentage share unchanged
- Earlier constitutional max: 550 members
- Art 75(1A): Council of Ministers ≤ 15% of Lok Sabha → up to ~122 ministers at 850
- Art 108 joint sitting — larger Lok Sabha dominance; ⚠ Rajya Sabha strength (Art 80) does not change automatically
- Gainers: UP, Bihar, Rajasthan, MP; relative losers: Kerala, Tamil Nadu
## 5. Delimitation Commission facts
- Constituted four times: 1952, 1963, 1973, 2002; last exercise completed 2008 (2001 Census)
- Statutory body under Delimitation Act; appointed by President; chair — retired SC judge; members — CEC + State Election Commissioners
- Orders not needing Parliament approval; ⚠ Article 329 bars courts; decides SC/ST reserved constituencies
- Art 331 (2 Anglo-Indian LS nominees) — discontinued by 104th Amendment (2019)
## 6. 131st Amendment Bill defeated
- ⚠ Constitution (131st Amendment) Bill, 2026 was defeated in April 2026 — revived debate on delinking women's reservation from delimitation
- Bill sought max Lok Sabha 550 → 850 (Art 81); changes in Arts 82, 170, 334A for delimitation on 2011 Census; needed special majority + ratification by half of States
- Package of three Bills: 131st Amendment Bill, Delimitation Bill, 2026 (new Commission replacing Delimitation Act, 2002 framework; would fix SC, ST and women's seats), Union Territories Laws (Amendment) Bill, 2026 (Delhi, J&K, Puducherry)
- Delimitation Bill 2026 withdrawn after the defeat; the Bill sought women's quota by 2029
- Women's representation: Lok Sabha ~14–15%; State Assemblies ~9%; Rwanda >60%, Sweden ~46%
- History: first Women's Reservation Bill 1996; passed Rajya Sabha in 2010 but lapsed; enacted 2023; ⚠ no OBC sub-quota
- Next Census ~2027 → implementation likely ~2033–34
- Delimitation Commission — temporary body, dissolves after work; orders laid before Parliament for information only; Art 329(a)
- Art 243T: SC/ST seats in every municipality in proportion to population; Art 169: Council abolition by ordinary law after Assembly special-majority resolution
## 7. Lok Sabha vote on 131st Amendment Bill
- 528 voted — 298 in favour, 230 against; ⚠ failed to get required special majority of 326 (Art 368)
- 106th Amendment Act brought into force from April 16, 2026 (procedural step); Act passed September 2023; valid 15 years
- Census every 10 years under Census Act, 1948; Women's Reservation Bill first introduced 1996
- Lok Sabha: est. 1952; max 552 (as per source); current 543; Art 83 — 5-year tenure
- ⚠ CA360 phrasing: 42nd Amendment (1976) froze seats until 2000; 84th extended freeze till 2026
### Lok Sabha expansion to ~816 seats for women's reservation
## 1. Proposal
- Aim: implement women's reservation without waiting for delimitation after next Census
- Strength: 543 → ~816 members; ~273 seats (one-third) reserved for women
- From: 2029 Lok Sabha elections; state-wise share of seats kept same in initial phase (1971 Census basis)
| Parameter | Current | Proposed |
| Total seats | 543 | ~816 |
| Women representation | ~14% | ~33% |
| Basis of allocation | 1971 Census | Same (initial phase) |
| Delimitation | Mandatory for change | Temporarily bypassed |
## 2. Constitutional angle
- Article 81: composition of Lok Sabha — needs amendment to raise seats
- Article 82: readjustment / delimitation after each Census
- 106th Constitutional Amendment Act, 2023 (Women's Reservation / Nari Shakti Vandan) — 33% reservation in Lok Sabha and State Assemblies; linked to delimitation
- Amendment needs special majority: two-thirds of members present and voting + majority of total membership
- ⚠ Seat freeze based on 1971 Census continues until after 2026 (first Census after 2026)
- ⚠ Expansion does not automatically trigger delimitation
## 3. Women's Reservation Act — features and roadmap
- Articles inserted: 330A (Lok Sabha), 332A (State Assemblies), 334A (commencement & duration)
- Duration: 15 years; sub-reservation for SC/ST women; rotation of reserved seats after each delimitation
- Linked to delimitation after first Census after 2026 — implementation unlikely before 2032–33
- Delimitation: Articles 82 and 170; Delimitation Commission appointed by President; orders have force of law — ⚠ not subject to judicial review
- Seat allocation freeze till after 2026 — 84th Amendment
- Proposed mechanism: ~273 extra women's seats allotted to parties by vote share in last general election (limited proportional representation) from pre-declared lists — hybrid with FPTP; temporary for one or two cycles
### Census 2027 begins — first fully digital census
## 1. Key facts
- 16th Census (8th since Independence) launched April 1, 2026 — ⚠ first fully digital; self-enumeration in 16 languages; 30 lakh+ staff
- Phase I (April–September 2026): house listing; Phase II (February 2027): population, caste
- Reference date March 1, 2027; snowbound areas (Ladakh, J&K, HP, Uttarakhand) October 1, 2026
- Cost ₹11,718.24 crore; ⚠ first full caste enumeration since 1931
- Census Act 1948, Census Rules 1990; first census 1872, first complete 1881; 2011 population 1.2108 billion
### Sabarimala review — nine-judge Bench
## 1. News
- Supreme Court constituted a nine-judge Bench for Sabarimala review petitions — case Kantaru Rajeevaru v Indian Young Lawyers Association
- Centre (2026): rigid definitions of "religious denomination" and "essential religious practices" distort plural nature of Hinduism
## 2. Background
- Temple: in Periyar Tiger Reserve, Kerala; deity Lord Ayyappa (celibate); 41-day penance
- Women aged 10–50 barred; upheld by Kerala HC in 1991 (S. Mahendran case)
- Administered by Travancore Devaswom Board; Rule 3(b) of Kerala Hindu Places of Public Worship Rules, 1965
| Year | Development |
| 2017 | Five-judge Constitution Bench |
| 2018 | 4:1 verdict — exclusion unconstitutional; Rule 3(b) struck down; dissent by Justice Indu Malhotra |
| 2019 | Referred to larger Bench |
## 3. Articles involved
- 14 (equality), 15 (non-discrimination), 17 (untouchability), 25 (freedom of religion), 26 (denominational rights — subject to public order, morality, health; ⚠ not absolute)
- Article 27: no tax for promotion of a religion; Article 28(1): no religious instruction in wholly State-funded institutions
- Judicial review basis: Articles 13, 32, 226; India follows procedure established by law
- Mazdoor Kisan Shakti Sangathan v UoI (2018): Section 144 CrPC cannot suppress legitimate protest — Article 19(1)(b)
## 4. Nine-judge Bench details
- Bench chaired by CJI Surya Kant; hearing began April 7, 2026; members include B.V. Nagarathna, M.M. Sundresh, Ahsanuddin Amanullah, Aravind Kumar, Augustine George Masih, Prasanna B. Varale, R. Mahadevan, Joymalya Bagchi
- Shirur Mutt case (1954) — established essential religious practices doctrine
- Art 145(3): substantial constitutional questions need at least five judges
- 2019 reference — 7 questions framed in 2020; interventions by Muslim Personal Law Board, Jain organisations
- Sabarimala reference: judgment reserved after 16 days of hearing (May 2026)
### Removal notices against CEC Gyanesh Kumar rejected
## 1. News
- Notices to remove CEC Gyanesh Kumar — signed by 63 Rajya Sabha and 130 Lok Sabha MPs — rejected by RS Chairman and LS Speaker: no prima facie "proved misbehaviour"
- Issues cited: bias, data non-disclosure (consistent with Puttaswamy privacy), Special Intensive Revision (SIR) of rolls
## 2. CEC — key facts
- ECI: constitutional body under Article 324; established 25 January 1950; single-member till 1989
- Appointment: by President on recommendation of selection committee — PM, LoP in Lok Sabha, a Union Cabinet Minister (CEC and Other ECs Act, 2023)
- Tenure: 6 years or 65 years of age, whichever earlier
- Removal: Article 324(5) — like SC judge; proved misbehaviour or incapacity; special majority in both Houses
- ECI conducts elections to Parliament, State Legislatures, President, Vice-President
## 3. Key judgments
| Case | Held |
| Mohinder Singh Gill (1978) | Art 324 gives plenary powers where law is silent |
| T.N. Seshan v UoI (1995) | Multi-member EC valid; CEC not superior to ECs in decisions |
| Anoop Baranwal v UoI (2023) | Committee of PM, LoP, CJI till Parliament makes law |
### Jan Vishwas (Amendment of Provisions) Bill 2026 passed
## 1. News
- Passed by Parliament — decriminalises minor offences; trust-based governance, ease of doing business
## 2. Key provisions
- Imprisonment replaced by monetary/civil penalties for minor offences; obsolete provisions removed
- Graded compliance: warnings and improvement notices for initial contraventions before penalties
- Enforcement shifted from criminal courts to administrative adjudication — designated officers + appellate authorities
- Periodic revision of fines — 10% increase every three years
- ⚠ Does not abolish penalties — converts criminal to civil penalties; expands earlier (2023, 2025 version) Jan Vishwas reforms
## 3. Jan Vishwas 2.0 details
- Called Jan Vishwas 2.0; ⚠ another source: amends 79 Central Acts, decriminalises ~717 provisions (vs "~80 Acts, 1,000+ provisions")
- Jan Vishwas Act, 2023: amended 42 Central laws
- Adjudicating Officers impose penalties; structured appeal; serious offences (public safety, environment, fraud) keep criminal liability
- Linked to World Bank B-READY (Business Ready) framework; concern — regulatory capture, fines as cost of business
### Seven of ten AAP Rajya Sabha MPs merge with BJP — anti-defection law
## 1. News
- 7 of AAP's 10 Rajya Sabha MPs announced merger with BJP — claim merger protection (two-thirds) under Tenth Schedule; precedent — TDP Rajya Sabha case
## 2. Anti-defection law
- 52nd Amendment, 1985 added Tenth Schedule; 91st Amendment, 2003 removed "split" (one-third), kept merger (two-thirds)
- Grounds: voluntarily giving up membership; defying whip (not condoned within 15 days); independent joining a party; nominated member joining after 6 months
- Members not joining merged party and staying separate are also protected
- Decided by Speaker/Chairman (⚠ not President/ECI)
| Case | Held |
| Kihoto Hollohan v Zachillhu (1992) | Speaker's decision subject to judicial review |
| Ravi S. Naik (1994) | Defection inferred from conduct |
| Keisham Meghachandra Singh (2020) | Decide within 3 months; independent tribunal suggested |
| Padi Kaushik Reddy v Telangana (2025) | Reforms for timely decisions |
## 3. RS Chairman
- Vice-President is ex-officio Chairman (Arts 64, 89); only casting vote; ⚠ LS Speaker certifies Money Bills
### Harivansh re-elected Rajya Sabha Deputy Chairman
## 1. Key facts
- Harivansh elected Deputy Chairman, Rajya Sabha for the ⚠ third time (voice vote, motion by J.P. Nadda) — post vacant after his second RS term ended April 9; election under Rule 7 by Chairman C.P. Radhakrishnan
- ⚠ He is now a nominated member (nominated by President Droupadi Murmu) — first nominated member to become Deputy Chairman
- Art 89(1): election of Deputy Chairman; 89(2): ceases on losing membership; Art 80: 12 nominated
- RS est. 1952; max 250 (238 + 12); current 245
### Samrat Choudhary sworn in as Bihar Chief Minister
## 1. Key facts
- BJP leader Samrat Choudhary unanimously elected NDA legislature leader and sworn in as Bihar CM in Patna — ⚠ first BJP Chief Minister of Bihar
- Followed resignation of Nitish Kumar after he was elected to the Rajya Sabha; Governor Syed Ata Hasnain
- Bihar: bicameral legislature; Assembly 243 seats
- Bihar, Nov 2025 (243 seats): NDA 202; BJP 89 (largest), JD(U) 85; Nitish Kumar sworn in for a record 10th time, later moved to the Rajya Sabha
- Samrat Choudhary (BJP) became Bihar's first BJP CM (Apr 2026; another note: CM from Mar 2026)
### Puducherry elections — UTs with legislature compared
## 1. Three models
| UT | Basis | Key point |
| Puducherry | Art 239A (14th Amendment, 1962) + Govt of UTs Act, 1963 | Legislature created by law; unicameral with elected + nominated members; closest to State |
| Delhi | Art 239AA (69th Amendment, 1991) — NCT | Legislature in Constitution; no power over public order, police, land |
| J&K | J&K Reorganisation Act, 2019 | Least autonomy; police, public order with LG; Ladakh UT without legislature |
- Puducherry — ex-French territory, integrated 1962
- K. Lakshminarayanan v UoI (2019): Puducherry LG cannot act independently in routine matters
## 2. Key articles
| Article | Provision |
| 239 | UTs administered by President through Administrator |
| 239AB | President's Rule in Delhi (like Art 356) |
| 240 | President's regulations for Andaman, Lakshadweep etc. |
| 246(4) | ⚠ Parliament can legislate on any subject (even State List) for UTs |
- Delhi LG can refer disagreement with Council of Ministers to President; disputes over control of "services"
### Meghalaya — Khasi and Garo made official languages
## 1. News
- Meghalaya Cabinet approved Meghalaya Official Languages Ordinance, 2026 — Khasi and Garo official alongside English; renewed demand for Eighth Schedule
- Legislative use after amending Meghalaya State Legislature (Continuance of English Language) Act, 1980
## 2. Official language provisions (Part XVII)
| Article | Provision |
| 343 | Hindi in Devanagari = Union official language; English for 15 years (till 1965) |
| 344 | Official Language Commission and Committee |
| 345 | State may adopt one or more languages or Hindi |
| 347 | President may recognise a language spoken by a section |
| 351 | Develop Hindi |
- Official Languages Act, 1963: English continues as associate language
- Eighth Schedule: originally 14 → now 22 languages; inclusion by constitutional amendment; ⚠ not part of Part XVII; ⚠ inclusion ≠ Union official language
| Amendment | Languages added |
| 21st (1967) | Sindhi |
| 71st (1992) | Konkani, Manipuri, Nepali |
| 92nd (2003) | Bodo, Santhali, Maithili, Dogri |
- ⚠ 102nd Amendment (2018) — NCBC constitutional status (not languages); classical language status is independent of Eighth Schedule
- Art 120(1): Parliament business in Hindi or English; presiding officer may allow mother tongue
- Ordinance under Art 213; Meghalaya CM Conrad Sangma; Governor C.H. Vijayshankar; state since 1972
### Women are 49.7% of elected Panchayat representatives
## 1. News
- Centre: women = 49.7% of elected PRI representatives — 12.14 lakh of 24.41 lakh; winning many unreserved seats too
- 19 States/UTs give 50% reservation (e.g. Bihar, Rajasthan, MP, AP, HP, Odisha); Gujarat, Haryana, Tripura fell short of targets (2025 data)
- ⚠ Ministry of Panchayati Raj has no national data on reserved vs unreserved wins; "Sarpanch Pati" proxy problem
## 2. 73rd Amendment (1992) facts
- In force 24 April 1993; Part IX (Arts 243–243O); Eleventh Schedule — 29 subjects
- Three tiers; intermediate tier optional for States with population below 20 lakh
- Art 243D: not less than one-third for women (⚠ Constitution mandates 1/3, not 50%) — seats and chairpersons; horizontal
- Art 243E: 5-year term; fresh polls within 6 months of dissolution; not needed if remainder < 6 months
- Art 243K: State Election Commission; Art 243I: State Finance Commission (every 5 years)
- ⚠ District Planning Committee (Art 243ZD) — from 74th Amendment, not 73rd
### SC — right to safe travel on highways is part of Article 21
## 1. News
- Supreme Court (suo motu, after November 2025 accidents in Rajasthan and Telangana killing 34) — safe travel on highways is part of Right to Life (Art 21)
- Banned new dhabas, eateries, fuel points, commercial structures within right-of-way of National Highways; heavy vehicles only in designated bays
- National Highways ~2% of road network but ~30% of road deaths
## 2. Article 21 judgments
| Case | Right |
| Hussainara Khatoon (1979) | Speedy trial |
| Olga Tellis (1985) | Livelihood |
| K.S. Puttaswamy (2017) | Privacy |
| Common Cause (2018) | Die with dignity (passive euthanasia) |
| Maneka Gandhi (1978) | "Golden Triangle" — Arts 14, 19, 21; Passport Act reviewable |
- ⚠ Art 21 applies to non-citizens too; cannot be suspended in Emergency (44th Amendment)
- Indra Sawhney — 27% OBC, 50% cap, creamy layer; S.R. Bommai (1994) — Art 356 judicial review, floor test
- Vishaka (1997) — workplace harassment; NALSA (2014) — third gender; Navtej Johar (2018) — Sec 377
### MHA declares Naxalism eliminated
## 1. Key facts
