- AP: Alluri Sitharama Raju; Telangana: Bhadradri Kothagudem, Mulugu; Jharkhand: West Singhbhum (District of Concern)

### Sattankulam custodial death verdict — death penalty to nine policemen
## 1. News
- Tamil Nadu trial court awarded death penalty to nine policemen — Sattankulam custodial murder case (2020); "rarest of rare"
- ~170 custodial deaths in 2025–26; high in Bihar, Rajasthan; India has not ratified UNCAT; no anti-torture law
## 2. Constitutional and legal safeguards
- Art 21 (life, dignity), Art 20(3) (no self-incrimination), Art 22 (arrest safeguards)
- NHRC: custodial deaths to be reported within 24 hours; ⚠ can only recommend, cannot punish
- CrPC Sec 41/41A: reasons for arrest/non-arrest; notice of appearance; Sec 176: magisterial inquiry into custodial death (⚠ not by police officer)

| Maneka Gandhi | Procedure must be fair, just, reasonable |

| Joginder Kumar v UP | Arrest must not be arbitrary |

| Prakash Singh v UoI | Police Complaints Authorities |
| Paramvir Singh Saini v Baljit Singh | CCTV mandatory in police stations |

### Delimitation on 2011 Census to fast-track women's reservation
## 1. News
- Centre considering delimitation based on 2011 Census to implement Women's Reservation Act, 2023 before the 2029 Lok Sabha polls
- Lok Sabha: 543 → 816 seats (~50% rise); ~273 seats for women; state proportions unchanged; possible lottery, every third seat
- Govt plan: Seats up ~50% while keeping southern States' share (23.8%); 129 → 195 under an 816-seat formula
- Amendments needed: Article 81, Article 170, Delimitation Commission Act — two-thirds majority
## 2. Women's Reservation Act
- 106th Constitutional Amendment Act, 2023 — Nari Shakti Vandan Adhiniyam
- 33% in Lok Sabha, State Assemblies, Delhi Assembly — incl. within SC/ST seats; ⚠ not Rajya Sabha or Legislative Councils
- 15 years validity; rotation after each delimitation
- Delimitation: Article 82 (Lok Sabha), Article 170 (States)
- Representation: ~15% Lok Sabha (2019–24), ~9% assemblies; 5% in first Lok Sabha
## 3. History
- Bills: 1996, 1998, 1999, 2008
- Committees: CSWI (1971), Margaret Alva (1987), Geeta Mukherjee (1996) — OBC women
- 73rd/74th Amendments (1992): 33% in PRIs and ULBs
- WEF Gender Gap 2023: India 25.3% parity in political empowerment; CEDAW 1979
### SC: conversion ends Scheduled Caste status
## 1. News
- Supreme Court: SC status limited to Hindus, Sikhs, Buddhists; conversion to other religions → immediate and complete loss of SC status
- Case: from Andhra Pradesh High Court — complaint under SC/ST (PoA) Act by Pastor Chintada Anand Paul; HC quashed charges
- ⚠ AP's 1977 Government Order gives only non-statutory concessions, not SC status
- Reconversion: must prove return, renunciation and acceptance by original caste — burden on claimant
## 2. Constitution (Scheduled Castes) Order, 1950
- Issued under Article 341(1); Clause 3 — only Hindus, Sikhs, Buddhists
- 1950 Hindus only → 1956 Sikhs added → 1990 Buddhists added
- Article 341(2): only Parliament can amend the list; roots in GoI Act 1935 (1936 Order)
| Aspect | SC | ST |
| Article | 341 | 342 |
| Basis | Untouchability | Tribal characteristics, isolation |
## 3. More facts
- SC upheld the AP High Court judgment of 30 April 2025; Bench Justices Prashant Kumar Mishra and Manmohan dismissed the SLP
- Related Articles: 341, 342, 15(4), 16(4)
### No-confidence motion against Lok Sabha Speaker Om Birla
## 1. News
- Opposition moved a motion for removal of Speaker Om Birla during the Budget Session
- Allegations: suspension of 100 Opposition MPs (Dec 2023); mics switched off; no Deputy Speaker for 12 years
## 2. Constitutional provisions
| Article | Provision |
| Article 93 | Election of Speaker and Deputy Speaker |
| Article 94(c) | Removal by resolution passed by a majority of all the members |
| Article 96 | Speaker shall not preside while removal resolution is under consideration |
| Article 100 | Voting; Speaker's casting vote in a tie |
## 3. Removal procedure
- 14 days' written notice to the Secretary-General; support of at least 50 members; Lok Sabha Rules 200–203
- ⚠ During the debate the Speaker can speak and vote in the first instance, but no casting vote
- Speaker's decisions final: Money Bill certification; Tenth Schedule disqualification
### Right to die with dignity — Harish Rana case
## 1. News
- Supreme Court allowed withdrawal of life support for Harish Rana — right to die with dignity part of Article 21
- Held: CANH (Clinically Assisted Nutrition and Hydration) is medical treatment; withdrawal in patient's best interest (family + medical boards)
## 2. Evolution of law
| Case | Holding |
| Gian Kaur v. State of Punjab (1996) | Right to live with dignity; ⚠ rejected right to die |
| Aruna Shanbaug v. Union of India (2011) | Passive euthanasia allowed in exceptional cases |
| Common Cause v. Union of India (2018) | Right to refuse treatment; living wills; guidelines simplified in 2023 |
- Passive euthanasia: withholding/withdrawing life-sustaining treatment — legal under strict conditions
- Article 21 cases: Hussainara Khatoon (speedy trial); Suchita Srivastava, Puttaswamy (reproductive choice)
## 3. More facts
- ⚠ First time the SC actually implemented passive euthanasia in a specific case; active euthanasia remains illegal
- P. Rathinam (1994) had recognised a right to die under Article 21 — overruled by Gian Kaur (1996); 2023 simplified living wills
### Nine-judge bench on the meaning of "industry"
## 1. News
- Nine-judge Constitution Bench led by CJI Surya Kant hearing what constitutes an "industry" under labour law
- Re-examines Bangalore Water Supply and Sewerage Board vs A. Rajappa (1978) — seven-judge bench, majority by Justice V.R. Krishna Iyer
## 2. 1978 tests
- Triple Test: (1) systematic activity, (2) employer–employee cooperation, (3) production/distribution of goods or services for human wants — ⚠ profit motive and capital irrelevant
- Dominant Nature Test: primary function decides status
- Brought hospitals, universities, charities and some welfare departments under labour law; two judges dissented
## 3. Later developments
- Industrial Disputes (Amendment) Act, 1982 sought to exclude hospitals, education, sovereign functions — ⚠ never notified
- 1996: Maharashtra Forest Dept held an industry; 2001: opposite for Gujarat Forest Dept
- State of UP vs Jai Bir Singh (2005) — five-judge reference; 2017 seven-judge bench referred to nine judges
- Issues framed: validity of triple test (in light of 1982 amendment and Industrial Relations Code, 2020); welfare activities; meaning of sovereign functions
- MSME (from July 1, 2020): medium — investment ≤ ₹50 crore, turnover ≤ ₹250 crore; all MSME loans are priority sector
## 4. Bench
- Hearings began March 17; members: CJI Surya Kant, B.V. Nagarathna, P.S. Narasimha, Dipankar Datta, Ujjal Bhuyan, Satish Chandra Sharma, Joymalya Bagchi, Alok Aradhe, Vipul M. Pancholi
- 1978 judgment paras 140 and 144 under review
- Constitution Bench: Article 145(3) — at least five judges
### Transgender Persons (Protection of Rights) Amendment Bill, 2026
## 1. News
- Introduced by Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment; amends the 2019 Act; may override NALSA (2014)
## 2. Key changes
- Abolishes self-identification — removes Section 4(2)
- Narrower definition: only socio-cultural identities (hijra, kinner etc.) and specific biological variations — ⚠ excludes gender-fluid, non-binary
- Medical Board (CMO-led) recommendation before District Magistrate issues certificate
- National Council state reps must be Director-level officers
- New offence — forced identity: kidnapping to force identity 10 years to life; for children life + ₹5 lakh fine; forced begging — adults 5–10 years, children 10–14 years RI
## 3. Background
- NALSA v. Union of India (2014): "third gender"; self-determination of gender identity
- 2019 Act: self-identification; DM certification; National Council for Transgender Persons; penalties 6 months–2 years; 32,424 ID cards issued
- Census 2011: ~4.88 lakh transgender persons — highest in UP, Andhra Pradesh, Maharashtra
- Legal Services Authorities give free legal aid to transgender persons with income below ₹2,00,000
## 4. More facts
- Introduced by Union Minister Virendra Kumar (MoSJE, est. 1985); identities listed — Kinner, Hijra, Aravani, Jogappa, intersex variations
- After recognition, gender can be changed in documents incl. birth certificates
### Census 2027 — symbols, digital tools and schedule

- ⚠ First fully digital Census; tools by C-DAC — HLBC (House Listing Block Creator), HL Mobile App, Self-Enumeration Portal, CMMS

# MCQ NOTES OF APP TOPIC 6:27
### Seat freeze & delimitation
## Seat freeze
- 42nd Amendment — froze seats on the 1971 Census, until 2000
- 84th Amendment (2001) — extended the freeze until the first Census after 2026
- At present — inter-State allocation on 1971 Census; constituency boundaries on 2001 Census
## Women's reservation & Lok Sabha expansion
- Proposal — bring women's reservation without waiting for post-Census delimitation, from the 2029 Lok Sabha polls
- Delimitation planned on the 2011 Census
- Seats 543 → 816, about 273 for women; States' proportions unchanged
- 131st Amendment Bill — earlier maximum 550; proposed maximum 850
- Special majority — two-thirds present and voting plus majority of total membership
| Article (Women's Reservation Act) | Relates to |
| 330A | Lok Sabha |
| 332A | State Assemblies |
| 334A | Commencement and duration |
## Other amendments & appointments
- 73rd Amendment — Part IX, Eleventh Schedule, Article 243D; District Planning Committee (Art. 243ZD) is from the 74th Amendment
| Amendment | Change |
| 21st | Sindhi |
| 71st | Konkani, Manipuri, Nepali |
| 92nd | Bodo, Santhali, Maithili, Dogri |
| 102nd | Constitutional status to NCBC |
- March 2026 Governors — R.N. Ravi → West Bengal; Jishnu Dev Varma → Maharashtra; Shiv Pratap Shukla → Telangana; Kavinder Gupta → Himachal Pradesh
- Tribunal members' tenure extended till 8 September 2026
- SC strength 34 → 38 (37 judges plus CJI)
### CEC & ECs: selection
- Anoop Baranwal (2023) — CJI on the panel only as an interim arrangement (SC, May 2026)
- 2023 Act panel — PM, LoP and a Union Cabinet Minister (nominated by PM) replacing the CJI
- Madras Bar Association v. Union of India — struck down parts of the Tribunals Reforms Act, 2021
- Article 239A — inserted by the 14th Amendment; with Govt of UTs Act 1963 gives Puducherry its legislature; Art. 239AA (69th) — Delhi
- Among UTs with legislatures — Puducherry closest to a State; J&K least autonomy
- K. Lakshminarayanan v UoI (2019) — Puducherry LG cannot act independently in routine matters
- Cabinet decision — amend the Prevention of Insults to National Honour Act, 1971 to protect Vande Mataram like the Anthem
- Essential Commodities Act, 1955 used (Iran-linked energy crisis) — Ministry of Consumer Affairs, Food and Public Distribution
### Election Commission
## Constitutional provisions
- ECI set up on 25 January 1950
- Article 324(2) — CEC and ECs appointed by the President, subject to Parliament's law
- Article 324(5) — EC removed only on the CEC's recommendation (not CAG); CEC removable like an SC judge
- CEC removal — proved misbehaviour or incapacity, special majority in both Houses; inquiry committee of three members
- Selection committee has a Union Cabinet Minister in place of the CJI
- ADR v. ECI / Bihar SIR — ECI may conduct SIR; power from Article 324 and RP Act, 1950
## Cases
- 7 May 2026 — SC called Parliament's delay "tyranny of the elected" (Justice Dipankar Datta)
- Mohinder Singh Gill (1978) — Article 324 gives ECI plenary powers where law is silent
- T.N. Seshan (1995) — multi-member EC valid; CEC not superior to other ECs in decisions
- Maneka Gandhi (1978) — Golden Triangle: Articles 14, 19 and 21; Passport Act reviewable
- Joginder Kumar v UP — arrest must not be arbitrary

- Removal motion against Speaker Om Birla; earlier failed motions against Mavalankar, Hukam Singh, Balram Jakhar
- K.C. Venugopal's privilege notice was against PM Modi; Rule 187 — Lok Sabha; Rule 222 — Rajya Sabha

- CANH — medical treatment that can be stopped in patient's best interest
- Aruna Shanbaug (2011) — passive euthanasia in exceptional cases; Gian Kaur rejected right to die; Common Cause — refusal of treatment, living wills

- Sabarimala 2018 — 4:1, exclusion unconstitutional; referred to larger Bench in 2019
- Centre in review — rigid definitions distort the plural nature of Hinduism
- Article 145(3) — at least five judges for substantial constitutional questions

- Sikhs added 1956; Buddhists 1990

### Election Commissioners appointment — "tyranny of the elected"
## 1. News
- SC on 7 May 2026 called Parliament's long delay in making an appointment law "tyranny of the elected" (remark by Justice Dipankar Datta)
- Hearing challenge (by ADR, 2024) to CEC and Other ECs (Appointment, Conditions of Service and Term of Office) Act, 2023
## 2. Selection panels
| Anoop Baranwal (March 2023, interim) | 2023 Act |
| PM | PM (Chairperson) |
| Leader of Opposition (LS) | Leader of Opposition (LS) |
| CJI | ⚠ Union Cabinet Minister nominated by PM |
- Search Committee: headed by Law Minister + 2 senior bureaucrats → shortlist of 5
- Section 8: Selection Committee may go beyond shortlist; Section 7(2): vacancy does not invalidate appointment
- Eligibility: Secretary-rank (current/former); no reappointment; EC+CEC total max 6 years

- Anoop Baranwal: voting is part of Article 19(1)(a) expression

### Inter-State redistribution of Lok Sabha seats on 2011 Census
## 1. Proposal
- Union considering Constitutional Amendment + fresh delimitation on 2011 Census; Lok Sabha to ~850 seats; enables 33% women's reservation
- Now: inter-State allocation on 1971 Census; constituency boundaries on 2001 Census
## 2. Freeze history
| Amendment | Effect |
| 42nd (1976) | Froze seats on 1971 Census — to reward population control |
| 84th (2001) | Freeze extended till first Census after 2026 |
| 87th (2003) | Constituency delimitation on 2001 Census; seat totals still frozen |
- Art 82 (Lok Sabha), Art 170 (Assemblies); Delimitation Commission orders cannot be challenged in courts
- Principle: "one person, one vote, one value"
## 3. Parliament static GK
- Art 79: Parliament = President + Lok Sabha + Rajya Sabha
- Art 80: RS max 250 (238 elected + 12 nominated); RS permanent — one-third retire every two years; seats by population (not equal)
- RS members elected only by elected MLAs (⚠ not MLCs) — STV proportional representation
- Art 170: Assembly max 500, min 60 (exceptions: Goa, Sikkim, Mizoram)
- Art 333 Anglo-Indian nomination abolished by 104th Amendment (2019)
## 4. Constitution (131st Amendment) Bill and Delimitation Bill
- Introduced: Constitution (131st Amendment) Bill — Lok Sabha max 850 (815 States + 35 UTs); Delimitation Bill on 2011 Census; one-third women's reservation
- Amends Art 81, Art 82 (removes post-2026 freeze) and Art 334A (early women's reservation)
- "Schedule" mechanism: law to list State-wise seats — Lok Sabha up ~50% while keeping each State's percentage share unchanged
- Earlier constitutional max: 550 members
- Art 75(1A): Council of Ministers ≤ 15% of Lok Sabha → up to ~122 ministers at 850
- Art 108 joint sitting — larger Lok Sabha dominance; ⚠ Rajya Sabha strength (Art 80) does not change automatically
- Gainers: UP, Bihar, Rajasthan, MP; relative losers: Kerala, Tamil Nadu
## 5. Delimitation Commission facts
- Constituted four times: 1952, 1963, 1973, 2002; last exercise completed 2008 (2001 Census)
- Statutory body under Delimitation Act; appointed by President; chair — retired SC judge; members — CEC + State Election Commissioners
- Orders not needing Parliament approval; ⚠ Article 329 bars courts; decides SC/ST reserved constituencies
- Art 331 (2 Anglo-Indian LS nominees) — discontinued by 104th Amendment (2019)
## 6. 131st Amendment Bill defeated
- ⚠ Constitution (131st Amendment) Bill, 2026 was defeated in April 2026 — revived debate on delinking women's reservation from delimitation
- Bill sought max Lok Sabha 550 → 850 (Art 81); changes in Arts 82, 170, 334A for delimitation on 2011 Census; needed special majority + ratification by half of States
- Package of three Bills: 131st Amendment Bill, Delimitation Bill, 2026 (new Commission replacing Delimitation Act, 2002 framework; would fix SC, ST and women's seats), Union Territories Laws (Amendment) Bill, 2026 (Delhi, J&K, Puducherry)
- Delimitation Bill 2026 withdrawn after the defeat; the Bill sought women's quota by 2029
- Women's representation: Lok Sabha ~14–15%; State Assemblies ~9%; Rwanda >60%, Sweden ~46%
- History: first Women's Reservation Bill 1996; passed Rajya Sabha in 2010 but lapsed; enacted 2023; ⚠ no OBC sub-quota
- Next Census ~2027 → implementation likely ~2033–34
- Delimitation Commission — temporary body, dissolves after work; orders laid before Parliament for information only; Art 329(a)
- Art 243T: SC/ST seats in every municipality in proportion to population; Art 169: Council abolition by ordinary law after Assembly special-majority resolution
## 7. Lok Sabha vote on 131st Amendment Bill
- 528 voted — 298 in favour, 230 against; ⚠ failed to get required special majority of 326 (Art 368)
- 106th Amendment Act brought into force from April 16, 2026 (procedural step); Act passed September 2023; valid 15 years
- Census every 10 years under Census Act, 1948; Women's Reservation Bill first introduced 1996
- Lok Sabha: est. 1952; max 552 (as per source); current 543; Art 83 — 5-year tenure
- ⚠ CA360 phrasing: 42nd Amendment (1976) froze seats until 2000; 84th extended freeze till 2026
### Lok Sabha expansion to ~816 seats for women's reservation
## 1. Proposal
- Aim: implement women's reservation without waiting for delimitation after next Census
- Strength: 543 → ~816 members; ~273 seats (one-third) reserved for women
- From: 2029 Lok Sabha elections; state-wise share of seats kept same in initial phase (1971 Census basis)
| Parameter | Current | Proposed |
| Total seats | 543 | ~816 |
| Women representation | ~14% | ~33% |
| Basis of allocation | 1971 Census | Same (initial phase) |
| Delimitation | Mandatory for change | Temporarily bypassed |
## 2. Constitutional angle
- Article 81: composition of Lok Sabha — needs amendment to raise seats
- Article 82: readjustment / delimitation after each Census
- 106th Constitutional Amendment Act, 2023 (Women's Reservation / Nari Shakti Vandan) — 33% reservation in Lok Sabha and State Assemblies; linked to delimitation
- Amendment needs special majority: two-thirds of members present and voting + majority of total membership
- ⚠ Seat freeze based on 1971 Census continues until after 2026 (first Census after 2026)
- ⚠ Expansion does not automatically trigger delimitation
## 3. Women's Reservation Act — features and roadmap
- Articles inserted: 330A (Lok Sabha), 332A (State Assemblies), 334A (commencement & duration)
- Duration: 15 years; sub-reservation for SC/ST women; rotation of reserved seats after each delimitation
- Linked to delimitation after first Census after 2026 — implementation unlikely before 2032–33
- Delimitation: Articles 82 and 170; Delimitation Commission appointed by President; orders have force of law — ⚠ not subject to judicial review
- Seat allocation freeze till after 2026 — 84th Amendment
- Proposed mechanism: ~273 extra women's seats allotted to parties by vote share in last general election (limited proportional representation) from pre-declared lists — hybrid with FPTP; temporary for one or two cycles
### Sabarimala review — nine-judge Bench
## 1. News
- Supreme Court constituted a nine-judge Bench for Sabarimala review petitions — case Kantaru Rajeevaru v Indian Young Lawyers Association
- Centre (2026): rigid definitions of "religious denomination" and "essential religious practices" distort plural nature of Hinduism
## 2. Background
- Temple: in Periyar Tiger Reserve, Kerala; deity Lord Ayyappa (celibate); 41-day penance
- Women aged 10–50 barred; upheld by Kerala HC in 1991 (S. Mahendran case)
- Administered by Travancore Devaswom Board; Rule 3(b) of Kerala Hindu Places of Public Worship Rules, 1965
| Year | Development |
| 2017 | Five-judge Constitution Bench |
| 2018 | 4:1 verdict — exclusion unconstitutional; Rule 3(b) struck down; dissent by Justice Indu Malhotra |
| 2019 | Referred to larger Bench |
## 3. Articles involved
- 14 (equality), 15 (non-discrimination), 17 (untouchability), 25 (freedom of religion), 26 (denominational rights — subject to public order, morality, health; ⚠ not absolute)
- Article 27: no tax for promotion of a religion; Article 28(1): no religious instruction in wholly State-funded institutions
- Judicial review basis: Articles 13, 32, 226; India follows procedure established by law
- Mazdoor Kisan Shakti Sangathan v UoI (2018): Section 144 CrPC cannot suppress legitimate protest — Article 19(1)(b)
## 4. Nine-judge Bench details
- Bench chaired by CJI Surya Kant; hearing began April 7, 2026; members include B.V. Nagarathna, M.M. Sundresh, Ahsanuddin Amanullah, Aravind Kumar, Augustine George Masih, Prasanna B. Varale, R. Mahadevan, Joymalya Bagchi
- Shirur Mutt case (1954) — established essential religious practices doctrine
- Art 145(3): substantial constitutional questions need at least five judges
- 2019 reference — 7 questions framed in 2020; interventions by Muslim Personal Law Board, Jain organisations
- Sabarimala reference: judgment reserved after 16 days of hearing (May 2026)
### Removal notices against CEC Gyanesh Kumar rejected
## 1. News
- Notices to remove CEC Gyanesh Kumar — signed by 63 Rajya Sabha and 130 Lok Sabha MPs — rejected by RS Chairman and LS Speaker: no prima facie "proved misbehaviour"
- Issues cited: bias, data non-disclosure (consistent with Puttaswamy privacy), Special Intensive Revision (SIR) of rolls
## 2. CEC — key facts
- ECI: constitutional body under Article 324; established 25 January 1950; single-member till 1989
- Appointment: by President on recommendation of selection committee — PM, LoP in Lok Sabha, a Union Cabinet Minister (CEC and Other ECs Act, 2023)
- Tenure: 6 years or 65 years of age, whichever earlier
- Removal: Article 324(5) — like SC judge; proved misbehaviour or incapacity; special majority in both Houses
- ECI conducts elections to Parliament, State Legislatures, President, Vice-President
## 3. Key judgments
| Case | Held |
| Mohinder Singh Gill (1978) | Art 324 gives plenary powers where law is silent |
| T.N. Seshan v UoI (1995) | Multi-member EC valid; CEC not superior to ECs in decisions |
| Anoop Baranwal v UoI (2023) | Committee of PM, LoP, CJI till Parliament makes law |
### Jan Vishwas (Amendment of Provisions) Bill 2026 passed
## 1. News
- Passed by Parliament — decriminalises minor offences; trust-based governance, ease of doing business
## 2. Key provisions
- Imprisonment replaced by monetary/civil penalties for minor offences; obsolete provisions removed
- Graded compliance: warnings and improvement notices for initial contraventions before penalties
- Enforcement shifted from criminal courts to administrative adjudication — designated officers + appellate authorities
- Periodic revision of fines — 10% increase every three years
- ⚠ Does not abolish penalties — converts criminal to civil penalties; expands earlier (2023, 2025 version) Jan Vishwas reforms
## 3. Jan Vishwas 2.0 details
- Called Jan Vishwas 2.0; ⚠ another source: amends 79 Central Acts, decriminalises ~717 provisions (vs "~80 Acts, 1,000+ provisions")
- Jan Vishwas Act, 2023: amended 42 Central laws
- Adjudicating Officers impose penalties; structured appeal; serious offences (public safety, environment, fraud) keep criminal liability
- Linked to World Bank B-READY (Business Ready) framework; concern — regulatory capture, fines as cost of business
### Seven of ten AAP Rajya Sabha MPs merge with BJP — anti-defection law
## 1. News
- 7 of AAP's 10 Rajya Sabha MPs announced merger with BJP — claim merger protection (two-thirds) under Tenth Schedule; precedent — TDP Rajya Sabha case
## 2. Anti-defection law
- 52nd Amendment, 1985 added Tenth Schedule; 91st Amendment, 2003 removed "split" (one-third), kept merger (two-thirds)
- Grounds: voluntarily giving up membership; defying whip (not condoned within 15 days); independent joining a party; nominated member joining after 6 months
- Members not joining merged party and staying separate are also protected
- Decided by Speaker/Chairman (⚠ not President/ECI)
| Case | Held |
| Kihoto Hollohan v Zachillhu (1992) | Speaker's decision subject to judicial review |
| Ravi S. Naik (1994) | Defection inferred from conduct |
| Keisham Meghachandra Singh (2020) | Decide within 3 months; independent tribunal suggested |
| Padi Kaushik Reddy v Telangana (2025) | Reforms for timely decisions |
## 3. RS Chairman
- Vice-President is ex-officio Chairman (Arts 64, 89); only casting vote; ⚠ LS Speaker certifies Money Bills
### Puducherry elections — UTs with legislature compared
## 1. Three models
| UT | Basis | Key point |
| Puducherry | Art 239A (14th Amendment, 1962) + Govt of UTs Act, 1963 | Legislature created by law; unicameral with elected + nominated members; closest to State |
| Delhi | Art 239AA (69th Amendment, 1991) — NCT | Legislature in Constitution; no power over public order, police, land |
| J&K | J&K Reorganisation Act, 2019 | Least autonomy; police, public order with LG; Ladakh UT without legislature |
- Puducherry — ex-French territory, integrated 1962
- K. Lakshminarayanan v UoI (2019): Puducherry LG cannot act independently in routine matters
## 2. Key articles
| Article | Provision |
| 239 | UTs administered by President through Administrator |
| 239AB | President's Rule in Delhi (like Art 356) |
| 240 | President's regulations for Andaman, Lakshadweep etc. |
| 246(4) | ⚠ Parliament can legislate on any subject (even State List) for UTs |
- Delhi LG can refer disagreement with Council of Ministers to President; disputes over control of "services"
### Meghalaya — Khasi and Garo made official languages
## 1. News
- Meghalaya Cabinet approved Meghalaya Official Languages Ordinance, 2026 — Khasi and Garo official alongside English; renewed demand for Eighth Schedule
- Legislative use after amending Meghalaya State Legislature (Continuance of English Language) Act, 1980
## 2. Official language provisions (Part XVII)
| Article | Provision |
| 343 | Hindi in Devanagari = Union official language; English for 15 years (till 1965) |
| 344 | Official Language Commission and Committee |
| 345 | State may adopt one or more languages or Hindi |
| 347 | President may recognise a language spoken by a section |
| 351 | Develop Hindi |
- Official Languages Act, 1963: English continues as associate language
- Eighth Schedule: originally 14 → now 22 languages; inclusion by constitutional amendment; ⚠ not part of Part XVII; ⚠ inclusion ≠ Union official language
| Amendment | Languages added |
| 21st (1967) | Sindhi |
| 71st (1992) | Konkani, Manipuri, Nepali |
| 92nd (2003) | Bodo, Santhali, Maithili, Dogri |
- ⚠ 102nd Amendment (2018) — NCBC constitutional status (not languages); classical language status is independent of Eighth Schedule
- Art 120(1): Parliament business in Hindi or English; presiding officer may allow mother tongue
- Ordinance under Art 213; Meghalaya CM Conrad Sangma; Governor C.H. Vijayshankar; state since 1972
### Women are 49.7% of elected Panchayat representatives
## 1. News
- Centre: women = 49.7% of elected PRI representatives — 12.14 lakh of 24.41 lakh; winning many unreserved seats too
- 19 States/UTs give 50% reservation (e.g. Bihar, Rajasthan, MP, AP, HP, Odisha); Gujarat, Haryana, Tripura fell short of targets (2025 data)
- ⚠ Ministry of Panchayati Raj has no national data on reserved vs unreserved wins; "Sarpanch Pati" proxy problem
## 2. 73rd Amendment (1992) facts
- In force 24 April 1993; Part IX (Arts 243–243O); Eleventh Schedule — 29 subjects
- Three tiers; intermediate tier optional for States with population below 20 lakh
- Art 243D: not less than one-third for women (⚠ Constitution mandates 1/3, not 50%) — seats and chairpersons; horizontal
- Art 243E: 5-year term; fresh polls within 6 months of dissolution; not needed if remainder < 6 months
- Art 243K: State Election Commission; Art 243I: State Finance Commission (every 5 years)
- ⚠ District Planning Committee (Art 243ZD) — from 74th Amendment, not 73rd
### SC — right to safe travel on highways is part of Article 21
## 1. News
- Supreme Court (suo motu, after November 2025 accidents in Rajasthan and Telangana killing 34) — safe travel on highways is part of Right to Life (Art 21)
- Banned new dhabas, eateries, fuel points, commercial structures within right-of-way of National Highways; heavy vehicles only in designated bays
- National Highways ~2% of road network but ~30% of road deaths
## 2. Article 21 judgments

### Sattankulam custodial death verdict — death penalty to nine policemen
## 1. News
- Tamil Nadu trial court awarded death penalty to nine policemen — Sattankulam custodial murder case (2020); "rarest of rare"
- ~170 custodial deaths in 2025–26; high in Bihar, Rajasthan; India has not ratified UNCAT; no anti-torture law
## 2. Constitutional and legal safeguards
- Art 21 (life, dignity), Art 20(3) (no self-incrimination), Art 22 (arrest safeguards)
- NHRC: custodial deaths to be reported within 24 hours; ⚠ can only recommend, cannot punish
- CrPC Sec 41/41A: reasons for arrest/non-arrest; notice of appearance; Sec 176: magisterial inquiry into custodial death (⚠ not by police officer)

| Prakash Singh v UoI | Police Complaints Authorities |

### Delimitation on 2011 Census to fast-track women's reservation
## 1. News
- Centre considering delimitation based on 2011 Census to implement Women's Reservation Act, 2023 before the 2029 Lok Sabha polls
- Lok Sabha: 543 → 816 seats (~50% rise); ~273 seats for women; state proportions unchanged; possible lottery, every third seat
- Govt plan: Seats up ~50% while keeping southern States' share (23.8%); 129 → 195 under an 816-seat formula
- Amendments needed: Article 81, Article 170, Delimitation Commission Act — two-thirds majority
## 2. Women's Reservation Act
- 106th Constitutional Amendment Act, 2023 — Nari Shakti Vandan Adhiniyam
- 33% in Lok Sabha, State Assemblies, Delhi Assembly — incl. within SC/ST seats; ⚠ not Rajya Sabha or Legislative Councils
- 15 years validity; rotation after each delimitation
- Delimitation: Article 82 (Lok Sabha), Article 170 (States)
- Representation: ~15% Lok Sabha (2019–24), ~9% assemblies; 5% in first Lok Sabha
## 3. History
- Bills: 1996, 1998, 1999, 2008
- Committees: CSWI (1971), Margaret Alva (1987), Geeta Mukherjee (1996) — OBC women
- 73rd/74th Amendments (1992): 33% in PRIs and ULBs
- WEF Gender Gap 2023: India 25.3% parity in political empowerment; CEDAW 1979
### SC: conversion ends Scheduled Caste status
## 1. News
- Supreme Court: SC status limited to Hindus, Sikhs, Buddhists; conversion to other religions → immediate and complete loss of SC status
- Case: from Andhra Pradesh High Court — complaint under SC/ST (PoA) Act by Pastor Chintada Anand Paul; HC quashed charges
- ⚠ AP's 1977 Government Order gives only non-statutory concessions, not SC status
- Reconversion: must prove return, renunciation and acceptance by original caste — burden on claimant
## 2. Constitution (Scheduled Castes) Order, 1950
- Issued under Article 341(1); Clause 3 — only Hindus, Sikhs, Buddhists
- 1950 Hindus only → 1956 Sikhs added → 1990 Buddhists added
- Article 341(2): only Parliament can amend the list; roots in GoI Act 1935 (1936 Order)
| Aspect | SC | ST |
| Article | 341 | 342 |
| Basis | Untouchability | Tribal characteristics, isolation |
## 3. More facts
- SC upheld the AP High Court judgment of 30 April 2025; Bench Justices Prashant Kumar Mishra and Manmohan dismissed the SLP
- Related Articles: 341, 342, 15(4), 16(4)
### Right to die with dignity — Harish Rana case
- ★ Right to life does not include right to die — Chenna Jagadeeswar v. State of A.P.
### Transgender Persons (Protection of Rights) Amendment Bill, 2026
## 1. News
- Introduced by Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment; amends the 2019 Act; may override NALSA (2014)
## 2. Key changes
- Abolishes self-identification — removes Section 4(2)
- Narrower definition: only socio-cultural identities (hijra, kinner etc.) and specific biological variations — ⚠ excludes gender-fluid, non-binary
- Medical Board (CMO-led) recommendation before District Magistrate issues certificate
- National Council state reps must be Director-level officers
- New offence — forced identity: kidnapping to force identity 10 years to life; for children life + ₹5 lakh fine; forced begging — adults 5–10 years, children 10–14 years RI
## 3. Background
- NALSA v. Union of India (2014): "third gender"; self-determination of gender identity
- 2019 Act: self-identification; DM certification; National Council for Transgender Persons; penalties 6 months–2 years; 32,424 ID cards issued
- Census 2011: ~4.88 lakh transgender persons — highest in UP, Andhra Pradesh, Maharashtra
- Legal Services Authorities give free legal aid to transgender persons with income below ₹2,00,000
## 4. More facts
- Introduced by Union Minister Virendra Kumar (MoSJE, est. 1985); identities listed — Kinner, Hijra, Aravani, Jogappa, intersex variations
- After recognition, gender can be changed in documents incl. birth certificates

- ⚠ First fully digital Census; tools by C-DAC — HLBC (House Listing Block Creator), HL Mobile App, Self-Enumeration Portal, CMMS

### SHANTI Act — nuclear sector opened to private players
## 1. Act
- Name: Sustainable Harnessing and Advancement of Nuclear Energy for Transforming India (SHANTI) Act, 2025 — amends liability regime of CLNDA 2010
- ⚠ Sources differ: other notes say SHANTI replaced Atomic Energy Act 1962 and CLNDA 2010
- ⚠ SHANTI replaced two laws (1962 + 2010)
- Private entities allowed: operate plants, manufacture equipment, fuel fabrication, enrichment of U-235 up to prescribed limit
- Reserved for Centre: enrichment beyond limits, spent fuel management
- AERB (est. 1983) gets statutory status — accountable to Parliament
## 2. Liability caps
- Excess covered by a Nuclear Liability Fund; ⚠ supplier liability eliminated (CLNDA allowed recourse against suppliers)
- Operators exempt for "grave natural disasters"; overall ceiling about ₹3,900 crore
- Liability cap: 300 mn SDR; no supplier liability; limited private role; draft Rules 2026
- Bodies: Atomic Energy Redressal Advisory Council; Nuclear Damage Claims Commission; appeals to Appellate Tribunal for Electricity (Electricity Act 2003)
## 3. Background
- Atomic Energy Act 1962 replaced 1948 Act; amended 1986, 1987, 2015; CLNDA 2010 — no-fault liability
- Nuclear share ~3% of electricity; targets missed — 10 GW by 2000 (got 2.86 GW), 20 GW by 2020 (got 6.78 GW); new target 100 GW by 2047
- ⚠ Reactor principle: controlled nuclear fission
| Plant | State |
| Tarapur | Maharashtra |
| Rajasthan (Rawatbhata) | Rajasthan |
| Kakrapar | Gujarat |
| Kudankulam (PWR) | Tamil Nadu |
### No-confidence (removal) motion against Speaker Om Birla
## 1. News
- Opposition moved notice against Lok Sabha Speaker Om Birla on 10 February 2026
- Reasons: LoP Rahul Gandhi not allowed to quote former Army Chief Gen M.M. Naravane's unpublished memoir; Speaker's remark that PM could have been attacked in the House
## 2. Removal procedure
- Article 94(c): removal by resolution passed by majority of all the then members (effective majority) — ⚠ not "present and voting"
- Notice: at least 14 days, in writing to Secretary-General
- Leave: at least 50 members must stand in support; discussion within 10 days of leave
- Rules 200–203 of Lok Sabha Rules; Rule 200A — charges specific, no sarcasm or imputations; mover speech capped 15 minutes
- Article 96: Speaker cannot preside but can speak and vote in first instance; ⚠ no casting vote
- On dissolution, Speaker continues until just before first meeting of next Lok Sabha
- ⚠ Removal provision does not apply to Rajya Sabha Chairman this way
## 3. Past motions — none succeeded
| Year | Speaker |
| 1954 | G.V. Mavalankar (first Speaker) |
| 1966 | Hukam Singh |
| 1987 | Balram Jakhar |
## 4. Articles and cases
| Article (LS/State) | Subject |
| 93 / 178 | election of Speaker, Deputy Speaker |
| 94 / 179 | vacation, resignation, removal |
| 95 / 180 | Deputy Speaker acts as Speaker |
| 96 / 181 | not to preside while removal resolution pending |
| 97 / 186 | salaries and allowances |
- Kihoto Hollohan (1993): Speaker's Tenth Schedule decisions subject to judicial review
- Nabam Rebia (2016): Speaker cannot decide disqualification while removal notice pending
- Keisham Meghachandra Singh (2020): disqualification pleas within three months
- Subhash Desai (2023): Speaker to set timelines
## 5. CA360 details
- Notice submitted by Congress Chief Whip K. Suresh on behalf of Congress, SP, DMK
- Grievances: not allowed to speak on President's address; suspension of 8 MPs

### Private member Bill on judicial diversity and SC regional benches
## 1. Bill
- Moved by: P. Wilson, DMK Rajya Sabha MP — constitutional amendment
- Data: SC/ST/OBC ~20% of higher-judiciary appointees; women <15%; religious minorities <5%
- Proposals: representation proportional to population for SC, ST, OBC, minorities, women; Centre to notify collegium recommendations within 90 days
- Regional benches: New Delhi, Kolkata, Mumbai, Chennai — constitutional matters stay with Delhi; SC pendency 90,000+ (Jan 2026)
## 2. Background
- Article 124 (SC judges), Article 217 (HC judges — CJI, HC CJ, Governor), Article 130 (seat of SC in Delhi or elsewhere by CJI with President's approval)
| Case | Outcome |
| First Judges (1981) | executive primacy |
| Second Judges (1993) | created collegium |
| Third Judges (1998) | collegium clarified — SC: CJI + 4 senior-most |
| NJAC (2015) | 99th Amendment, 2014 struck down — violated basic structure |
- NJAC composition: CJI, two senior judges, Law Minister, two eminent persons
- Collegium: Not in the Constitution (Three Judges Cases, 1998)
- ⚠ Power to increase SC judges lies with Parliament; strength 8 (1950) → 10 (1956) → 13 (1960) → 17 (1977) → 25 (1986) → 30 (2008) → 33 (2019) excluding CJI
### Sabarimala review — religious freedom and anti-exclusion test
## 1. News
- SC to hear final arguments on review petitions in Indian Young Lawyers Association vs State of Kerala (2018)
## 2. Case
- Women aged 10–50 barred — Lord Ayyappa as Naishtika Brahmachari; Rule 3(b) of Kerala Hindu Places of Public Worship (Authorisation of Entry) Rules, 1965
- September 2018: five-judge bench, 4:1 — devotees ⚠ not a separate religious denomination; Rule 3(b) unconstitutional
- Dissent: Justice Indu Malhotra — respect essential religious practice
## 3. Doctrines
| Test | Focus |
| Essential Religious Practices | religious essentiality — origin Sastri Yagnapurushadji vs Muldas Bhudardas Vaishya |
| Anti-Exclusion Test | dignity and equality — proposed by Justice D.Y. Chandrachud |
- Article 25: individual freedom of religion; Article 26: rights of religious denominations — subject to public order, morality, health
### SC: menstrual health a fundamental right
## 1. Judgment
- Dr Jay Thakur vs Union of India — menstrual health part of Article 21 (dignity, privacy, bodily autonomy)
- Linked to Articles 14 and 21A; free biodegradable sanitary pads for girls in Classes 6–12; separate toilets incl. for differently-abled; vending machines and incinerators
- Provision is a constitutional responsibility, not charity
### Proposal for an Indian Scientific Service (ISS)
## 1. Proposal
- Indian Scientific Service: a permanent All-India scientific cadre alongside civil services — ⚠ to complement, not replace IAS
- Scientists now governed by Central Civil Services (Conduct) Rules, 1964
- Features: national recruitment with peer evaluation; scientists embedded in ministries/regulators; separate service rules
- Illustrative cadres: Environmental & Ecological; Climate & Atmospheric; Water & Hydrological; Marine & Ocean; Public Health & Biomedical; Disaster Risk & Resilience; Energy & Resources; S&T Policy; Agricultural & Food Systems; Regulatory Science
- Models: US, UK, France, Germany, Japan

### Delimitation after Census 2027
## 1. News
- Freeze on inter-State Lok Sabha seat distribution ends after Census 2027 — first redistribution since 1976
## 2. Background
- 1976 (42nd Amendment): seats frozen at 1971 population
- 84th Amendment (2001): freeze extended till first Census after 2026; allowed internal boundary readjustment (2001 Census)
- 2002–08 Delimitation Commission: only redrew internal boundaries
- Representation still reflects 548 million people vs today's 1.47 billion
- LS seats: 543 frozen on 1971 Census (54.8 crore); next readjustment after Census 2027
- Articles: Arts. 82, 170, 330, 332; orders not challengeable in court
- ⚠ Four Delimitation Commissions constituted (till Dec 2023)
## 3. Projections (Lok Sabha ~888 seats)
| State | Now → projected | Share |
| Uttar Pradesh | 80 → ~151 | — |
| Bihar | 40 → ~82 | UP+Bihar over 26% |
| Tamil Nadu | 39 → ~53 | 7.2% → 6.0% |
| Kerala | 20 → ~23 | 3.7% → 2.6% |
## 4. Options
- Extend freeze (Article 14 risk); expand Lok Sabha to 750–900; weighted formula (population 70–80% + development 20–30%); strengthen Rajya Sabha (15/10/5 seats model); bifurcate UP; phased — half in 2034, rest 2039
### Census 2027 — first phase notified
## 1. Facts
- ⚠ 16th Census overall, 8th after Independence; under Census Act 1948 and Census Rules 1990; budget ₹11,718 crore
- Phase 1 — HLHC: April–September 2026; Phase 2 — Population Enumeration: February 2027 (separate schedule for Ladakh and snow-bound areas)
- Office of Registrar General and Census Commissioner (MHA)
- ⚠ First fully digital Census — Android/iOS apps; self-enumeration up to 15 days before field work
- CMMS (Census Management and Monitoring System); GIS House Listing Block Creator; Census-as-a-Service (CaaS)
- ⚠ Caste data collected electronically — first caste enumeration since 1931
### Governor's address to State Legislature — controversy
## 1. News
- Tamil Nadu: portions skipped 2022, 2023; since 2024 no Article 176 address
## 2. Provisions
| Article | Content |
| 176 | mandatory address — first session after general election and first session of every year; Motion of Thanks |
| 175 | discretionary address/messages to House(s) |
| 163 | aid and advice of Council of Ministers |
| 159 | Governor's oath |
- Origin: Section 63, GoI Act 1935; convention from April 1937 (provincial autonomy)
## 3. Judgments and commissions
| Case/Body | Point |
| Shamsher Singh (1974) | 7-judge bench — Governor is constitutional head, acts on aid and advice |
| Nabam Rebia (2016) | 5-judge bench — Arts 175/176 exercised on ministerial advice |
| State of TN v. Governor of TN (2024) | discretion cannot stall elected government |
| Sarkaria Commission (1988) | Governor — "lynchpin of cooperative federalism" |
| Punchhi Commission (2007) | limit extra roles like Chancellor |
## 4. Background
- Governor: minimum age 35; cannot be MP/MLA; office of profit disqualifies; dual role — head of State and Union representative
- Discretionary: report for President's Rule (Art 356), reserving Bills (Art 200)
- Article 174: summoning, proroguing, dissolving — only on cabinet advice; Nabam Rebia (2016) — no independent discretion under Art 174
- Rajasthan HC (1966) and Syed Habibullah cases — procedural defects in delivery do not invalidate proceedings
### IICDEM–2026 and ECINET launch
## 1. News
- India International Conference on Democracy and Election Management (IICDEM)–2026 by ECI, New Delhi, 21–23 January 2026
- ~100 delegates from ~70 countries — largest global election conference hosted by India; 36 thematic groups
- IIC-DEM 2026: First India International Conference on Democracy and Election Management — Bharat Mandapam; by IIIDEM; ~100 delegates from 70+ countries; CEC Gyanesh Kumar
- Showcased Special Intensive Revision (SIR) of electoral rolls
## 2. ECINET
- Single digital platform launched at Bharat Mandapam; conceived by CEC Gyanesh Kumar and ECs Sukhbir Singh Sandhu, Vivek Joshi (announced May 2025)
- Integrates 40+ ECI apps — Voter Helpline, cVIGIL, Suvidha 2.0, ESMS, Saksham, KYC; for ~one billion voters
- ECINET: App - one-stop ECI platform (draft rolls, services) (1 Aug 2026)
- cVIGIL: Citizens report code violations; Saksham: For PwD voters; Suvidha: Candidates' permissions
- ⚠ Saksham = PwD voters; Suvidha = candidates; cVIGIL = citizens
- Complies with RPA 1950 and 1951
### Ministry of Home Affairs — Year End Review 2025
## 1. Left-Wing Extremism
- Target: eliminate Naxalism by 31 March 2026 — Naxalmukt Bharat Abhiyan
- Karreguttalu Hills (Chhattisgarh–Telangana border): biggest-ever anti-Naxal operation — 31 Naxalites in 21 days, no SF casualties (May 2025)

### Passive euthanasia — Harish Rana case
## 1. News

## 2. Law
- Active euthanasia: illegal; passive: legal under guidelines
- BNS 2023: Sec 100 culpable homicide, 101 murder, 108 abetment of suicide
- Article 21 includes right to die with dignity

### SHANTI Bill, 2025 — nuclear energy reform
## 1. News

### National Judicial Policy and NJAC revival plea
## 1. News

- Created by: 99th Constitutional Amendment Act, 2014
- Members (6): CJI (Chairperson), two senior-most SC judges, Union Law Minister, two eminent persons (chosen by PM + CJI + LoP in Lok Sabha)
- Struck down: 2015 — Fourth Judges Case (Supreme Court Advocates-on-Record Association vs Union of India) — violated judicial independence (basic structure)
- ⚠ Any two members could veto a name
- Judicial independence as basic structure: Kesavananda Bharati (1973)

## 2. Parliamentary privileges

- Extend to: Attorney General — ⚠ not the President
- Article 105(3): until defined by law, privileges as of British House of Commons on 26 January 1950 — no law enacted yet
- Freedom from arrest (civil cases): during session and 40 days before/after — Section 135A, CPC 1908
- 44th Amendment, 1978: fair press reporting of proceedings (except secret sittings)
- Committee of Privileges: 15 members (Lok Sabha), 10 (Rajya Sabha) — semi-judicial
- 2008 Lok Sabha Privileges Committee opposed codification

### Right to Disconnect Bill, 2025
## 1. News
- Private Member's Bill on Right to Disconnect introduced in Parliament — right not to respond to work calls/emails beyond working hours
- ⚠ India's first formal legislative attempt on after-hours digital work
- Context: 29 labour laws consolidated into four Labour Codes; OSH Code, 2020 silent on digital labour
- Gap: no definition of digital "work"; not explicitly linked to Article 21

- France: collective bargaining on digital communication; Germany: strict hour caps

### SHANTI Act 2025
## What it replaces and opens up
- SHANTI Bill, 2025 replaces the Atomic Energy Act, 1962 and the Civil Liability for Nuclear Damage Act, 2010
- Ends the NPCIL monopoly: first-ever private and foreign participation in nuclear power generation
- Private firms may operate plants, manufacture equipment, make fuel and enrich U-235 up to the set limit
- Kept for the Centre: enrichment beyond the limit and spent fuel management
## Liability and regulation
- Liability cap: 300 million SDR
- Supplier liability eliminated (not retained); liability above the cap covered by a Nuclear Liability Fund
- AERB (Atomic Energy Regulatory Board, set up 1983) given statutory status, accountable to Parliament
- Section 39: Centre can declare nuclear information restricted; overrides the RTI Act for it
- IAEA safeguards apply to reactors using imported uranium

### SHANTI Act — nuclear sector opened to private players
## 1. Act
- Name: Sustainable Harnessing and Advancement of Nuclear Energy for Transforming India (SHANTI) Act, 2025 — amends liability regime of CLNDA 2010
- ⚠ Sources differ: other notes say SHANTI replaced Atomic Energy Act 1962 and CLNDA 2010
- ⚠ SHANTI replaced two laws (1962 + 2010)
- Private entities allowed: operate plants, manufacture equipment, fuel fabrication, enrichment of U-235 up to prescribed limit
- Reserved for Centre: enrichment beyond limits, spent fuel management
- AERB (est. 1983) gets statutory status — accountable to Parliament
## 2. Liability caps
- Excess covered by a Nuclear Liability Fund; ⚠ supplier liability eliminated (CLNDA allowed recourse against suppliers)
- Operators exempt for "grave natural disasters"; overall ceiling about ₹3,900 crore
- Liability cap: 300 mn SDR; no supplier liability; limited private role; draft Rules 2026
- Bodies: Atomic Energy Redressal Advisory Council; Nuclear Damage Claims Commission; appeals to Appellate Tribunal for Electricity (Electricity Act 2003)
## 3. Background
- Atomic Energy Act 1962 replaced 1948 Act; amended 1986, 1987, 2015; CLNDA 2010 — no-fault liability
- Nuclear share ~3% of electricity; targets missed — 10 GW by 2000 (got 2.86 GW), 20 GW by 2020 (got 6.78 GW); new target 100 GW by 2047
- ⚠ Reactor principle: controlled nuclear fission
| Plant | State |
| Tarapur | Maharashtra |
| Rajasthan (Rawatbhata) | Rajasthan |
| Kakrapar | Gujarat |
| Kudankulam (PWR) | Tamil Nadu |
### No-confidence (removal) motion against Speaker Om Birla
## 1. News
- Opposition moved notice against Lok Sabha Speaker Om Birla on 10 February 2026
- Reasons: LoP Rahul Gandhi not allowed to quote former Army Chief Gen M.M. Naravane's unpublished memoir; Speaker's remark that PM could have been attacked in the House
## 2. Removal procedure
- Article 94(c): removal by resolution passed by majority of all the then members (effective majority) — ⚠ not "present and voting"
- Notice: at least 14 days, in writing to Secretary-General
- Leave: at least 50 members must stand in support; discussion within 10 days of leave
- Rules 200–203 of Lok Sabha Rules; Rule 200A — charges specific, no sarcasm or imputations; mover speech capped 15 minutes
- Article 96: Speaker cannot preside but can speak and vote in first instance; ⚠ no casting vote
- On dissolution, Speaker continues until just before first meeting of next Lok Sabha
- ⚠ Removal provision does not apply to Rajya Sabha Chairman this way
## 3. Past motions — none succeeded
| Year | Speaker |
| 1954 | G.V. Mavalankar (first Speaker) |
| 1966 | Hukam Singh |
| 1987 | Balram Jakhar |
## 4. Articles and cases
| Article (LS/State) | Subject |
| 93 / 178 | election of Speaker, Deputy Speaker |
| 94 / 179 | vacation, resignation, removal |
| 95 / 180 | Deputy Speaker acts as Speaker |
| 96 / 181 | not to preside while removal resolution pending |
| 97 / 186 | salaries and allowances |
- Kihoto Hollohan (1993): Speaker's Tenth Schedule decisions subject to judicial review
- Nabam Rebia (2016): Speaker cannot decide disqualification while removal notice pending
- Keisham Meghachandra Singh (2020): disqualification pleas within three months
- Subhash Desai (2023): Speaker to set timelines
## 5. CA360 details
- Notice submitted by Congress Chief Whip K. Suresh on behalf of Congress, SP, DMK
- Grievances: not allowed to speak on President's address; suspension of 8 MPs
### Private member Bill on judicial diversity and SC regional benches
## 1. Bill
- Moved by: P. Wilson, DMK Rajya Sabha MP — constitutional amendment
- Data: SC/ST/OBC ~20% of higher-judiciary appointees; women <15%; religious minorities <5%
- Proposals: representation proportional to population for SC, ST, OBC, minorities, women; Centre to notify collegium recommendations within 90 days
- Regional benches: New Delhi, Kolkata, Mumbai, Chennai — constitutional matters stay with Delhi; SC pendency 90,000+ (Jan 2026)
## 2. Background
- Article 124 (SC judges), Article 217 (HC judges — CJI, HC CJ, Governor), Article 130 (seat of SC in Delhi or elsewhere by CJI with President's approval)
| Case | Outcome |
| First Judges (1981) | executive primacy |
| Second Judges (1993) | created collegium |
| Third Judges (1998) | collegium clarified — SC: CJI + 4 senior-most |
| NJAC (2015) | 99th Amendment, 2014 struck down — violated basic structure |
- NJAC composition: CJI, two senior judges, Law Minister, two eminent persons
- Collegium: Not in the Constitution (Three Judges Cases, 1998)
- ⚠ Power to increase SC judges lies with Parliament; strength 8 (1950) → 10 (1956) → 13 (1960) → 17 (1977) → 25 (1986) → 30 (2008) → 33 (2019) excluding CJI
### SC: menstrual health a fundamental right
## 1. Judgment
- Dr Jay Thakur vs Union of India — menstrual health part of Article 21 (dignity, privacy, bodily autonomy)
- Linked to Articles 14 and 21A; free biodegradable sanitary pads for girls in Classes 6–12; separate toilets incl. for differently-abled; vending machines and incinerators
- Provision is a constitutional responsibility, not charity
### Proposal for an Indian Scientific Service (ISS)
- ISS — a permanent All-India scientific cadre, complementing (not replacing) the IAS
- National recruitment with peer evaluation (not only state-level exams); scientists placed in ministries and regulators; separate service rules
- Example cadre: Environmental & Ecological
### Privileges Committee not constituted for two years
## 1. News
- Lok Sabha Privileges and Ethics Committees not constituted for nearly two years
- Privileges Committee — semi-judicial; privileges under Article 105 (Parliament) and 194 (states)
- Lok Sabha max 552 members; Rajya Sabha max 250; RS Chairman — VP C.P. Radhakrishnan
### Bonded Labour System (Abolition) Act, 1976 — 50 years
- (No confirmed facts: this subsection's only MCQ, a PYQ on a politician completing 50 years as legislator in March 2017, has no official key.)

- Bihar order banned the sale (not consumption) of meat, fish and eggs near schools, educational institutions and temples
### Delimitation after Census 2027
- ★ Four Delimitation Commissions constituted by the Government of India up to December 2023
- Delimitation — fixing boundaries of constituencies so seats match population
### Census 2027 — first phase notified
## 1. Facts
- ⚠ 16th Census overall, 8th after Independence; under Census Act 1948 and Census Rules 1990; budget ₹11,718 crore
- Phase 1 — HLHC: April–September 2026; Phase 2 — Population Enumeration: February 2027 (separate schedule for Ladakh and snow-bound areas)
- Office of Registrar General and Census Commissioner (MHA)
- ⚠ First fully digital Census — Android/iOS apps; self-enumeration up to 15 days before field work
- CMMS (Census Management and Monitoring System); GIS House Listing Block Creator; Census-as-a-Service (CaaS)
- ⚠ Caste data collected electronically — first caste enumeration since 1931
### Governor's address to State Legislature — controversy
## 1. News
- Tamil Nadu: portions skipped 2022, 2023; since 2024 no Article 176 address
## 2. Provisions
| Article | Content |
| 176 | mandatory address — first session after general election and first session of every year; Motion of Thanks |
| 175 | discretionary address/messages to House(s) |
| 163 | aid and advice of Council of Ministers |
| 159 | Governor's oath |
- Origin: Section 63, GoI Act 1935; convention from April 1937 (provincial autonomy)
## 3. Judgments and commissions
| Case/Body | Point |
| Shamsher Singh (1974) | 7-judge bench — Governor is constitutional head, acts on aid and advice |
| Nabam Rebia (2016) | 5-judge bench — Arts 175/176 exercised on ministerial advice |
| State of TN v. Governor of TN (2024) | discretion cannot stall elected government |
| Sarkaria Commission (1988) | Governor — "lynchpin of cooperative federalism" |
| Punchhi Commission (2007) | limit extra roles like Chancellor |
## 4. Background
- Governor: minimum age 35; cannot be MP/MLA; office of profit disqualifies; dual role — head of State and Union representative
- Discretionary: report for President's Rule (Art 356), reserving Bills (Art 200)
- Article 174: summoning, proroguing, dissolving — only on cabinet advice; Nabam Rebia (2016) — no independent discretion under Art 174
- Rajasthan HC (1966) and Syed Habibullah cases — procedural defects in delivery do not invalidate proceedings
### IICDEM–2026 and ECINET launch
## 1. News
- India International Conference on Democracy and Election Management (IICDEM)–2026 by ECI, New Delhi, 21–23 January 2026
- ~100 delegates from ~70 countries — largest global election conference hosted by India; 36 thematic groups
- IIC-DEM 2026: First India International Conference on Democracy and Election Management — Bharat Mandapam; by IIIDEM; ~100 delegates from 70+ countries; CEC Gyanesh Kumar
- Showcased Special Intensive Revision (SIR) of electoral rolls
## 2. ECINET
- Single digital platform launched at Bharat Mandapam; conceived by CEC Gyanesh Kumar and ECs Sukhbir Singh Sandhu, Vivek Joshi (announced May 2025)
- Integrates 40+ ECI apps — Voter Helpline, cVIGIL, Suvidha 2.0, ESMS, Saksham, KYC; for ~one billion voters
- ECINET: App - one-stop ECI platform (draft rolls, services) (1 Aug 2026)
- cVIGIL: Citizens report code violations; Saksham: For PwD voters; Suvidha: Candidates' permissions
- ⚠ Saksham = PwD voters; Suvidha = candidates; cVIGIL = citizens
- Complies with RPA 1950 and 1951
### Ministry of Home Affairs — Year End Review 2025
## 1. Left-Wing Extremism
- Target: eliminate Naxalism by 31 March 2026 — Naxalmukt Bharat Abhiyan
- Karreguttalu Hills (Chhattisgarh–Telangana border): biggest-ever anti-Naxal operation — 31 Naxalites in 21 days, no SF casualties (May 2025)
- Narayanpur: 27 Maoists incl. CPI-Maoist General Secretary Nambala Keshav Rao alias Basavaraju — first such high-level elimination in three decades (May 2025)
- 2025: 312 LWE cadres eliminated (highest ever); most-affected districts down to 3; affected districts 11
- Two Central Committee Members eliminated in Abujhmad; Bokaro region freed
- Bastar: Bastar Pandum, Bastar Dussehra, Bastar Olympics
- ⚠ Most-affected districts decreased (not increased)
## 2. Security and counter-terror
- New Multi-Agency Centre (MAC) inaugurated in New Delhi
- Operation Sindoor and Operation Mahadev — Pahalgam attack terrorists eliminated
- NIA conviction rate: ~95%
- J&K: 12 Hurriyat-linked organisations severed ties with separatism; Watan Ko Jano youth initiative
## 3. Cyber, laws, borders
- I4C launched e-Zero FIR — automatic FIR for cyber financial crimes
- New criminal laws: completed one year
- Narcotics: 29 drug traffickers convicted in 2025
- Immigration and Foreigners Bill, 2025; new OCI Portal (5 million+ cardholders); Fast Track Immigration – Trusted Traveller Programme
- Vibrant Villages Programme (VVP and VVP-2) — border villages
## 4. Disaster, NE, Census
- Funds: over ₹15,000 crore under SDRF, ₹2,267 crore under NDRF; Urban Flood Risk Management Programme
- North-East: violent incidents down 70%, civilian casualties down 85%; Assam Rifles land transfer in Mizoram
- Census 2027: in two phases with caste enumeration
- Bharatiya Bhasha Anubhag — promotion of Indian languages; Zonal Councils strengthened

### SHANTI Bill, 2025 — nuclear energy reform
## 1. News
- Bill: Sustainable Harnessing and Advancement of Nuclear Energy for Transforming India (SHANTI) Bill, 2025 — introduced in Lok Sabha
- Replaces: Atomic Energy Act, 1962 and Civil Liability for Nuclear Damage (CLND) Act, 2010
- ⚠ First-ever private and foreign participation in nuclear power generation — ends NPCIL monopoly
## 2. Key provisions
- Private/foreign firms: can build, own, operate, decommission plants
- Retained by government: fuel production, heavy water, radioactive waste, enrichment/reprocessing, transport of sensitive materials
- AERB: gets statutory status, accountable to Parliament
- Liability: supplier liability removed; operator liability capped and linked to reactor capacity
- Atomic Disputes Tribunal; Claims Commissioners
- Section 39: Centre can declare nuclear information "restricted" — overrides RTI Act
## 3. Targets and numbers
| Item | Figure |
| Current capacity | 8.18 GW |
| Target 2032 | 22 GW |
| Target 2047 | 100 GW (needs ₹15 lakh crore) |
| Nuclear Energy Mission (Budget 2025–26) | ₹20,000 crore |
## 4. Background
- Atomic Energy Act, 1962 replaced the 1948 law; amended 1986, 1987, 2015 (JVs of govt companies)
- CLND Act 2010: no-fault liability
- IAEA safeguards: apply to reactors using imported uranium
### National Judicial Policy and NJAC revival plea
- CJI Surya Kant asked for a National Judicial Policy to reduce divergence across courts
### Political funding — electoral trusts after electoral bonds verdict
## 1. News
- February 2024: Supreme Court struck down the Electoral Bonds Scheme (2018) — violated Article 19(1)(a) (right to information); called quid pro quo "institutionalised corruption"
- FY 2024–25: electoral trusts became the main lawful channel for corporate funding
## 2. Electoral trusts
- Introduced: Electoral Trusts Scheme, 2013 by CBDT
- Disclosure: annual report to ECI — donor names, amounts, recipient parties; ⚠ not anonymous to ECI
- Not disclosed: which company funded which party
- Prudent Electoral Trust: ₹33,330.54 crore (86.38% among top 10); FY 2024–25 received ₹2,668.49 crore, gave ₹2,180.71 crore to BJP and ₹216.34 crore to Congress
- BJP share: 84.65% of declared direct corporate donations (FY14–FY24); 71.67% of electoral trust funds
## 3. Electoral Bond Scheme facts
| Item | Detail |
| Denominations | ₹1,000, ₹10,000, ₹1 lakh, ₹10 lakh, ₹1 crore |
| Validity | 15 days |
| Eligible parties | At least 1% votes in last election |
- Corporate donations banned: 1969–1985
- ⚠ No legal cap on party expenditure — only candidates capped
### Breach of privilege notice against eight Congress MPs
## 1. News
- Lok Sabha Speaker Om Birla examining a BJP MP's breach of privilege notice against eight Congress members for disruption during the VB-G RAM G Bill, 2025 debate
## 2. Parliamentary privileges
- Articles: 105 and 122 (Parliament); 194 and 212 (State Legislatures)
- Extend to: Attorney General — ⚠ not the President
- Article 105(3): until defined by law, privileges as of British House of Commons on 26 January 1950 — no law enacted yet
- Freedom from arrest (civil cases): during session and 40 days before/after — Section 135A, CPC 1908
- 44th Amendment, 1978: fair press reporting of proceedings (except secret sittings)
- Committee of Privileges: 15 members (Lok Sabha), 10 (Rajya Sabha) — semi-judicial
- 2008 Lok Sabha Privileges Committee opposed codification
## 3. Key judgments
| Case | Year | Held |
| Pandit M.S.M. Sharma vs Sri Krishna Sinha (Searchlight) | 1958 | Privileges under Art 194(3) prevail over press freedom |
| P.V. Narasimha Rao (JMM Bribery) | 1998 | Immunity for bribes linked to speech/vote |
| State of Kerala vs K. Ajith | 2021 | Privileges do not override criminal law |
| Sita Soren vs Union of India | 2024 | ⚠ Overruled JMM case — bribery not protected |

### Four Labour Codes come into force
## 1. News
- Effective: 21 November 2025 — 4 Labour Codes replace 29 Central labour laws
- Labour: in Concurrent List (Entries 22–24, 35, List III)
| Code | Laws merged |
| Code on Wages, 2019 | 4 |
| Code on Social Security, 2020 | 9 (EPF, ESI, gratuity, maternity benefit) |
| Industrial Relations Code, 2020 | 3 (Trade Unions, Industrial Disputes, Industrial Employment) |
| OSH Code, 2020 | 13 (factories, mines, contract labour, construction) |
## 2. Code on Wages
- Applies to all employees; National Floor Wage — States cannot fix minimum wage below it
- Wage = Basic + DA + Retaining Allowance; deductions max 50%
- Hours: 8–12/day, max 48/week; overtime at least 2x
- Payment: monthly within 7 days; on resignation/termination within 2 days; mandatory wage slips
## 3. Code on Social Security
- ⚠ First time legal definition of gig and platform workers; Social Security Fund
- Aggregators: contribute 1–2% of annual turnover, capped at 5% of amount payable to gig workers
- Fixed-term employees: ⚠ gratuity after 1 year (earlier 5 years for permanent)
- EPFO: establishments with 20+ employees; ESIC: pan-India, mandatory even with 1 worker in hazardous work (earlier 10)
- Inspector-cum-facilitators replace inspectors
## 4. Industrial Relations Code
- ⚠ Layoff/retrenchment/closure permission threshold: 100 → 300 workers
- IRC absorbed ID Act 1947, Trade Unions Act 1926
- Strike: notice within 60 days before; no strike within 14 days of notice; mass casual leave by >50% workers = strike
- Negotiating union: 51% membership; council — unions with ≥20%
## 5. OSH Code
- Factory licence: with power 10 → 20 workers; without power 20 → 40
- Contract labour: applies when contractor has 50+ (earlier 20)
- Women: may work before 6 am / after 7 pm with consent and safety
- Mandatory appointment letters; annual free health check
- Safety Committees: ≥500 factory, ≥250 construction, ≥100 mine workers
### Digital Personal Data Protection (DPDP) Rules, 2025
## 1. News
- Notified: 14 November 2025 — operationalise the DPDP Act, 2023 (passed August 2023)
- Draft Rules: released January 2025
- Immediate effect: formation of Data Protection Board of India (DPBI) + the RTI amendment
- Phased: most provisions apply after 12–18 months (consent system, erasure, DPOs, audits, breach reporting — 2026–27)
## 2. Key features
- Citizen rights: erasure, correction, grievance redressal, appoint digital nominee (after death/incapacity)
- Consent: informed consent; withdrawal as easy as giving; Consent Managers
- Children: targeted ads restricted; limited exemption for parents to track child's location
- Significant Data Fiduciaries (SDFs): graded obligations
- DPBI: "digital by design" — works as a digital office; online complaints; can drop proceedings on a voluntary undertaking
- ⚠ DPBI is a subordinate office of MeitY (criticised for lack of independence)
## 3. RTI amendment
- ⚠ Section 8(1)(j) of RTI Act, 2005 — the public interest override for personal information removed
## 4. Background
| Term | Meaning |
| Data Principal | Individual whose data is processed |
| Data Fiduciary | Entity deciding purpose and means of processing |
- Puttaswamy (2017): privacy a fundamental right under Article 21
- Srikrishna Committee draft — 2018
### Chandigarh under Article 240 — Constitution (131st Amendment) Bill, 2025
## 1. News
- Proposal: Constitution (131st Amendment) Bill, 2025 to add Chandigarh to Article 240
- MHA clarified: no final decision; ⚠ no Bill in Winter Session 2025
- Effect if passed: independent Administrator/L-G; President could make regulations for Chandigarh
## 2. Article 240
- President makes regulations for "peace, progress and good government" of UTs: A&N Islands, Lakshadweep, DNH & DD, Puducherry (certain conditions)
- Regulation has force of an Act of Parliament; can amend/repeal Parliament's laws for that UT
## 3. Chandigarh status
| Fact | Detail |
| UT + joint capital | Of Punjab and Haryana — Punjab Reorganisation Act, 1966 (passed 18 Sep 1966) |
| Haryana | 17th state |
| Administrator | ⚠ Governor of Punjab (since 1984) — not Haryana Governor |
| Property split | ⚠ 60:40 (Punjab:Haryana) |
| Capital law | Capital of Punjab (Development and Regulation) Act, 1952 |
| Periphery | Punjab New Capital (Periphery) Control Act, 1952 — 16 km radius |
| Became Punjab capital | 1953, inaugurated by President Rajendra Prasad |
| Land | 22 villages of Kharar |
| 1970 (29 January) | Centre said Chandigarh should go to Punjab as a whole |
### Cannabis cultivation vs bhang — Kerala HC on NDPS Act
## 1. News
- Kerala High Court (4 Nov 2025): dismissed plea of a man caught with five cannabis plants on terrace
- Ruling: cultivation of any cannabis plant is illegal even with no flowering/fruiting tops; includes keeping in pots
## 2. NDPS Act, 1985 — definitions
| Term | Meaning |
| Charas | Resin of cannabis (incl. hashish oil) |
| Ganja | Flowering or fruiting tops (contain THC) |
| Excluded | Leaves and seeds without flowering tops |
| Cannabis plant — Section 2(iv) | Any plant of genus cannabis, irrespective of age/flowering |
- ⚠ Bhang is made from leaves — not a narcotic under NDPS; States regulate it (UP, Rajasthan licensed shops; Assam bans it)
- Section 8(b): prohibits cultivation of any cannabis plant without approval
- Section 14: Govt may allow cultivation for industrial (hemp), horticultural, medical/scientific use
## 3. Punishments
| Item | Small quantity | Commercial quantity |
| Ganja | up to 1 kg — jail up to 1 year, fine up to ₹10,000 | 20 kg+ — 10–20 years, fine ₹1–2 lakh |
| Charas | up to 100 g | 1 kg+ |
- Cultivation: up to 10 years RI and ₹1 lakh fine
## 4. Background
- Industrial hemp: ⚠ Uttarakhand first to issue licences (2018); also UP, HP, MP
- India's first medical cannabis clinic: Bengaluru (2020)
- UN Commission on Narcotic Drugs: founded 1946, HQ Vienna
- Recreational use legal: Canada, Uruguay, 15 US states; 50+ countries allow medicinal cannabis
- Delhi HC hearing challenge by Great Legalisation Movement India Trust
- NDPS Act enacted under international pressure, mainly USA
### UPSC centenary — 100 years (1 October 2025)
## 1. News
- Date: 1 October 2025 — UPSC entered its centenary year
## 2. History
| Year | Milestone |
| 1919 | Government of India Act, 1919 first envisaged an independent recruitment body |
| 1924 | Lee Commission recommended it |
| October 1926 | Public Service Commission set up — first chairman Sir Ross Barker |
| 1935 | GoI Act, 1935 — Federal Public Service Commission |
## 3. Features
- CSE: world's largest competitive examination — 10–12 lakh applicants; 2,500+ centres
- Mains: 48 optional subjects; answers in English or any of the 22 constitutionally recognised languages
- Reforms: online portals, face-recognition technology, PRATIBHA Setu (helps interview-stage candidates not finally selected to find jobs)
- ⚠ UPSC recruits for civil, technical, medical, forest, engineering and statistical services — not civil services only
### Crisis of journalism
## 1. Key points
- Journalism: called the "fourth pillar of democracy"
- BBC income down £1 billion (inflation-adjusted) since 2010; NPR (USA) funding threats; Prasar Bharati never achieved autonomy
- Open-source journalism example: The Hindu's probe into voter-list manipulation in Aland, Karnataka
- Law: Whistle Blowers Protection Act, 2014 — poorly implemented
- Non-profit model: ProPublica (USA)
### SC: serving judicial officers can be directly recruited as district judges — Rejanish KV vs K Deepa
## 1. Verdict
- Date: 9 October 2025; five-judge Constitution Bench led by CJI B.R. Gavai
- Held: judicial officers in subordinate judiciary can compete for district judge via direct recruitment (earlier only advocates with 7 years practice)
- Overruled: Satya Narain Singh (1984) and Dheeraj Mor vs High Court of Delhi (2020)
- Concurring: Justice Sundresh — absolute bar violates Article 14
## 2. Directions
- 7 years continuous experience (advocate + judicial officer combined)
- Minimum age: 35 years uniform
## 3. Background
- Articles 233–237 (Part VI) — subordinate courts
- Article 233: district judges appointed by Governor in consultation with High Court
- Other judicial officers: Governor after consulting State PSC and High Court
- CJM: offences up to 7 years; Judicial Magistrate: up to 3 years; death sentence by Sessions Judge needs HC confirmation
### Rebuilding trust in public service recruitment
## 1. Key points
- Scams: Bihar (2023) police exam leaks/proxies; Punjab PSC (2021) scandal; Uttarakhand SSC leaks — CBI inquiry
- Reforms suggested: biometric verification, encrypted paper transmission, AI analytics, fast-track courts, national regulator
### 130th Constitution Amendment Bill, 2025 — removal of detained PM/CM/Ministers
## 1. Provision
- PM, CMs or Ministers to vacate office if arrested and detained for 30 consecutive days on charges punishable with at least five years imprisonment
## 2. Concerns
- Presumption of innocence; separation of powers; collective responsibility; potential misuse
## 3. Background
- Article 75(1): President appoints PM, Ministers on PM's advice; Article 164(1): Governor appoints CM
- Articles 75(3), 164(2): collective responsibility to legislature
| Case | Point |
| Manoj Narula v Union of India (2014) | PM's advice binding unless constitutionally disqualified |
| Rai Sahib Ram Jawaya Kapur v State of Punjab (1955) | Parliamentary accountability |
| S.R. Bommai v Union of India (1994) | Floor test, legislative supremacy |
### Gender equity in higher judiciary — case for AIJS
## 1. Data (India Justice Report 2025)
- Women judges: 14% in High Courts; 3.1% in Supreme Court — 1 of 34 SC judges
- Only one woman Chief Justice among 25 High Courts
- Lower judiciary: women ~38% (open competitive exams)
- SC Centre for Research and Planning (2023): 20% of district court complexes lack separate women's toilets
## 2. AIJS
- Article 312: Parliament can create new All-India Services incl. All-India Judicial Service
- Backed by President Droupadi Murmu — Constitution Day 2023
- UPSC CSE 2024: 1,009 selected — OBC 318, SC 160, ST 87, EWS 109; women 11 of top 25 incl. Ranks 1 and 2; IPS — 54 women (28%)
### India's refugee policy — need for a law
## 1. Framework
- ⚠ India not a signatory to 1951 Refugee Convention or 1967 Protocol
- Immigration and Foreigners Act, 2025 (from April 2025) replaced Foreigners Act 1946, Registration of Foreigners Act 1939, Passport (Entry into India) Act 1920; subsumed Immigration (Carriers' Liability) Act 2000
## 2. Refugee groups

| Tibetans | ~63,000; rehabilitation policy 2014 |
| Sri Lankan Tamils | ~90,000; September 2025 exemption from penal provisions for those arrived before 9 January 2015 |
| Others (Myanmar, Afghanistan etc.) | 2.11 lakh persons of concern (June 2023) |
- CAA 2019: six minorities from Pakistan, Afghanistan, Bangladesh who entered before 31 December 2014
### 20 years of the RTI Act
## 1. News
- 12 October 2025: 20 years since RTI Act, 2005 came into full effect (12 October 2005)
- First Chief Information Commissioner: Wajahat Habibullah (2005–2010)
- SC order, 7 January 2025: criticised vacancies in CIC/SICs
- DPDP Act, 2023 amended Section 8(1)(j) — privacy made near-absolute; critic Shailesh Gandhi
## 2. Key provisions
| Section | Provision |
| 3 | Every citizen has right to information |
| 4 | Suo motu proactive disclosure |
| 6 & 7 | Procedure; reply in 30 days |
| 8 | Exemptions |
| 12–17 | Central and State Information Commissions |
| 20 | Penalties |
- Basis: Article 19(1)(a)
### Kerala hijab row — St. Rita's Public School, Kochi
## 1. News
- School: St. Rita's Public School — church-run, CBSE-affiliated, Palluruthy, Kochi (Ernakulam)
- Issue: Class 8 student wearing hijab — school cited uniform policy and a 2018 Kerala High Court ruling upholding private institutions' right to frame dress codes
## 2. Constitutional angle

- Article 14, Article 19(1)(g) (institutional autonomy), Article 21/21A (education)
- Courts apply a proportionality test
### Surrender of key Maoist leaders — decline of LWE
## 1. Key points
- Senior leader Mallojula Venugopal Rao (former Politburo member) surrendered — said Chinese/Russian models irrelevant
- LWE districts: from 90+ a decade ago to 11; core in Bijapur, Narayanpur, Sukma (Chhattisgarh)
- Salwa Judum banned by Supreme Court in 2011; strategy "Clear–Hold–Develop"
- Global parallels: Shining Path (Peru), FARC (Colombia), NPA (Philippines)
### Uttarakhand proposes amendments to UCC Rules, 2025
## 1. Background
- ⚠ Uttarakhand: first state to enact and operationalise a UCC (UCC Act, 2024) — Article 44 (DPSP)
- Petitions in Uttarakhand High Court on privacy (K.S. Puttaswamy, 2017); Senior Advocate Vrinda Grover
## 2. Key amendments
- Aadhaar authentication no longer mandatory — passport, voter ID allowed
- Disclosure of pregnancy/children in live-in relationships deleted
- Registrar can't inform parents of registrants below 21; no verification with religious leaders; 30-day provisional certificates scrapped
- Caste details no longer required
| Offence (aligned with BNS 2023) | Penalty |
| Non-registration / false declaration | Up to 3 months or ₹25,000 |
| Live-in with a minor | Up to 6 months or ₹50,000 |
| Bigamy / live-in while married | Up to 7 years |
| Forced or fraudulent marriage | Up to 7 years + fine |
### JeM's online course for women — "Tufat al-Muminat"
## 1. News

- Starts: November 2025; led by relatives of leaders incl. Masood Azhar's sisters; enrolment donations used for fundraising
