### Pan-India Special Intensive Revision (SIR) of electoral rolls; SC examines ECI powers
## 1. News
- ECI to announce pan-India SIR on 27 October 2025 — first phase 10–15 states, incl. 2026 poll states Tamil Nadu, West Bengal, Kerala, Assam, Puducherry
- CEOs conference: 22–23 October 2025, New Delhi — map current electors with last SIR
## 2. Background
- Representation of the People Act, 1950; Registration of Electors Rules, 1960; Article 324
- SVEEP: Systematic Voters' Education and Electoral Participation
## 3. SC examines ECI's powers (Article 324)
- First phase (Bihar) under Section 21(3), RPA 1950 (source dates it June 2024); second phase — 12 States/UTs, ~51 crore voters; Aadhaar allowed as identity proof
- SC order, 10 July 2025 framed the question whether ECI can conduct SIR; did not stay Bihar SIR — ordered draft roll publication and Aadhaar inclusion
- Petitioners' counsel: Prashant Bhushan, Vrinda Grover, Neha Rathi — citing Article 326, 14, 21
| Case / provision | Point |
| Lal Babu Hussain (1995) | Burden of proving citizenship lies on new registrants, not existing voters |
| Mohinder Singh Gill (1978) | Article 324 powers are plenary but within constitutional limits |
| Article 326 | Universal adult suffrage — only citizens vote |
| Section 16, RPA 1950 | Only citizens can be registered |
## 4. Background — ECI
- Article 324(2): CEC + ECs as President decides; currently three-member
- Selection committee (2023): PM (chair), Leader of Opposition in Lok Sabha, a Union Cabinet Minister nominated by PM
- Tenure: 6 years or 65 years; CEC removable only like an SC judge
- Advises President/Governors on disqualification — Articles 103 and 192
### UK interest in India's e-Courts Project Phase III
## 1. News
- UK delegation to visit New Delhi on 6 November 2025 — Law Ministry and SC e-Committee
## 2. e-Courts phases
| Phase | Detail |
| I (2010–2015) | 14,000+ courts computerised; Case Information System |
| II (2015–2023) | e-filing, e-payment, National Judicial Data Grid (NJDG), virtual courts |
| III (2023–2027) | Cabinet nod September 2023; outlay ₹7,210 crore; 3,108 crore documents to be digitised; 25 PB cloud (₹1,205.20 crore); 1,150 virtual courts (₹413.08 crore) |
- Origin: National Policy and Action Plan for ICT in Indian Judiciary, 2005 (SC e-Committee); implemented by Department of Justice with NIC; under NeGP
### SC upholds Calcutta HC order to resume MGNREGS in West Bengal
## 1. Verdict
- 27 October 2025: SC dismissed Centre's appeal against Calcutta HC order of 18 June 2025 (resume MGNREGS from 1 August 2025)
- Funds stopped 21 December 2021; work permits halted by March 2022; ~25 million workers affected
- Petitioner: Paschim Banga Khet Majoor Samity (PBKMS); NREGA Sangharsh Morcha convenor Anuradha Talwar
## 2. Background — MGNREGS
- MGNREGA 2005; scheme from 2006; 100 days guaranteed wage work per rural household
- Funding: wages 100% Centre; material & admin 75:25
- Unemployment allowance if work not given within 15 days; social audit by Gram Sabha
- VB-G RAM G Act, 2025: Replaces MGNREGA from 1 Jul 2026; 125 days; base wage ₹300/day
### Freebies vs welfare — electoral accountability
## 1. Key points
- Bihar 2025: promises over ₹8 lakh crore/year (~3× state budget); ₹10,000 to 75 lakh women under ₹7,500 crore pre-poll scheme
- 2019 corporate tax cut: 30% → 22% — cost ₹1.45 lakh crore/year
- S. Subramaniam Balaji vs State of TN (2013): manifesto promises not "corrupt practice" under Section 123, RPA
- SC (November 2023) admitted plea to treat freebies as corrupt practice — Justice B.V. Nagarathna
- Punjab: 300 free units; debt-to-GSDP 47.2% (2023–24); Rajasthan: ₹56,000 crore pre-poll schemes
- Good welfare: NTR ₹2/kg rice (AP, 1980s); Bihar girls' bicycle scheme (+30% enrolment)
- Oxfam (2022): richest 1% own 51.5% of wealth; bottom 60% own 5%
### SC Constitution Bench on seniority in Higher Judicial Services
## 1. News
- Hearings began: 28 October 2025 — five-judge bench led by CJI B.R. Gavai: seniority between promotees and direct recruits
- Promotees ~three-fourths of cadre but direct recruits dominate senior posts
| High Court data | Detail |
| Bihar | 91 Principal District Judges — 86 direct recruits, 5 promotees |
| UP | 70 — 58 direct, 12 promotees |
| Bombay | 19 elevated since 2020 — 16 direct |
| Andhra Pradesh HC | Recommended 50% of HC appointments from service be those who began as Civil Judges |
## 2. Framework
- All India Judges Association case (2002): 75% promotion (50% merit-cum-seniority + 25% LDCE) and 25% direct recruitment; 40-point roster (from R.K. Sabarwal, 1995)
- May 2025 SC: LDCE 25% restored; qualifying service 5 → 3 years; 3 years' practice for Civil Judge entry; quotas on sanctioned strength
- District judiciary handles ~90% of litigation
## 3. Background — NALSA
- Legal Services Authorities Act, 1987; Article 39A; Patron-in-Chief CJI; Executive Chairman — second senior-most SC judge
### Vice-Presidential Election 2025 — C.P. Radhakrishnan elected
## 1. News
- Winner: C.P. Radhakrishnan (NDA) defeated B. Sudershan Reddy (joint opposition)
- Turnout: over 98% of MPs voted; 13 MPs abstained (govt sources)
- Returning Officer: P.C. Mody, Rajya Sabha Secretary General; counting in new Parliament building
## 2. Background — Vice-President
- Article 66: election by electoral college of elected + nominated members of both Houses — ⚠ State legislatures do not vote
- Vice-President C.P. Radhakrishnan elected Sep 2025 [GK for date]
- Electoral college: VP = elected + nominated members of both Houses (no MLAs); President = includes elected MLAs
- Method: proportional representation by single transferable vote, secret ballot
- Eligibility: citizen, at least 35 years, no office of profit; term 5 years, re-election allowed
- Role: ex-officio Chairman of Rajya Sabha (⚠ not Lok Sabha); acts as President in vacancy
### Presidential Reference — Governors sitting on Bills (Article 200)
## 1. News
- Bench: five-judge Presidential Reference Bench led by CJI B.R. Gavai
- Issue: Governors delaying assent indefinitely — e.g. Tamil Nadu Governor sat on 10 Bills for 4 years (since 2020)
- Earlier ruling (April 2025, TN Governor case): three-month timeline for Governor and President
- Opinion (Nov 2025, 5-judge Constitution Bench): Governor has three options under Art. 200 - no indefinite inaction; no fixed or court-imposed timelines for the Governor or President (Art. 201) because the text says "as soon as possible"; "deemed assent" rejected; Art. 142 cannot replace their role; courts may issue limited mandamus against prolonged, unexplained inaction
## 2. Background — Article 200 options
- Assent; withhold assent; return for reconsideration (not Money Bill); reserve for President
- ⚠ Article 200 itself prescribes no timeline — says "as soon as possible"
## 3. Presidential reference under Article 143(1)
- Trigger: April 2025 two-judge Bench held TN Governor R.N. Ravi's delay unconstitutional, used Article 142 to set timelines; pocket veto unconstitutional
- States: reference relitigates verdict (stare decisis) — Abhishek Manu Singhvi (TN), Gopal Subramanium; Governor acts on aid and advice — Article 163
- Centre: Governor not a "postman"; AG R. Venkataraman — timelines = judicial amendment; States cannot use Article 32 against Governor
- GoI Act 1935: Governor had absolute veto
- ⚠ Advisory opinion under Article 143 is not binding
## 4. More provisions and commissions
- Reference made: May 2025 on Articles 200 and 201; April 2025 case — State of Tamil Nadu vs Governor of Tamil Nadu
- Article 201: President's assent to reserved Bills; Article 163(2): Governor's discretion final, not questioned in court
- Mandatory reservation: e.g. Bill reducing High Court powers
- Cases cited: Nabam Rebia (2006 per source), K.M. Singh (2020)
| Commission | Recommendation |
| Sarkaria (1987) | Discretion only in rare cases; President to dispose reserved Bills in 6 months |
| Punchhi (2010) | Governor to decide on Bills in 6 months |
### SC stays provisions of Waqf (Amendment) Act, 2025

- Verdict: Act's validity upheld overall; arbitrary provisions stayed

| State Waqf Boards | Max 3 non-Muslims of 11; CEO preferably Muslim |
| Mandatory registration | Upheld — required since 1923 |
| Protected monuments | Religious practice continues — Section 5(6), AMASR Act 1958 |
| Tribal areas restriction | Upheld (JPC view) |
### Electoral roll integrity — Bihar SIR and Aland (Karnataka) case
## 1. News
- Bihar: Special Intensive Revision (SIR) of rolls — deletion data disclosed only after Supreme Court intervention
- Aland (Karnataka): fraudulent Form-7 applications to delete ~6,000 voters in 2023; previous victory margin under 700 votes; ECI denied IP logs/phone records
- Form-7: objection to inclusion / deletion of a name in the roll
## 2. ECI response — Aadhaar-linked phone e-sign
- New 'e-sign' feature on ECINet portal and app — Aadhaar-linked mobile verification mandatory for online registration, deletion, correction
- Trigger: allegations by LoP Rahul Gandhi on Aland deletions (2023 Karnataka polls)
- ⚠ Aadhaar not needed to cast a vote
### Bihar SIR — SC directs ECI to accept Aadhaar as 12th document
## 1. News
- Order: Supreme Court directed ECI to accept Aadhaar as the 12th valid document in Bihar Special Intensive Revision (SIR)
- ⚠ Aadhaar is proof of identity/address, not citizenship
- Legal basis: Section 23(4), Representation of the People Act, 1950 — ECI may use Aadhaar to authenticate roll entries
- Bench: Justices Surya Kant and Joymalya Bagchi; petitioner Ashwini Kumar Upadhyay sought pan-India SIR
- Next hearing on legality of SIR: 7 October 2025
### Immigration and Foreigners Order, 2025
## 1. News
- Notified: 1 September 2025 by MHA — replaces Foreigners (Tribunal) Order, 1964
- Parent law: Immigration and Foreigners Act, 2025 (passed April 2025)
- Foreigners Tribunals (FTs): powers of Judicial Magistrate of First Class; can issue arrest warrants, send to detention/holding centres
- FT size: capped at 3 members; ex-parte orders reviewable if appealed within 30 days
- FTs exist only in Assam: 100 tribunals; order applies across India
- Detention: for the first time a clear legal basis
- Employment barred (without Centre approval): power, water supply, petroleum, defence, space, nuclear, human rights work
## 2. Immigration and Foreigners (Exemption) Order, 2025
- Notified: 1 September 2025 by MHA — exempts from passport/visa requirement for entry, stay, exit
- Exempted: citizens of Nepal and Bhutan; Tibetan refugees; six minorities (Hindus, Sikhs, Buddhists, Jains, Parsis, Christians) from Afghanistan, Pakistan, Bangladesh; Sri Lankan Tamil refugees who sheltered in India on or before 9 January 2015 and registered
- ⚠ Exemption is not citizenship — they remain "illegal migrants" under Citizenship Act
- Sri Lankan Tamils were excluded from CAA 2019; civil war ended 2009
## 3. Background
- IMDT Act 1983 — struck down by SC in 2005 → 11 IMDTs became FTs
- Assam NRC 2019: excluded 19 lakh people
### Restoration of statehood to Jammu and Kashmir — SC seeks Centre's response
## 1. News
- Case: Zahoor Ahmed Bhat vs UT of Jammu and Kashmir — SC sought Union's response on restoring statehood
- SC verdict 11 December 2023: upheld abrogation of Article 370 and 35A; directed restoration of statehood and Assembly polls
- Assembly elections: 90-member J&K Assembly polls in October 2024; UT still run by Lieutenant Governor
## 2. Background — creation of States
| Provision | Power |
| Article 1 | India a "Union of States" |
| Article 2 | Admit/establish new States (e.g. Sikkim 1975) |
| Article 3 | Form new States, alter area, boundaries, names (e.g. Telangana 2014) |
- Goa (1961) and Sikkim (1975) — acquired territories later made States
- Number of States: 14 in 1956 (States Reorganisation Act) → 29 before J&K Reorganisation Act, 2019
- ⚠ Article 3 allows diminishing area, but converting a State into a UT is criticised as needing a constitutional amendment
- Federalism part of Basic Structure — Kesavananda Bharati (1973); Rajya Sabha — Article 83
### SC refers Pramati judgment (RTE exemption for minority schools) to larger bench
## 1. News
- September 2025: two-judge bench — Justices Dipankar Datta and Manmohan — said Pramati (2014) went "too far"; referred to larger bench (likely 7 judges)
- Context case: applicability of Teacher Eligibility Test (TET) to minority schools
- Observation: Article 21A and Article 30(1) must co-exist
## 2. Judicial history
| Case | Ruling |
| Society for Unaided Private Schools of Rajasthan vs UoI (2012) | Upheld RTE as reasonable restriction; exempted unaided minority schools |
| Pramati Educational & Cultural Trust vs UoI (2014) — 5-judge bench | Exempted both aided and unaided minority schools |
## 3. Background — RTE Act 2009
- Operationalises: Article 21A — free and compulsory education 6–14 years
- Section 12(1)(c): 25% entry-level seats in private unaided schools for disadvantaged groups (State reimburses)
- Bans: capitation fees, corporal punishment; TET for teachers
- Article 30(1): minorities' right to establish and administer educational institutions
### Census 2027 — geotagging of all buildings
## 1. News
- First: all buildings to be geotagged (latitude–longitude) during Houselisting Operations (HLO) — ⚠ HLO phase, not Population Enumeration
- Census 2027: after a 6-year delay; fully digital
### Ladakh protests — statehood and Sixth Schedule demands
## 1. News
- Violence in Leh: 4 dead; trigger — two elderly hunger strikers with Sonam Wangchuk hospitalised on 23 September 2025; Leh shutdown 24 September turned violent
- Groups: Leh Apex Body (LAB) and Kargil Democratic Alliance (KDA)
- Four demands: statehood; Sixth Schedule (Article 244); job/education reservation for locals; greater representation
## 2. May 2025 measures
- 95% job reservation for locals; 33% reservation for women in LAHDC; domicile criteria; recognition of Bhoti, Purgi, Balti, Shina
- 5 August 2019: Ladakh made UT without legislature
### SC guidelines on DNA evidence — Kattavellai v. State of Tamil Nadu
## 1. Guidelines
- Case: Kattavellai @ Devakar v. State of Tamil Nadu
- Collection: documented packaging (FIR no., IO, hospital), signed by doctor, IO and independent witnesses
- Transport: to FSL within 48 hours; delays recorded
- Trial: samples not opened/resealed without trial court permission
- Chain of Custody Register: maintained till outcome; part of trial record
## 2. Earlier cases
| Case | Holding |
| Anil v. State of Maharashtra (2014) | DNA reliable only with quality control |
| Manoj v. State of MP (2022) | Rejected — sample from open area |
| Rahul v. State of Delhi (2022) | Rejected — sample in Malkhana for 2 months |
- DNA = opinion evidence: Section 45, Indian Evidence Act = Section 39, Bharatiya Sakshya Adhiniyam 2023 — needs corroboration
### Karnataka HC dismisses X Corp plea — Section 79 takedowns and Sahyog Portal
## 1. News
- Verdict: Karnataka HC dismissed X Corp challenge to take-down orders under Section 79, IT Act 2000
- Sahyog Portal: upheld — Central/State agencies and local police can send requests under Section 79(3)(b); "an instrument of public good"
- ⚠ Section 79 (intermediary liability) differs from Section 69A blocking upheld in Shreya Singhal (2015)

## 1. News
- September 2025: Delhi HC protected Aishwarya Rai Bachchan, Abhishek Bachchan, Karan Johar against AI deepfakes and merchandise misuse
- Law: no single statute — Copyright Act 1957 Sections 38A (performer rights) and 38B (moral rights); Trade Marks Act 1999 (passing off Section 27); Article 21

### Right to Internet as a Fundamental Right
## 1. Judicial recognition
| Case | Holding |
| Faheema Shirin vs State of Kerala (2019) — Kerala HC | Internet access part of Article 21 (education, privacy) |
| Anuradha Bhasin vs Union of India (2020) — SC, 10 January 2020 | Against indefinite internet shutdowns; internet a platform for free speech, trade (Art 19) |
- Articles linked: 14, 19, 21, 21A
- UN: declared internet access a human right in 2016
## 2. Data
- BharatNet: target 6.5 lakh villages; only 1.99 lakh (30.4%) connected as of January 2025 — ⚠ less than 50%
### Criminal defamation and free speech
## 1. Facts
- Law: Sections 499 and 500 IPC — up to 2 years jail, fine or both
- Subramanian Swamy vs Union of India (2016): SC upheld criminal defamation — reputation part of Article 21
- 22 September 2025: Justice M.M. Sundresh flagged misuse (case against Foundation for Independent Journalism)
- UK has abolished criminal defamation
### Kolhan's Manki-Munda system — Ho adivasi protest
## 1. News
- Protest: 9 September 2025 by Ho adivasis in West Singhbhum (Jharkhand) against DC Chandan Kumar for allegedly removing Mundas
## 2. The system
| Post | Role |
| Munda | Head of one village — disputes; ⚠ no revenue role pre-British |
| Manki | Head of 8–15 villages — appellate authority |
- Wilkinson's Rules: 31 principles by Captain Thomas Wilkinson (Political Agent, Kolhan) — source dates them 1833 (agent appointed 1837) — first colonial recognition of adivasi customary governance
- Mora Ho vs State of Bihar (2000), Patna HC: Wilkinson's Rules are customs, not formal law
### Provincial citizenship and domicile politics
## 1. Points
- Term coined by: Alok Ranjan (JNU)
- Examples: Jharkhand (formed 2000) domicile policy; J&K domicile rules after 2019
- Myron Weiner (1978): "Sons of the Soil"; States Reorganisation Commission (1955) warned against domicile discrimination
- Articles: 15, 16, 16(2), 19
### Constitution (130th Amendment) Bill, 2025 — removal of arrested Ministers
## 1. News
- Introduced: in Lok Sabha by the Union Home Minister
- Amends: Article 75 (Council of Ministers incl. PM)
- Provision: a Minister arrested and detained for 30 consecutive days on allegation of an offence punishable with 5 years or more imprisonment shall be removed by the President on the advice of the PM by the 31st day
- Applies to: both Union and State Ministers
- UTs: separate amendments to Government of Union Territories Act, 1963 and J&K Reorganisation Act, 2019
- Backdrop: V. Senthil Balaji case (2023), Tamil Nadu — Minister continued in office despite arrest in money-laundering case
- ⚠ Not limited to Union Ministers — applies to State Ministers too
## 2. Related bills and procedure
- Bills: Government of UTs (Amendment) Bill, 2025; J&K Reorganisation (Amendment) Bill, 2025; Promotion and Regulation of Online Gaming Bill, 2025 (same session)
- Rules waived: Rule 19A (prior notice before introduction) and Rule 19B (advance circulation to MPs) of Lok Sabha
- Reason: Monsoon Session ending 21 August 2025
- Critic: Congress MP Abhishek Singhvi — could be weaponised by "biased central agencies"
## 3. Background
- Article 75: Ministers hold office during the pleasure of the President
- Article 164(1): State Ministers hold office during the pleasure of the Governor
- Constitution did not explicitly bar Ministers in detention from holding office
## 4. More provisions and JPC
- Covers: PM, Chief Ministers, Union and State Ministers — custody 30 consecutive days without bail
- Removal by: President (PM/Union Ministers) or Governor (CM/State Ministers) on advice of the executive head
- Automatic cessation: if advice not tendered by the 31st day, the person automatically ceases to be Minister
- Re-appointment: allowed after release from custody under Article 75(1)
- ⚠ No permanent disqualification — can be re-appointed
- Three bills: Govt of UTs (Amendment), Constitution (130th Amendment), J&K Reorganisation (Amendment) — all 2025
- JPC: 31 members — 21 Lok Sabha + 10 Rajya Sabha; report by the Winter Session
- Status: JPC; deadline extended (Jul 2026); left out of the 2026 Monsoon Session agenda (Jul 2026)
- JPC background: ad hoc body of both Houses; can summon officials and documents; recommendations not binding
### Section 152 BNS — sedition debate and press freedom
## 1. News
- Event: Assam Police summoned The Wire founding editor Siddharth Varadarajan and consulting editor Karan Thapar under Section 152 of Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita (BNS)
- Related to: coverage of Operation Sindoor
- Same day: Supreme Court issued notice on The Wire's petition challenging Section 152 and protected journalists from coercive action
- Lapses: summons lacked FIR date, offence details, FIR copy — against BNSS provisions
## 2. Background
- Section 152 BNS: seen as rebranded Section 124A IPC (sedition) — SC kept 124A in abeyance in 2022
- Covers: acts endangering sovereignty, unity and integrity of India; vague term "knowingly"
- ⚠ Unlike 124A, Section 152 does not demand a high standard of proof of intent
- Right involved: Article 19(1)(a) — freedom of speech and expression
### Promotion and Regulation of Online Gaming Act, 2025
## 1. News
- Passed: Lok Sabha by voice vote, Monsoon Session
- Bans: offering, operation, facilitation, advertisement, promotion and participation in online money games (fantasy sports, card games with stakes)
- Promotes: e-sports and recreational/social gaming — through a regulatory authority
- Persons: Speaker Om Birla; IT Minister Ashwini Vaishnaw; IT Secretary S. Krishnan
- Became law: 22 August 2025 after Presidential assent
## 2. Classification of games
| Category | Detail |
| E-sports | recognised under National Sports Governance Act, 2025; registration; entry fee allowed, performance-based prizes (e.g. Call of Duty, GTA competitive) |
| Online social games | casual/educational; no betting; promoted by govt |
| Real Money Games (RMGs) | stake money/tokens for monetary reward (poker, rummy, fantasy sports, paid ludo) — completely banned |
## 3. Penalties and institutions
- Offering RMGs / fund transactions: up to 3 years jail, fine up to ₹1 crore
- Advertising RMGs: up to 2 years, fine up to ₹50 lakh
- Offences: cognisable and non-bailable (BNSS 2023)
- Blocking: CERT-In; Interpol for foreign operators
- ⚠ No penalty for players — only operators/advertisers
- Regulatory Authority; funds from Consolidated Fund of India for social gaming
- Earlier: IT Rules, 2021 amendments (2023) — KYC, parental controls, age rating
## 4. Data and issues
- Losses: ~₹15,000 crore a year to RMGs; 32 gaming suicides in Karnataka in 31 months
- Fraud cited: ₹400 crore via Chinese app FIEWIN; ₹30,000 crore GST evasion; ₹2,000 crore tax evasion (2022)
- Constitution: betting & gambling — Entries 34 & 62, State List
- GST: uniform 28% on entry fee/deposit (2023) — SC stayed notices
- Challenge ground: Article 19(1)(g) — skill vs chance
- Industry: 2 lakh jobs, 400+ firms
- ⚠ Act has no explicit child-protection provisions

- Creates: 22nd IIM at Guwahati, Assam
- Outlay: ₹555 crore

- Passed: Lok Sabha 19 August 2025; Rajya Sabha 20 August 2025 (Opposition absent — demanded discussion on Bihar SIR)

| Before 2014 | 13 IIMs in 53 years |
| After 2014 | 9 new IIMs |
| Students | 3,500 (2013–14) → 9,800 (2024–25) |
### Machine-readable electoral rolls debate
## 1. News
- Rolls: over 99 crore entries; EC shares them as image PDFs (no voter photos)
- Database: ERONET — EC's digital application for voter details; rolls prepared by district officials
- EC stand: before 2019 elections, EC told CEOs to stop uploading machine-readable rolls (prevent foreign access)
- Case: Kamal Nath vs EC of India (2018) — SC refused to compel EC to give machine-readable rolls; citizens may use OCR
- Scale: over 6 crore pages nationwide
### Presidential Reference under Article 143 on timelines for assent to Bills
## 1. News
- Reference by: President Droupadi Murmu — can President and Governors be judicially compelled to act within timelines on State Bills?
- Bench: Constitution Bench led by CJI B.R. Gavai
- Trigger: SC judgment of 8 April 2025 — Justices J.B. Pardiwala and R. Mahadevan
- Tamil Nadu Governor R.N. Ravi's delay on 10 re-passed Bills held illegal
- ⚠ First time judicially enforceable timelines imposed on Governors and President
## 2. Background — Article 143
- 143(1): President may refer a question of law or fact of public importance for advisory opinion
- 143(2): disputes on pre-Constitution treaties, agreements
- Opinion: advisory, not binding (but persuasive)
- ⚠ SC cannot directly overrule its own precedent in a Reference — can only clarify or suggest a larger bench
### 50 years of the Emergency — Cabinet resolution
## 1. News
- 25 June 2025: Union Cabinet resolution honouring resistance to the Emergency; day called 'Samvidhan Hatya Diwas'
- Emergency: declared 25 June 1975 by PM Indira Gandhi on "internal disturbance" under Article 352; lasted 21 months till 21 March 1977
- ⚠ Article 352, not 356
## 2. Background
| Type | Article |
| National Emergency | 352 — war, external aggression, armed rebellion |
| President's Rule | 356 (with 365) |
| Financial Emergency | 360 |
- Emergency provisions: Articles 352–360
- Features: Article 19 rights suspended; press censorship; arrests under MISA; ADM Jabalpur v. Shivkant Shukla (habeas corpus suspended); forced sterilisation
- 42nd Amendment (1976): "Mini-Constitution" — added Socialist, Secular to Preamble; DPSPs over FRs; curbed judiciary
- 44th Amendment (1978): Janata govt — reversed many changes; "internal disturbance" → "armed rebellion"
### ECINET used in June 2025 bypolls
## 1. News
- ECINET: Election Commission Integrated Network — announced 4 May 2025; integrates 40+ ECI apps
- First used: bypolls of 19 June 2025 in Kerala, Gujarat, Punjab, West Bengal; results 23 June
- Turnout upload: directly by presiding officers (⚠ not returning officers) before leaving polling station
### Draft Registration Bill, 2025
## 1. News
- Released by: Ministry of Rural Development (MoRD) — draft for public comments
- Replaces: Registration Act, 1908 (117 years old)
- Why: land/property disputes are over two-thirds of civil cases
## 2. Key provisions
| Section | Provision |
| Sec 29 | end-to-end digital + offline registration; Aadhaar or offline ID; 29(4) — no denial for lack of Aadhaar |
| Sec 12 | compulsory registration expanded — agreement of sale, power of attorney, mortgage by deposit of title deeds, corporate mergers/demergers (Companies Act 2013) |
| Sec 4(5) | new Additional/Assistant Inspector Generals of Registration |
| Sec 58, 64 | refusal (e.g. delay over four months, wills exempt); 64(3) — Inspector General can cancel fraudulent registration; appeal in 30 days |
| Penalty | jail cut from 7 years to 3 years |
- ⚠ Aadhaar is not mandatory; penalty is reduced, not increased
- Concern: delegation to Common Service Centres (CSCs)
### AP High Court — SC status lost on conversion to Christianity
## 1. Ruling
- Judge: Justice N. Harinath, AP High Court
- Held: SC status ceases immediately on conversion to Christianity; protections under SC/ST (Prevention of Atrocities) Act, 1989 not available
- Case: Pastor Chintada Anand of Kothapalem, Guntur — complaint (January 2021) against Akkala Ramireddy and others; proceedings quashed
- Counsel: Phani Dutt (petitioners); Eerla Satish Kumar (Anand)
## 2. Basis
- Constitution (Scheduled Castes) Order, 1950 — only those professing Hinduism, Sikhism or Buddhism can be SC
- ⚠ Christianity and Islam not included
### AP Cabinet approves Ordinance on SC sub-categorisation
## 1. News
- Approved by: AP Cabinet chaired by CM N. Chandrababu Naidu — draft Ordinance
- 59 SC communities in three groups:
| Group | Castes | Reservation |
| Group-I | 12 | 1% |
| Group-II | 18 | 6.5% |
| Group-III | 29 | 7.5% |
- ⚠ Total = 15% SC quota
- Roster: 200-point roster in two cycles of 100
- Unit: whole State as one unit, based on 2011 census; districts become units after next census; all 26 districts
- First use: DSC teacher recruitment
# MCQ NOTES OF APP TOPIC 6:29
### Four Labour Codes
## The four Codes
- Four Labour Codes in force from 21 November 2025; replaced 29 Central labour laws
| Code | Laws merged |
| Code on Wages, 2019 | 4 |
| Code on Social Security, 2020 | 9 |
| Industrial Relations Code, 2020 | 3 |
| OSH Code, 2020 | 13 |
- Code on Social Security, 2020 (not the Code on Wages) — first legal definition of gig and platform workers; Social Security Fund
- Code on Wages: overtime at least twice the normal rate; deductions capped at 50%
- Fixed-term employees: gratuity after 1 year (5 years applied to permanent employees)
- IR Code merged the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 and Trade Unions Act, 1926
- IR Code: layoff/retrenchment/closure permission threshold raised from 100 to 300 workers (not 500)
- OSH Code: women may work before 6 am / after 7 pm with consent and safety measures; appointment letters and annual free health check mandatory
## Ladakh, LWE and States
- September 2025 Ladakh protests: four demands incl. Statehood; by Leh Apex Body and Kargil Democratic Alliance
- May 2025 Ladakh measures: 33% women's reservation in LAHDC; 95% job reservation for locals
- SC banned Salwa Judum in 2011; Clear–Hold–Develop is a strategy
- LWE districts: from 90+ a decade ago down to 11; core in Bijapur, Narayanpur, Sukma (Chhattisgarh)
- Goa taken 1961, Sikkim 1975; both later made States
- Kolhan Manki-Munda system: Manki heads 8–15 villages, appellate authority; Munda heads one village
- 'Provincial citizenship' — term by Alok Ranjan (JNU); Myron Weiner — 'Sons of the Soil'
- Examples: Jharkhand's domicile policy (State formed 2000) and J&K's post-2019 domicile rules
## Courts, evidence, rights
- Kattavellai @ Devakar v. State of Tamil Nadu — DNA samples to the FSL within 48 hours
- Rahul v. State of Delhi (2022) — sample left in the Malkhana for 2 months (documented packaging with signatures = collection guideline)
- DM Entertainment v. Baby Gift House — restraint on Daler Mehndi dolls; Jackie Shroff case — protection against e-commerce and AI chatbots
- e-Courts Project — from the SC e-Committee's National Policy and Action Plan of 2005; Department of Justice with NIC under NeGP
- NALSA: Patron-in-Chief CJI; Executive Chairman the second senior-most SC judge
## Other
- MGNREGA: unemployment allowance if work not given within 15 days
- India's first medical cannabis clinic — Bengaluru (2020); Uttarakhand first State to issue industrial-hemp licences (2018)
- Recruitment trust reforms: biometric checks, encrypted paper transfer, AI analytics, a national regulator (not an ombudsman)
- Uttarakhand UCC Rules 2025 amendments: disclosure of pregnancy and children (and caste) in live-ins deleted; Registrar cannot inform parents of registrants below 21
- CAA 2019 — six minorities from Pakistan, Afghanistan, Bangladesh who entered before 31 December 2014; 9 January 2015 cut-off = September 2025 exemption for Sri Lankan Tamils
- Machine-readable rolls: before 2019 elections the EC told CEOs to stop uploading them
- Vice-President electoral college: both Houses of Parliament (elected and nominated); State MLAs do not vote
### DPDP Rules 2025
## DPDP framework
- DPDP Rules, 2025 notified 14 November 2025 (draft January 2025); enforce the DPDP Act, 2023
- Data Principal = individual whose data is processed; Data Fiduciary decides purpose and means
- Withdrawing consent as easy as giving it
- Data Protection Board — office under MeitY (digital by design)
- RTI Act Section 8(1)(j) amended: public-interest exception for personal information removed
## RTI Act sections
| Section | Subject |
| 3 | right to information |
| 4 | suo motu disclosure |
| 8 | exemptions from disclosure |
| 20 | penalties |
- First Chief Information Commissioner — Wajahat Habibullah (2005–2010)
## UPSC
- UPSC entered its centenary year on 1 October 2025
- Public Service Commission set up October 1926 (not 1924); first chairman Sir Ross Barker
## Rulings and rights
- Article 370/35A abrogation upheld 11 December 2023; statehood and Assembly polls directed
- Karnataka HC (X Corp case) upheld the Sahyog Portal as a tool for public good (requests under Section 79(3)(b), IT Act)
- Delhi HC (September 2025) protected Aishwarya Rai Bachchan, Abhishek Bachchan, Karan Johar against AI deepfakes and merchandise misuse
- Article 21 is one of the legal bases of personality rights
- Kerala hijab row: Article 25 subject to public order, morality and health; courts apply the proportionality test
- Mora Ho v. State of Bihar — Wilkinson's Rules for Kolhan held to be customs
- AP High Court: SC status ceases immediately on becoming Christian; no SC/ST (PoA) Act, 1989 protection
## Other
- NDPS Act Section 14 — allows cannabis growing for industrial, horticultural, medical, scientific use; Section 8(b) bans it without approval
- India Justice Report 2025: women 14% of HC judges, 3.1% of SC judges (1 of 34)
- e-Courts Phase II (2015–2023) — e-filing, e-payment, NJDG, virtual courts; Phase I — Case Information System, computerisation of 14,000+ courts
- Census 2027 — after a 6-year delay, fully digital
### Bills in Parliament
## Constitution (130th Amendment) Bill, 2025
- PM, Chief Ministers, Ministers vacate office after 30 consecutive days of custody (without bail); removal by the 31st day
- Charges punishable with at least 5 years
- Amends Article 75 (Ministers hold office during the President's pleasure)
- Backdrop: V. Senthil Balaji case (Tamil Nadu, 2023)
- No permanent disqualification; re-appointment possible after release
- JPC: 21 Lok Sabha + 10 Rajya Sabha members; a JPC is an ad hoc body
- Left out of the July 2026 Monsoon Session agenda; JPC deadline extended
## Online Gaming Act, 2025
- Promotion and Regulation of Online Gaming Act, 2025 — assent 22 August 2025
- Bans online money games; promotes e-sports and social games (social games have no betting)
- Offences cognisable and non-bailable; offering RMGs up to 3 years and ₹1 crore; advertising up to 2 years and ₹50 lakh
- Players not penalised
- Betting and gambling: State List, Entries 34 and 62
- 28% GST on entry fee/deposit (2023)
- Challenge (skill vs chance) under Article 19(1)(g)
## Draft Registration Bill, 2025
- Section 29 — digital and offline registration; cannot be denied for lack of Aadhaar (Section 12 = compulsory registration)
- Land/property disputes = over two-thirds of civil cases
- Worry: handover to Common Service Centres (CSCs)
## Chandigarh and Article 240
- Chandigarh not listed under Article 240 (President's regulation-making power)
- Haryana — 17th State; Periphery Control Act — 16 km; land from 22 villages of Kharar
## Other
- IIM (Amendment) Bill, 2025 — 22nd IIM at Guwahati; amends IIM Act, 2017; admissions from 2025–26
- Collective responsibility — Articles 75(3) and 164(2); 75(1)/164(1) = appointment
- Article 312 — Parliament can create All-India Services incl. an AIJS; President Droupadi Murmu backed AIJS on Constitution Day 2023
- Article 3 — new States, alter area/boundaries/names; Article 2 — admission of States (e.g. Sikkim 1975)
- RTI Act Section 4 — suo motu disclosure (Sections 6, 7 = procedure)
- JeM women's-wing online course begins November 2025
- SC May 2025 district judiciary framework: qualifying service 5 → 3 years; LDCE restored at 25%; 3 years' practice for Civil Judge entry
- After Aland deletion allegations, ECINet e-sign: Aadhaar-linked mobile verification for online registration, deletion, correction
- Form-7 — objection to inclusion/deletion in the electoral roll
- Criminal defamation (IPC 499, 500) — up to 2 years, fine or both
- 42nd Amendment, 1976 — 'Mini-Constitution'; added 'Socialist' and 'Secular'
- AP SC sub-categorisation: draft Ordinance approved by Cabinet under N. Chandrababu Naidu; first use for DSC teacher recruitment
### Governor & Bills (Art 200)
## Article 200
- Governor to act "as soon as possible" — no fixed timeline
- May reserve a Bill for the President (not the Supreme Court)
## Presidential Reference 2025
- Followed the April 2025 (8 April) ruling on Tamil Nadu Governor R.N. Ravi: delay unconstitutional, timelines under Article 142, pocket veto unconstitutional (bench incl. Justice J.B. Pardiwala)
- First time judicially enforceable timelines imposed on Governors and the President
- Reference on Articles 200 and 201; Constitution Bench led by CJI B.R. Gavai
- Centre: the Governor is not merely a "postman"; timelines = judicial amendment
- Cases cited: Nabam Rebia (2006), K.M. Singh (2020)
- Ruling: no court-imposed fixed timelines; limited mandamus for long unexplained inaction; deemed assent and Article 142 substitution rejected
- Article 143(1) opinion — advisory, not binding (persuasive)
- Article 143(2) — disputes on pre-Constitution treaties; in a Reference the Court cannot overrule its precedent, only clarify or suggest a larger bench
| Commission | Recommendation |
| Punchhi (2010) | Governor to decide on Bills within six months |
| Sarkaria (1987) | discretion only in rare cases; President to dispose of reserved Bills in six months |
## Other
- Pan-India SIR announced 27 October 2025; first phase 10–15 States/UTs incl. TN, WB, Kerala, Assam, Puducherry
- Census 2027 geotagging during Houselisting Operations (not Population Enumeration)
- DNA evidence — opinion evidence requiring corroboration (Section 45 Evidence Act; Section 39 BSA 2023)
### Landmark rulings
## District judiciary
- SC overruled Dheeraj Mor v. High Court of Delhi (2020) (and Satya Narain Singh, 1984): serving judicial officers may seek direct recruitment as district judges
- Justice Sundresh: total bar breaks Article 14
- Uniform minimum age 35 years; seven years' experience may combine advocacy and judicial service
- Promotees ≈ three-fourths of the HJS cadre

- District judiciary handles ~90% of litigation
- Article 233 — district judges, appointed by Governor in consultation with the HC (other officers: after consulting State PSC and HC)

- Religious practice continues at protected monuments (Section 5(6), AMASR Act, 1958)

- Vice-President: at least 35, no office of profit; re-election allowed

- Provincial citizenship / domicile: Articles 15, 16, 16(2), 19

### Digital Personal Data Protection (DPDP) Rules, 2025
## 1. News
- Notified: 14 November 2025 — operationalise the DPDP Act, 2023 (passed August 2023)
- Draft Rules: released January 2025
- Immediate effect: formation of Data Protection Board of India (DPBI) + the RTI amendment
- Phased: most provisions apply after 12–18 months (consent system, erasure, DPOs, audits, breach reporting — 2026–27)
## 2. Key features
- Citizen rights: erasure, correction, grievance redressal, appoint digital nominee (after death/incapacity)
- Consent: informed consent; withdrawal as easy as giving; Consent Managers
- Children: targeted ads restricted; limited exemption for parents to track child's location
- Significant Data Fiduciaries (SDFs): graded obligations
- DPBI: "digital by design" — works as a digital office; online complaints; can drop proceedings on a voluntary undertaking
- ⚠ DPBI is a subordinate office of MeitY (criticised for lack of independence)
## 3. RTI amendment
- ⚠ Section 8(1)(j) of RTI Act, 2005 — the public interest override for personal information removed
## 4. Background
| Term | Meaning |
| Data Principal | Individual whose data is processed |
| Data Fiduciary | Entity deciding purpose and means of processing |
- Puttaswamy (2017): privacy a fundamental right under Article 21
- Srikrishna Committee draft — 2018

### Cannabis cultivation vs bhang — Kerala HC on NDPS Act
## 1. News
- Kerala High Court (4 Nov 2025): dismissed plea of a man caught with five cannabis plants on terrace
- Ruling: cultivation of any cannabis plant is illegal even with no flowering/fruiting tops; includes keeping in pots
## 2. NDPS Act, 1985 — definitions
| Term | Meaning |
| Charas | Resin of cannabis (incl. hashish oil) |
| Ganja | Flowering or fruiting tops (contain THC) |
| Excluded | Leaves and seeds without flowering tops |
| Cannabis plant — Section 2(iv) | Any plant of genus cannabis, irrespective of age/flowering |
- ⚠ Bhang is made from leaves — not a narcotic under NDPS; States regulate it (UP, Rajasthan licensed shops; Assam bans it)
- Section 8(b): prohibits cultivation of any cannabis plant without approval
- Section 14: Govt may allow cultivation for industrial (hemp), horticultural, medical/scientific use
## 3. Punishments
| Item | Small quantity | Commercial quantity |
| Ganja | up to 1 kg — jail up to 1 year, fine up to ₹10,000 | 20 kg+ — 10–20 years, fine ₹1–2 lakh |
| Charas | up to 100 g | 1 kg+ |
- Cultivation: up to 10 years RI and ₹1 lakh fine
## 4. Background
- Industrial hemp: ⚠ Uttarakhand first to issue licences (2018); also UP, HP, MP
- India's first medical cannabis clinic: Bengaluru (2020)
- UN Commission on Narcotic Drugs: founded 1946, HQ Vienna
- Recreational use legal: Canada, Uruguay, 15 US states; 50+ countries allow medicinal cannabis
- Delhi HC hearing challenge by Great Legalisation Movement India Trust
- NDPS Act enacted under international pressure, mainly USA

### SC: serving judicial officers can be directly recruited as district judges — Rejanish KV vs K Deepa
## 1. Verdict
- Date: 9 October 2025; five-judge Constitution Bench led by CJI B.R. Gavai
- Held: judicial officers in subordinate judiciary can compete for district judge via direct recruitment (earlier only advocates with 7 years practice)
- Overruled: Satya Narain Singh (1984) and Dheeraj Mor vs High Court of Delhi (2020)
- Concurring: Justice Sundresh — absolute bar violates Article 14
## 2. Directions
- 7 years continuous experience (advocate + judicial officer combined)
- Minimum age: 35 years uniform
## 3. Background
- Articles 233–237 (Part VI) — subordinate courts
- Article 233: district judges appointed by Governor in consultation with High Court
- Other judicial officers: Governor after consulting State PSC and High Court
- CJM: offences up to 7 years; Judicial Magistrate: up to 3 years; death sentence by Sessions Judge needs HC confirmation
### 130th Constitution Amendment Bill, 2025 — removal of detained PM/CM/Ministers
## 1. Provision
- PM, CMs or Ministers to vacate office if arrested and detained for 30 consecutive days on charges punishable with at least five years imprisonment
## 2. Concerns
- Presumption of innocence; separation of powers; collective responsibility; potential misuse
## 3. Background
- Article 75(1): President appoints PM, Ministers on PM's advice; Article 164(1): Governor appoints CM
- Articles 75(3), 164(2): collective responsibility to legislature
| Case | Point |
| Manoj Narula v Union of India (2014) | PM's advice binding unless constitutionally disqualified |
| Rai Sahib Ram Jawaya Kapur v State of Punjab (1955) | Parliamentary accountability |
| S.R. Bommai v Union of India (1994) | Floor test, legislative supremacy |

| Case / provision | Point |
| Lal Babu Hussain (1995) | Burden of proving citizenship lies on new registrants, not existing voters |
| Mohinder Singh Gill (1978) | Article 324 powers are plenary but within constitutional limits |
| Article 326 | Universal adult suffrage — only citizens vote |

### Vice-Presidential Election 2025 — C.P. Radhakrishnan elected
## 1. News
- Winner: C.P. Radhakrishnan (NDA) defeated B. Sudershan Reddy (joint opposition)
- Turnout: over 98% of MPs voted; 13 MPs abstained (govt sources)
- Returning Officer: P.C. Mody, Rajya Sabha Secretary General; counting in new Parliament building
## 2. Background — Vice-President
- Article 66: election by electoral college of elected + nominated members of both Houses — ⚠ State legislatures do not vote
- Vice-President C.P. Radhakrishnan elected Sep 2025 [GK for date]
- Electoral college: VP = elected + nominated members of both Houses (no MLAs); President = includes elected MLAs
- Method: proportional representation by single transferable vote, secret ballot
- Eligibility: citizen, at least 35 years, no office of profit; term 5 years, re-election allowed
- Role: ex-officio Chairman of Rajya Sabha (⚠ not Lok Sabha); acts as President in vacancy
### Presidential Reference — Governors sitting on Bills (Article 200)
## 1. News
- Bench: five-judge Presidential Reference Bench led by CJI B.R. Gavai
- Issue: Governors delaying assent indefinitely — e.g. Tamil Nadu Governor sat on 10 Bills for 4 years (since 2020)
- Earlier ruling (April 2025, TN Governor case): three-month timeline for Governor and President
- Opinion (Nov 2025, 5-judge Constitution Bench): Governor has three options under Art. 200 - no indefinite inaction; no fixed or court-imposed timelines for the Governor or President (Art. 201) because the text says "as soon as possible"; "deemed assent" rejected; Art. 142 cannot replace their role; courts may issue limited mandamus against prolonged, unexplained inaction
## 2. Background — Article 200 options
- Assent; withhold assent; return for reconsideration (not Money Bill); reserve for President
- ⚠ Article 200 itself prescribes no timeline — says "as soon as possible"
## 3. Presidential reference under Article 143(1)
- Trigger: April 2025 two-judge Bench held TN Governor R.N. Ravi's delay unconstitutional, used Article 142 to set timelines; pocket veto unconstitutional
- States: reference relitigates verdict (stare decisis) — Abhishek Manu Singhvi (TN), Gopal Subramanium; Governor acts on aid and advice — Article 163
- Centre: Governor not a "postman"; AG R. Venkataraman — timelines = judicial amendment; States cannot use Article 32 against Governor
- GoI Act 1935: Governor had absolute veto
- ⚠ Advisory opinion under Article 143 is not binding
## 4. More provisions and commissions
- Reference made: May 2025 on Articles 200 and 201; April 2025 case — State of Tamil Nadu vs Governor of Tamil Nadu
- Article 201: President's assent to reserved Bills; Article 163(2): Governor's discretion final, not questioned in court
- Mandatory reservation: e.g. Bill reducing High Court powers
- Cases cited: Nabam Rebia (2006 per source), K.M. Singh (2020)
| Commission | Recommendation |
| Sarkaria (1987) | Discretion only in rare cases; President to dispose reserved Bills in 6 months |
| Punchhi (2010) | Governor to decide on Bills in 6 months |
### Restoration of statehood to Jammu and Kashmir — SC seeks Centre's response
## 1. News
- Case: Zahoor Ahmed Bhat vs UT of Jammu and Kashmir — SC sought Union's response on restoring statehood
- SC verdict 11 December 2023: upheld abrogation of Article 370 and 35A; directed restoration of statehood and Assembly polls
- Assembly elections: 90-member J&K Assembly polls in October 2024; UT still run by Lieutenant Governor
## 2. Background — creation of States
| Provision | Power |
| Article 1 | India a "Union of States" |
| Article 2 | Admit/establish new States (e.g. Sikkim 1975) |
| Article 3 | Form new States, alter area, boundaries, names (e.g. Telangana 2014) |
- Goa (1961) and Sikkim (1975) — acquired territories later made States
- Number of States: 14 in 1956 (States Reorganisation Act) → 29 before J&K Reorganisation Act, 2019
- ⚠ Article 3 allows diminishing area, but converting a State into a UT is criticised as needing a constitutional amendment
- Federalism part of Basic Structure — Kesavananda Bharati (1973); Rajya Sabha — Article 83
### SC refers Pramati judgment (RTE exemption for minority schools) to larger bench
## 1. News
- September 2025: two-judge bench — Justices Dipankar Datta and Manmohan — said Pramati (2014) went "too far"; referred to larger bench (likely 7 judges)
- Context case: applicability of Teacher Eligibility Test (TET) to minority schools
- Observation: Article 21A and Article 30(1) must co-exist
## 2. Judicial history
| Case | Ruling |
| Society for Unaided Private Schools of Rajasthan vs UoI (2012) | Upheld RTE as reasonable restriction; exempted unaided minority schools |
| Pramati Educational & Cultural Trust vs UoI (2014) — 5-judge bench | Exempted both aided and unaided minority schools |
## 3. Background — RTE Act 2009
- Operationalises: Article 21A — free and compulsory education 6–14 years
- Section 12(1)(c): 25% entry-level seats in private unaided schools for disadvantaged groups (State reimburses)
- Bans: capitation fees, corporal punishment; TET for teachers
- Article 30(1): minorities' right to establish and administer educational institutions
### SC guidelines on DNA evidence — Kattavellai v. State of Tamil Nadu
## 1. Guidelines
- Case: Kattavellai @ Devakar v. State of Tamil Nadu
- Collection: documented packaging (FIR no., IO, hospital), signed by doctor, IO and independent witnesses
- Transport: to FSL within 48 hours; delays recorded
- Trial: samples not opened/resealed without trial court permission
- Chain of Custody Register: maintained till outcome; part of trial record
## 2. Earlier cases
| Case | Holding |
| Anil v. State of Maharashtra (2014) | DNA reliable only with quality control |
| Manoj v. State of MP (2022) | Rejected — sample from open area |
| Rahul v. State of Delhi (2022) | Rejected — sample in Malkhana for 2 months |
- DNA = opinion evidence: Section 45, Indian Evidence Act = Section 39, Bharatiya Sakshya Adhiniyam 2023 — needs corroboration
### Personality rights — Delhi HC protects celebrities from AI misuse
## 1. News
- September 2025: Delhi HC protected Aishwarya Rai Bachchan, Abhishek Bachchan, Karan Johar against AI deepfakes and merchandise misuse
- Law: no single statute — Copyright Act 1957 Sections 38A (performer rights) and 38B (moral rights); Trade Marks Act 1999 (passing off Section 27); Article 21
| Case | Holding |
| R. Rajagopal v. State of TN (1994) | Right to control identity under Art 21 |
| Rajinikanth (Madras HC, 2015) | Restrained "Main Hoon Rajnikanth" |
| DM Entertainment v. Baby Gift House (2010) | Daler Mehndi dolls restrained |
| Anil Kapoor (Delhi HC, 2023) | Protected "jhakaas" |
| Jackie Shroff (Delhi HC, 2024) | E-commerce, AI chatbots |
| Arijit Singh (Bombay HC, 2024) | AI voice cloning restrained |
| Digital Collectibles v. Galactus Funware (2023) | Refused to expand rights at cost of free speech |
### Right to Internet as a Fundamental Right
- United Nations declared internet access a human right in 2016
### Constitution (130th Amendment) Bill, 2025 — removal of arrested Ministers
## 1. News
- Introduced: in Lok Sabha by the Union Home Minister
- Amends: Article 75 (Council of Ministers incl. PM)
- Provision: a Minister arrested and detained for 30 consecutive days on allegation of an offence punishable with 5 years or more imprisonment shall be removed by the President on the advice of the PM by the 31st day
- Applies to: both Union and State Ministers
- UTs: separate amendments to Government of Union Territories Act, 1963 and J&K Reorganisation Act, 2019
- Backdrop: V. Senthil Balaji case (2023), Tamil Nadu — Minister continued in office despite arrest in money-laundering case
- ⚠ Not limited to Union Ministers — applies to State Ministers too
## 2. Related bills and procedure
- Bills: Government of UTs (Amendment) Bill, 2025; J&K Reorganisation (Amendment) Bill, 2025; Promotion and Regulation of Online Gaming Bill, 2025 (same session)
- Rules waived: Rule 19A (prior notice before introduction) and Rule 19B (advance circulation to MPs) of Lok Sabha
- Reason: Monsoon Session ending 21 August 2025
- Critic: Congress MP Abhishek Singhvi — could be weaponised by "biased central agencies"
## 3. Background
- Article 75: Ministers hold office during the pleasure of the President
- Article 164(1): State Ministers hold office during the pleasure of the Governor
- Constitution did not explicitly bar Ministers in detention from holding office
## 4. More provisions and JPC
- Covers: PM, Chief Ministers, Union and State Ministers — custody 30 consecutive days without bail
- Removal by: President (PM/Union Ministers) or Governor (CM/State Ministers) on advice of the executive head
- Automatic cessation: if advice not tendered by the 31st day, the person automatically ceases to be Minister
- Re-appointment: allowed after release from custody under Article 75(1)
- ⚠ No permanent disqualification — can be re-appointed
- Three bills: Govt of UTs (Amendment), Constitution (130th Amendment), J&K Reorganisation (Amendment) — all 2025
- JPC: 31 members — 21 Lok Sabha + 10 Rajya Sabha; report by the Winter Session
- Status: JPC; deadline extended (Jul 2026); left out of the 2026 Monsoon Session agenda (Jul 2026)
- JPC background: ad hoc body of both Houses; can summon officials and documents; recommendations not binding
### Section 152 BNS — sedition debate and press freedom
## 1. News
- Event: Assam Police summoned The Wire founding editor Siddharth Varadarajan and consulting editor Karan Thapar under Section 152 of Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita (BNS)
- Related to: coverage of Operation Sindoor
- Same day: Supreme Court issued notice on The Wire's petition challenging Section 152 and protected journalists from coercive action
- Lapses: summons lacked FIR date, offence details, FIR copy — against BNSS provisions
## 2. Background
- Section 152 BNS: seen as rebranded Section 124A IPC (sedition) — SC kept 124A in abeyance in 2022
- Covers: acts endangering sovereignty, unity and integrity of India; vague term "knowingly"
- ⚠ Unlike 124A, Section 152 does not demand a high standard of proof of intent
- Right involved: Article 19(1)(a) — freedom of speech and expression
### Promotion and Regulation of Online Gaming Act, 2025
## 1. News
- Passed: Lok Sabha by voice vote, Monsoon Session
- Bans: offering, operation, facilitation, advertisement, promotion and participation in online money games (fantasy sports, card games with stakes)
- Promotes: e-sports and recreational/social gaming — through a regulatory authority
- Persons: Speaker Om Birla; IT Minister Ashwini Vaishnaw; IT Secretary S. Krishnan
- Became law: 22 August 2025 after Presidential assent
## 2. Classification of games
| Category | Detail |
| E-sports | recognised under National Sports Governance Act, 2025; registration; entry fee allowed, performance-based prizes (e.g. Call of Duty, GTA competitive) |
| Online social games | casual/educational; no betting; promoted by govt |
| Real Money Games (RMGs) | stake money/tokens for monetary reward (poker, rummy, fantasy sports, paid ludo) — completely banned |
## 3. Penalties and institutions
- Offering RMGs / fund transactions: up to 3 years jail, fine up to ₹1 crore
- Advertising RMGs: up to 2 years, fine up to ₹50 lakh
- Offences: cognisable and non-bailable (BNSS 2023)
- Blocking: CERT-In; Interpol for foreign operators
- ⚠ No penalty for players — only operators/advertisers
- Regulatory Authority; funds from Consolidated Fund of India for social gaming
- Earlier: IT Rules, 2021 amendments (2023) — KYC, parental controls, age rating
## 4. Data and issues
- Losses: ~₹15,000 crore a year to RMGs; 32 gaming suicides in Karnataka in 31 months
- Fraud cited: ₹400 crore via Chinese app FIEWIN; ₹30,000 crore GST evasion; ₹2,000 crore tax evasion (2022)
- Constitution: betting & gambling — Entries 34 & 62, State List
- GST: uniform 28% on entry fee/deposit (2023) — SC stayed notices
- Challenge ground: Article 19(1)(g) — skill vs chance
- Industry: 2 lakh jobs, 400+ firms
- ⚠ Act has no explicit child-protection provisions
### IIM (Amendment) Bill, 2025 — 22nd IIM at Guwahati
## 1. News
- Amends: Indian Institutes of Management Act, 2017
- Creates: 22nd IIM at Guwahati, Assam
- Outlay: ₹555 crore
- Admissions: from 2025-26 (Education Minister Dharmendra Pradhan)
- Passed: Lok Sabha 19 August 2025; Rajya Sabha 20 August 2025 (Opposition absent — demanded discussion on Bihar SIR)
## 2. Background
| Item | Detail |
| First IIM | 1961 |
| Before 2014 | 13 IIMs in 53 years |
| After 2014 | 9 new IIMs |
| Students | 3,500 (2013–14) → 9,800 (2024–25) |
### Machine-readable electoral rolls debate
- EC shared rolls as image PDFs, without voter photographs (ERONET = voter-details app)
### Presidential Reference under Article 143 on timelines for assent to Bills
## 1. News
- Reference by: President Droupadi Murmu — can President and Governors be judicially compelled to act within timelines on State Bills?
- Bench: Constitution Bench led by CJI B.R. Gavai
- Trigger: SC judgment of 8 April 2025 — Justices J.B. Pardiwala and R. Mahadevan
- Tamil Nadu Governor R.N. Ravi's delay on 10 re-passed Bills held illegal
- ⚠ First time judicially enforceable timelines imposed on Governors and President
## 2. Background — Article 143
- 143(1): President may refer a question of law or fact of public importance for advisory opinion
- 143(2): disputes on pre-Constitution treaties, agreements
- Opinion: advisory, not binding (but persuasive)
- ⚠ SC cannot directly overrule its own precedent in a Reference — can only clarify or suggest a larger bench
### 50 years of the Emergency — Cabinet resolution
## 1. News
- 25 June 2025: Union Cabinet resolution honouring resistance to the Emergency; day called 'Samvidhan Hatya Diwas'
- Emergency: declared 25 June 1975 by PM Indira Gandhi on "internal disturbance" under Article 352; lasted 21 months till 21 March 1977
- ⚠ Article 352, not 356
## 2. Background
| Type | Article |
| National Emergency | 352 — war, external aggression, armed rebellion |
| President's Rule | 356 (with 365) |
| Financial Emergency | 360 |
- Emergency provisions: Articles 352–360
- Features: Article 19 rights suspended; press censorship; arrests under MISA; ADM Jabalpur v. Shivkant Shukla (habeas corpus suspended); forced sterilisation
- 42nd Amendment (1976): "Mini-Constitution" — added Socialist, Secular to Preamble; DPSPs over FRs; curbed judiciary
- 44th Amendment (1978): Janata govt — reversed many changes; "internal disturbance" → "armed rebellion"
### AP High Court — SC status lost on conversion to Christianity
- Ruling by Justice N. Harinath; person involved: Pastor Chintada Anand
### AP Cabinet approves Ordinance on SC sub-categorisation
## 1. News
- Approved by: AP Cabinet chaired by CM N. Chandrababu Naidu — draft Ordinance
- 59 SC communities in three groups:
| Group | Castes | Reservation |
| Group-I | 12 | 1% |
| Group-II | 18 | 6.5% |
| Group-III | 29 | 7.5% |
- ⚠ Total = 15% SC quota
- Roster: 200-point roster in two cycles of 100
- Unit: whole State as one unit, based on 2011 census; districts become units after next census; all 26 districts
- First use: DSC teacher recruitment

### Census 2027 — first fully digital census
## 1. Key facts
- Started: 1 April 2026 — India's first fully digital census
- Number: 16th overall; 8th after Independence; after a gap of 16 years
- Budget: ₹11,718.24 crore
- Personnel: more than 3 million officials, enumerators and supervisors
- Self-enumeration: online portal in 16 languages; households get a Self-Enumeration ID
- Pre-test: November 2025
## 2. Phases and reference dates
| Item | Detail |
| Phase I | April–September 2026 — house listing, housing conditions, amenities, assets |
| Phase II | February 2027 — population, socio-economic, education, caste |
| Reference date (most of India) | 1 March 2027 |
| Reference date (snow-bound) | 1 October 2026 — Ladakh, J&K, Himachal Pradesh, Uttarakhand |
## 3. Caste enumeration
- ⚠ First since 1931 — caste of every individual recorded (earlier only SC/ST)
- Legal basis: Section 3 of the Census Act, 1948
## 4. Background
- Law: Census Act, 1948 and Census Rules, 1990; Gazette notification by Ministry of Home Affairs
- Authority: Registrar General & Census Commissioner, India (MHA)
- Constitution: Article 246, Union List Entry 69; Articles 81 and 82 — Lok Sabha seats and delimitation based on census
- History: first census 1872; first complete census 1881; first after Independence 1951; last 2011 (121.08 crore)
- ⚠ 2021 Census postponed due to COVID-19

- Nature: annual statistical report; first published 1995

| Indicator | Earlier | Latest |

| GER — women, higher education | 28.5 (2021–22) | 30.2 (2022–23) |
| LFPR — rural women | 37.5% (2022) | 45.9% (2025) |
| Growth in managerial positions (2017–2025) | Men 73.80% | Women 102.54% |
- Gender parity: achieved from primary to higher secondary level
- LFPR (15+): highest growth among rural women
## 3. Background
- MoSPI: est. 1999; HQ New Delhi; Minister Rao Inderjit Singh
